
 
 
 Locality of Bernoulli Site Percolation on Transitive Graphs 
 
 
 
   
 
  // Restore the saved color scheme preference, or
  // enact the browser preference if "automatic", 
  // without expecting DOM load to have completed.
  //
  // Also restore any saved readingmode and ToC display preferences.
  function initializeReadingPreferences() {
    let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
    if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
      if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
        saved_theme = "dark";
      }
    }
    if (saved_theme == "dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
    }

    const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
    if (tocDisplay) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
    }
    const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
    if (readingMode) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
    }
    // Pre-apply spinout-banner dismissal here, before the banner paints, so it
    // never flashes in only to be hidden later by the deferred arxiv-header.js.
    // Key matches arxiv-header.js: "arxiv-banner-dismissed:" + data-banner-name.
    if (localStorage.getItem('arxiv-banner-dismissed:spinout-nonprofit')) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-banner-dismissed", "");
    }
  }
  // Run as soon as JS starts, to minimize repainting
  initializeReadingPreferences();

arXiv:2609.07026v1 [math.PR] 07 Sep 2026 
 
 
 Locality of Bernoulli Site Percolation on Transitive Graphs 
 
 
 Tho Tran
 
 Affiliation:  Department of Mathematics, National University of Singapore
 
    
 Tan M. Nguyen
 
 Affiliation:  Department of Mathematics, National University of Singapore
 

 Abstract 
    
 We prove locality of the critical probability for Bernoulli site
percolation on infinite, connected, locally finite, vertex-transitive
graphs, under the usual assumption that the critical probabilities stay
uniformly below one. The proof develops site-percolation forms of the
two-ghost inequality, sharp-threshold and snowballing estimates, and the
nonunimodular height method. We also establish the plentiful-tube geometry
needed for the multiscale argument from quantitative structure and
random-walk estimates. 
  
 
 Contents

 2  Preliminaries and lower semicontinuity

 1  Introduction and main result 

 
 Let  G = ( V , E ) G=(V,E)  be an infinite, connected, locally finite,
vertex-transitive graph. In Bernoulli site percolation the variables
 ( ω ⁡ ( v ) ) v ∈ V (\omega(v))_{v\in V}  are independent and 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( ω ⁡ ( v ) = 1 ) = p . \mathbb{P}_{p}(\omega(v)=1)=p. 
 
 
 A path is open when all its vertices are open, and 
 

 
 
 p c s ​ ( G ) := inf { p ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] : ℙ p ​ ( o ↔ ∞ ) > 0 } . p_{c}^{\rm s}(G):=\inf\{p\in[0,1]:\mathbb{P}_{p}(o\leftrightarrow\infty)>0\}. 
 
 
 
 
 Theorem 1.1   (Site locality) . 
 
 Let  ( G n ) n ≥ 1 (G_{n})_{n\geq 1}  and  G G  be infinite, connected, locally finite,
vertex-transitive graphs. If  G n → G G_{n}\to G  locally and
 lim sup n p c s ​ ( G n ) < 1 \limsup_{n}p_{c}^{\rm s}(G_{n})<1 , then 
 

 
 
 p c s ​ ( G n ) ⟶ p c s ​ ( G ) . p_{c}^{\rm s}(G_{n})\longrightarrow p_{c}^{\rm s}(G). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Here local convergence means that, after choosing arbitrary roots, the
rooted radius- r r  balls of  G n G_{n}  and  G G  are isomorphic for every
fixed  r r  and all sufficiently large  n n . Transitivity makes this
definition independent of the chosen roots. 
 
 
 Radius convention.  If a ball, sphere, or radial connectivity
event is displayed with a noninteger radius  r r , that radius means
 ⌊ r ⌋ \lfloor r\rfloor . Endpoints of an interval of admissible integer
scales are rounded inward. All additive constants in radial comparisons
are chosen to absorb this convention. 
 
 
 Selection convention.  Every graph considered here is countable.
Fix an enumeration of its vertices and use the induced length-lexicographic
order on finite paths, sets, and families. Whenever a measurable finite
witness is required, choose the least admissible one in this order.
Choices entering a mass transport are always made equivariantly, or the
transport is summed over all admissible choices. 
 
 
 Unimodularity convention.  Throughout the paper, a transitive
graph is called unimodular or nonunimodular according to the canonical
action of its full automorphism group  Aut ⁡ ( G ) \operatorname{Aut}(G) . In
Section 5 we write  Γ = Aut ⁡ ( G ) \Gamma=\operatorname{Aut}(G)  to keep the modular
formulas readable; no assertion there is made for a different transitive
subgroup. 
 
 
 The proof begins in Section 2 with the product-kernel and shared-junction
lemmas used by later explorations, followed by lower semicontinuity.
Section 3 proves the site two-ghost estimate. Section 4 extracts the
finite-energy path-opening lemma used in the final deduction. Section 5
proves the nonunimodular height
estimates in vertex coordinates, using the modular framework and tilted
mass transport from
 [ 15 , Section 2.1 and Proposition 2.2] .
Section 6 proves the site snowballing estimate, and Section 7 supplies the
geometric input and carries out the unrestricted multiscale induction.
Section 8 combines lower semicontinuity with the published
polynomial-growth case, the nonunimodular result of Section 5, and the
remaining canonically unimodular superpolynomial-growth result of
Section 7. 
 
 
 The bond-percolation proof of Easo and Hutchcroft
 [ 10 ]  motivates the overall strategy. The main new
site-percolation ingredients are the nonunimodular theorem of Section 5,
the snowballing estimate of Section 6, and the low-growth step of
Section 7. The geometric argument in Section 7 is proved from the
structure, presentation, heat-kernel, and random-walk results cited there. 
 
 
 Constants may depend on the common degree  d d  and a compact parameter
interval  a ≤ p ≤ 1 − b a\leq p\leq 1-b . This is enough: local convergence makes the
degrees eventually equal, elementary exploration gives
 p c s ≥ 1 / ( d − 1 ) p_{c}^{\rm s}\geq 1/(d-1) , and the hypothesis supplies  b > 0 b>0 . 
 
 
 
 2  Preliminaries and lower semicontinuity 

 
 2.1  Stopped product explorations 

 
 Lemma 2.1   (Stopped product kernel) . 
 
 Let  I I  be countable, let  ( U i ) i ∈ I (U_{i})_{i\in I}  be independent variables
with laws  ( μ i ) i ∈ I (\mu_{i})_{i\in I}  on standard Borel spaces, and let
 𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be an auxiliary sigma-field independent of all  U i U_{i} .
Consider an algorithm that, before its  n n -th query, chooses an
as-yet-unqueried index  Q n Q_{n}  using only  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  and the preceding
query results. It may stop after finitely many queries or continue
forever. Let  ℱ ∞ \mathcal{F}_{\infty}  be the sigma-field generated by the
auxiliary data, the query identities, and the observed results, and let
 𝒬 \mathcal{Q}  be the random set of queried indices. 
 
 
 For every finite deterministic  S ⊆ I S\subseteq I , every bounded measurable
 f f  of  ( U i ) i ∈ S (U_{i})_{i\in S} , and every bounded
 ℱ ∞ \mathcal{F}_{\infty} -measurable  H H , 
 

 
 
 𝔼 [ H 𝟏 { S ∩ 𝒬 = ∅ } f ( ( U i ) i ∈ S ) ] = 𝔼 [ H 𝟏 { S ∩ 𝒬 = ∅ } ] ∫ f d ⨂ i ∈ S μ i . \mathbb{E}\!\left[H\mathbf{1}_{\{S\cap\mathcal{Q}=\varnothing\}}f((U_{i})_{i\in S})\right]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[H\mathbf{1}_{\{S\cap\mathcal{Q}=\varnothing\}}\right]\int f\,d\!\bigotimes_{i\in S}\mu_{i}. 
 
 (2.1) 
 
 Thus, conditionally on the terminal exploration sigma-field, the
unqueried coordinates have their original product law in the precise
finite-dimensional sense of ( 2.1 ). 
 
 
 The conclusion remains valid when a query reports only the cell
containing  U Q n U_{Q_{n}}  in a finite measurable partition chosen before
that coordinate is read. At a finite history of positive probability,
the queried coordinates have the product of their one-coordinate
conditional laws, while unqueried coordinates retain their original
laws. At a terminal history the same assertion is understood as an
identity of regular conditional kernels, determined by its
finite-dimensional integrals. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Stop the algorithm after  n n  queries and write
 ℱ n \mathcal{F}_{n}  and  𝒬 n \mathcal{Q}_{n}  for the resulting sigma-field and
queried set. Induction on  n n  gives 
 

 
 
 𝔼 [ H n 𝟏 { S ∩ 𝒬 n = ∅ } f ( ( U i ) i ∈ S ) ] = 𝔼 [ H n 𝟏 { S ∩ 𝒬 n = ∅ } ] ∫ f d ⨂ i ∈ S μ i \mathbb{E}\!\left[H_{n}\mathbf{1}_{\{S\cap\mathcal{Q}_{n}=\varnothing\}}f((U_{i})_{i\in S})\right]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[H_{n}\mathbf{1}_{\{S\cap\mathcal{Q}_{n}=\varnothing\}}\right]\int f\,d\!\bigotimes_{i\in S}\mu_{i} 
 
 
 for every bounded  ℱ n \mathcal{F}_{n} -measurable  H n H_{n} . Indeed, the next
query identity is measurable before its coordinate is read. On the
branch  Q n + 1 ∈ S Q_{n+1}\in S  the indicator on both sides vanishes; on the
other branches the newly read variable is independent of
 ( U i ) i ∈ S (U_{i})_{i\in S} , so conditioning on  ℱ n \mathcal{F}_{n}  preserves the
factorization. 
 
 
 For bounded  H ∈ ℱ ∞ H\in\mathcal{F}_{\infty} , put
 H n = 𝔼 ⁡ [ H ∣ ℱ n ] H_{n}=\mathbb{E}[H\mid\mathcal{F}_{n}] . Martingale convergence gives
 H n → H H_{n}\to H  in  L 1 L^{1} , and
 𝟏 { S ∩ 𝒬 n = ∅ } \mathbf{1}_{\{S\cap\mathcal{Q}_{n}=\varnothing\}}  decreases to
 𝟏 { S ∩ 𝒬 = ∅ } \mathbf{1}_{\{S\cap\mathcal{Q}=\varnothing\}} . Passing to the limit
proves ( 2.1 ). If a query reports a partition
cell rather than the full coordinate, the finite-stage joint density is
the product of the indicators of the reported cells times the original
product density. Dividing by its probability gives the product of the
restricted one-coordinate laws. The same martingale argument passes this
identity to the terminal sigma-field.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 2.2  Shared junctions and lower semicontinuity 

 
 Lemma 2.2   (Finite Bernoulli disjoint occurrence) . 
 
 Let  I I  be finite and let  ℙ \mathbb{P}  be a Bernoulli product measure
on  { 0 , 1 } I \{0,1\}^{I} . For events  A , B ⊆ { 0 , 1 } I A,B\subseteq\{0,1\}^{I} , let
 A □ B A\mathbin{\square}B  be the event that there are disjoint coordinate
sets  K , L ⊆ I K,L\subseteq I  such that fixing the coordinates of the current
configuration on  K K  certifies  A A , while fixing those on  L L 
certifies  B B . Then 
 

 
 
 ℙ ⁡ ( A □ B ) ≤ ℙ ⁡ ( A ) ​ ℙ ​ ( B ) . \mathbb{P}(A\mathbin{\square}B)\leq\mathbb{P}(A)\mathbb{P}(B). 
 
 (2.2) 
 
 For increasing  A , B A,B , this is the inequality introduced by van den
Berg and Kesten  [ 26 ] ; the logical input used here,
for arbitrary  A , B A,B , is the main product-space theorem of
 [ 19 ] . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The cited Reimer theorem gives ( 2.2 ) for arbitrary events
in a finite product probability space, and therefore also its increasing
specialization. The separate van den Berg–Kesten citation records the
original increasing-event result used historically in percolation.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma 2.3   (Shared-junction BK inequality) . 
 
 Let  0 < p ≤ 1 0<p\leq 1 , let  T T  be a finite tree rooted at  r r , and map
its vertices injectively to  J = { x a : a ∈ V ⁡ ( T ) } ⊆ V ⁡ ( G ) J=\{x_{a}:a\in V(T)\}\subseteq V(G) .
For each  a ​ b ∈ E ⁡ ( T ) ab\in E(T) , let  𝒞 a ​ b \mathscr{C}_{ab}  be a two-terminal
open-path event that includes the openness of  x a , x b x_{a},x_{b} , has a witness
whose internal vertices avoid  J J , and whose restriction is independent
of the states of  J ∖ { x a , x b } J\setminus\{x_{a},x_{b}\} . After conditioning every
site of  J J  open, write 
 

 
 
 □ V ∖ J { 𝒞 a ​ b : a ​ b ∈ E ⁡ ( T ) } \mathop{\square}_{V\setminus J}\{\mathscr{C}_{ab}:ab\in E(T)\} 
 
 
 for ordinary BK disjoint occurrence in the residual product space
 { 0 , 1 } V ∖ J \{0,1\}^{V\setminus J} : each residual event has a finite witness
set contained in  V ∖ J V\setminus J , and these witness sets are pairwise
disjoint. Thus the symbol  □ V ∖ J \square_{V\setminus J}  permits no shared
residual coordinate; only the already conditioned coordinates in  J J 
are omitted from the witnesses. Let  𝒜 \mathscr{A}  be the corresponding
unconditional event, including the required openness of the junctions.
Then 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( 𝒜 | x r ​  open ) ≤ ∏ a ​ b ∈ E ⁡ ( T ) ℙ p ​ ( 𝒞 a ​ b ) p . \mathbb{P}_{p}\left(\mathscr{A}\,\middle|\,x_{r}\text{ open}\right)\leq\prod_{ab\in E(T)}\frac{\mathbb{P}_{p}(\mathscr{C}_{ab})}{p}. 
 
 (2.3) 
 
 In particular, if  𝒜 T \mathscr{A}_{T}  is the event that every edge  a ​ b ab 
is represented by an open connection between  x a , x b x_{a},x_{b} , with the
representing paths disjoint apart from prescribed common endpoints, then 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( 𝒜 T ∣ x r ​  open ) ≤ ∏ a ​ b ∈ E ⁡ ( T ) ℙ p ​ ( x a ↔ x b ) p . \mathbb{P}_{p}(\mathscr{A}_{T}\mid x_{r}\text{ open})\leq\prod_{ab\in E(T)}\frac{\mathbb{P}_{p}(x_{a}\leftrightarrow x_{b})}{p}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Condition first on every site of  J J  being open. First require every
residual witness to lie in a common finite set  Λ ⊇ J \Lambda\supseteq J ,
and denote the resulting restrictions by  𝒞 a ​ b , Λ \mathscr{C}_{ab,\Lambda} 
and  𝒜 Λ \mathscr{A}_{\Lambda} , respectively.
By the definition of  □ V ∖ J \square_{V\setminus J} , the path interiors are
completely disjoint witness sets in the finite product space on
 Λ ∖ J \Lambda\setminus J . Iterating Lemma  2.2  gives 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( 𝒜 Λ | J ​  open ) ≤ ∏ a ​ b ∈ E ⁡ ( T ) ℙ p ​ ( 𝒞 a ​ b , Λ ∣ J ​  open ) . \mathbb{P}_{p}\left(\mathscr{A}_{\Lambda}\,\middle|\,J\text{ open}\right)\leq\prod_{ab\in E(T)}\mathbb{P}_{p}(\mathscr{C}_{ab,\Lambda}\mid J\text{ open}). 
 
 
 Along an increasing finite exhaustion of  V V , each side increases to
the corresponding unrestricted finite-path event: every witness uses
only finitely many vertices. Continuity from below therefore gives the
same display with  𝒜 \mathscr{A}  and  𝒞 a ​ b \mathscr{C}_{ab} .
By the stated insensitivity to the other junction coordinates, 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( 𝒞 a ​ b ∣ J ​  open ) = ℙ p ​ ( 𝒞 a ​ b ∣ x a , x b ​  open ) = ℙ p ​ ( 𝒞 a ​ b ) p 2 . \mathbb{P}_{p}(\mathscr{C}_{ab}\mid J\text{ open})=\mathbb{P}_{p}(\mathscr{C}_{ab}\mid x_{a},x_{b}\text{ open})=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{p}(\mathscr{C}_{ab})}{p^{2}}. 
 
 
 Under the law conditioned on  x r x_{r}  being open, the other
 | V ⁡ ( T ) | − 1 |V(T)|-1  junctions are open with probability  p | V ⁡ ( T ) | − 1 p^{|V(T)|-1} .
Since  | E ⁡ ( T ) | = | V ⁡ ( T ) | − 1 |E(T)|=|V(T)|-1 , multiplication cancels one of the two endpoint
factors for each edge and proves ( 2.3 ). For the final
assertion, observe that an interior cannot contain a third junction: that
junction is an endpoint of another representing path, contradicting the
assumed disjointness. Hence every witnessing arm is internally contained
in  V ∖ J V\setminus J , and its restricted probability is at most the
corresponding unrestricted connectivity probability.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma 2.4   (Lower semicontinuity) . 
 
 If  G n → G G_{n}\to G , then 
 

 
 
 p c s ​ ( G ) ≤ lim inf n p c s ​ ( G n ) . p_{c}^{\rm s}(G)\leq\liminf_{n}p_{c}^{\rm s}(G_{n}). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The case  p = 0 p=0  is immediate, so fix
 0 < p < p c s ​ ( G ) 0<p<p_{c}^{\rm s}(G) . The vertex-cut characterization
 [ 16 , Theorem 2.3]  of the site critical point gives a vertex cutset  Π \Pi ,
separating  o o 
from infinity, such that 
 

 
 
 s Π := ∑ v ∈ Π ℙ p ​ ( o ↔ v ​  without meeting  ​ Π ∖ { v } ) < min ⁡ { 1 , p } . s_{\Pi}:=\sum_{v\in\Pi}\mathbb{P}_{p}(o\leftrightarrow v\text{ without meeting }\Pi\setminus\{v\})<\min\{1,p\}. 
 
 (2.4) 
 
 In particular  o ∉ Π o\notin\Pi , since otherwise its summand is  p p .
The component  C C  of  o o  in  G ∖ Π G\setminus\Pi  is finite; otherwise
local finiteness and Konig’s infinity lemma give a ray avoiding  Π \Pi .
Replace  Π \Pi  by the finite set
 Π ′ = { v ∈ Π : v ∼ C } \Pi^{\prime}=\{v\in\Pi:v\sim C\} . This is still a cutset. Moreover, for
 v ∈ Π ′ v\in\Pi^{\prime} , a path from  o o  to  v v  avoiding the other vertices of
 Π ′ \Pi^{\prime}  stays in  C C  until its last vertex, so its event is unchanged
if  Π \Pi  is replaced by  Π ′ \Pi^{\prime} . Thus the sum for  Π ′ \Pi^{\prime}  is no
larger than the sum in ( 2.4 ). Relabel  Π ′ \Pi^{\prime}  as  Π \Pi .
Choose  R R  so that the closed one-neighborhood of  C ∪ Π C\cup\Pi , and
hence every edge incident to this finite template, is contained in
 B R ​ ( o ) B_{R}(o) . The event in each summand of ( 2.4 ) then has a witness inside
 C ∪ { v } C\cup\{v\} , and the assertion that  Π \Pi  separates this component
uses only the incident edges just included. Consequently ( 2.4 ) depends
on the rooted  R R -ball and has the same value in  G n G_{n}  for all large
 n n . By transitivity the same finite rooted
cutset template is available at every vertex of  G n G_{n} . Fix one
automorphic copy  ( C x , Π x ) (C_{x},\Pi_{x})  rooted at each vertex  x x . Along every
self-avoiding ray starting at  o o , stop first when it exits  C o C_{o} ,
then first when its remaining tail exits the copy rooted at that stopping
vertex, and so on. Every stopping time is finite because every  C x C_{x}  is
finite, and the successive path segments have disjoint interiors; only
consecutive segments share their prescribed stopping vertex. 
 
 
 For  v ∈ Π v\in\Pi , write
 a v = ℙ p ​ ( o ↔ v ​  without meeting  ​ Π ∖ { v } ) a_{v}=\mathbb{P}_{p}(o\leftrightarrow v\text{ without meeting }\Pi\setminus\{v\}) . For any prescribed sequence of  k k  exits, the
formal shared-junction inequality, Lemma  2.3 , applied
to the path tree with the restricted segment events bounds the
probability of its disjoint segment witnesses
by 
 

 
 
 p ​ ∏ i = 1 k a v i p . p\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{a_{v_{i}}}{p}. 
 
 
 This remains an upper bound if different copied templates overlap: the
chronological pieces of the self-avoiding witness ray still have disjoint
interiors. Summing over the finitely many exit choices at each stage shows
that the probability that some open ray reaches its  k k -th stopping
vertex is at most 
 

 
 
 p ​ ( s Π p ) k . p\left(\frac{s_{\Pi}}{p}\right)^{k}. 
 
 
 It tends to zero, so  o o  does not percolate in  G n G_{n}  at  p p . Thus
 p ≤ p c s ​ ( G n ) p\leq p_{c}^{\rm s}(G_{n})  eventually. Let
 p ↑ p c s ​ ( G ) p\uparrow p_{c}^{\rm s}(G) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 3  Site two-ghost inequality 

 
 For a finite open cluster  K K , put 
 

 
 
 ∂ V K = { z ∉ K : z ∼ x ​  for some  ​ x ∈ K } , T ⁡ ( K ) = K ∪ ∂ V K , \partial_{V}K=\{z\notin K:z\sim x\text{ for some }x\in K\},\qquad T(K)=K\cup\partial_{V}K, 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 H p ​ ( K ) = p ​ | ∂ V K | − ( 1 − p ) ​ | K | . H_{p}(K)=p|\partial_{V}K|-(1-p)|K|. 
 
 
 When the root  o o  is closed, we set  K o = ∅ K_{o}=\varnothing . Every expression
below containing a quotient by  | K o | |K_{o}|  or  | T ⁡ ( K o ) | |T(K_{o})|  is, by
convention, zero on this event; each quotient is evaluated only when the
root is open.
Let  𝒢 h \mathcal{G}_{h}  be an independent ghost field on vertices, each
vertex being marked with probability  1 − e − h 1-e^{-h} . Let  𝒯 v \mathscr{T}_{v} 
be the event that  v v  is closed and touches two distinct open clusters
that both meet  𝒢 h \mathcal{G}_{h} , at least one of which is finite. 
 
 
 Lemma 3.1   (Site two-ghost) . 
 
 Let  0 < p < 1 0<p<1  and  h > 0 h>0 . On a unimodular transitive graph of
degree  d d , 
 

 
 
 ℙ p , h ​ ( 𝒯 v ) ≤ C d ​ 1 − p p ​ h . \mathbb{P}_{p,h}(\mathscr{T}_{v})\leq C_{d}\sqrt{\frac{1-p}{p}\,h}. 
 
 (3.1) 
 
 Consequently, if  𝒮 v , m \mathscr{S}_{v,m}  is the event that  v v  is
closed and touches two distinct clusters of size at least  m m , at
least one finite, then 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( 𝒮 v , m ) ≤ C d ​ 1 − p p ​ m . \mathbb{P}_{p}(\mathscr{S}_{v,m})\leq C_{d}\sqrt{\frac{1-p}{pm}}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof.  Force  v v  closed temporarily. Let  N f ​ ( v ) N_{f}(v)  be the number
of distinct finite, ghost-marked open clusters adjacent to  v v , and let
 I ⁡ ( v ) I(v)  indicate that an infinite open cluster is adjacent to  v v .
Every infinite cluster meets the positive-intensity ghost field almost
surely. We have 
 
 
 
 
 1 𝒯 v \displaystyle 1_{\mathscr{T}_{v}} 
 ≤ 1 { v ​ closed } ​ [ N f ​ ( v ) − 1 { N f ​ ( v ) ≥ 1 , I ⁡ ( v ) = 0 } ] , \displaystyle\leq 1_{\{v\ {\rm closed}\}}\left[N_{f}(v)-1_{\{N_{f}(v)\geq 1,\ I(v)=0\}}\right], 
 
 (3.2) 
 
 
 ℙ ⁡ ( v ​  closed ​ ∣ ω | V ∖ { v } ) \displaystyle\mathbb{P}(v\text{ closed}\mid\omega|_{V\setminus\{v\}}) 
 = 1 − p p ​ ℙ ​ ( v ​  open ​ ∣ ω | V ∖ { v } ) . \displaystyle=\frac{1-p}{p}\mathbb{P}(v\text{ open}\mid\omega|_{V\setminus\{v\}}). 
 
 
 Indeed, the bracket is  r − 1 ≥ 1 r-1\geq 1  when  v v  touches  r ≥ 2 r\geq 2 
finite marked clusters and is  r ≥ 1 r\geq 1  when it also touches an infinite
cluster. 
 
 
 For a finite cluster  K K , write
 G ⁡ ( K ) = | K ∩ 𝒢 h | G(K)=|K\cap\mathcal{G}_{h}| , and distribute its contribution uniformly
over  T ⁡ ( K ) T(K) . More explicitly, a closed boundary vertex  v v  sends
 | T ⁡ ( K ) | − 1 |T(K)|^{-1}  to each vertex of  T ⁡ ( K ) T(K)  for every adjacent finite
ghost-marked cluster  K K . An open vertex  v v  with finite cluster
 K v K_{v}  sends  − ( 1 − p ) / ( p | T ( K v ) | ) -(1-p)/(p|T(K_{v})|)  to every vertex of  T ⁡ ( K v ) T(K_{v})  when
 G ⁡ ( K v ∖ { v } ) ≥ 1 G(K_{v}\setminus\{v\})\geq 1 . Equivalently, the contribution made by a
sender  x ∈ T ⁡ ( K ) x\in T(K) , to each recipient in  T ⁡ ( K ) T(K) , is 
 

 
 
 1 | T ⁡ ( K ) | [ 1 { x ∈ ∂ V K } 1 { G ( K ) ≥ 1 } − 1 − p p 1 { x ∈ K } 1 { G ( K ∖ { x } ) ≥ 1 } ] . \frac{1}{|T(K)|}\left[1_{\{x\in\partial_{V}K\}}1_{\{G(K)\geq 1\}}-\frac{1-p}{p}1_{\{x\in K\}}1_{\{G(K\setminus\{x\})\geq 1\}}\right]. 
 
 (3.3) 
 
 This mass is diagonally invariant. Its absolute outgoing mass is at most
 d + ( 1 − p ) / p d+(1-p)/p , so the unimodular mass-transport principle
 [ 3 , Corollary 3.5]  applies to the
positive and negative parts as justified below. Summing ( 3.3 ) over
senders gives, for each  K K , 
 

 
 
 H p ​ ( K ) p ​ | T ⁡ ( K ) | 1 { G ( K ) ≥ 1 } + 1 − p p ​ | T ⁡ ( K ) | 1 { G ( K ) = 1 } . \frac{H_{p}(K)}{p|T(K)|}1_{\{G(K)\geq 1\}}+\frac{1-p}{p|T(K)|}1_{\{G(K)=1\}}. 
 
 (3.4) 
 
 For clarity, the signed use is justified without an implicit
integrability convention. Split ( 3.3 ) into its positive and negative
parts and, for mass sent from  x x , first retain only clusters  K K 
with  | K | ≤ n |K|\leq n  and  K ⊆ B n ​ ( x ) K\subseteq B_{n}(x) . This truncation remains
diagonally invariant. Apply the nonnegative mass-transport principle to
each truncated part. The absolute outgoing mass is bounded by
 d + ( 1 − p ) / p d+(1-p)/p , uniformly in  n n ; nonnegative mass transport gives the
same bound for the incoming mass. Monotone convergence for the two
parts, followed by subtraction, gives ( 3.4 ).
The last term is the endpoint correction: when  K K  has exactly one
ghost, the marked vertex does not contribute to the deletion event in
the second term of ( 3.3 ). The expected outgoing mass is precisely the
expectation of the upper bound in the first line of ( 3.2 ), after the
open/closed switch at its source. 
 
 
 A vertex lies in at most  d d  external cluster boundaries and at most
one open cluster. We use the following cluster-averaging identity. For
every nonnegative invariant functional  f f  of a finite open cluster,
mass transport, first spreading  f ⁡ ( K ) f(K)  uniformly over  T ⁡ ( K ) T(K)  and
then uniformly over  K K , gives 
 

 
 
 𝔼 ∑ K : o ∈ T ⁡ ( K ) f ⁡ ( K ) | T ⁡ ( K ) | = 𝔼 [ f ⁡ ( K o ) | K o | 𝟏 { o ​  open , | K o | < ∞ } ] . \mathbb{E}\sum_{K:o\in T(K)}\frac{f(K)}{|T(K)|}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{f(K_{o})}{|K_{o}|}\mathbf{1}_{\{o\text{ open},\ |K_{o}|<\infty\}}\right]. 
 
 
 Since  | T ⁡ ( K ) | ≤ ( d + 1 ) ​ | K | |T(K)|\leq(d+1)|K| , the right side is at most
 ( d + 1 ) ​ 𝔼 ​ [ f ⁡ ( K o ) / | T ⁡ ( K o ) | ; o ​  open , | K o | < ∞ ] (d+1)\mathbb{E}[f(K_{o})/|T(K_{o})|;o\text{ open},|K_{o}|<\infty] .
Apply this separately to the absolute first term and to the positive
correction in ( 3.4 ). Thus ( 3.2 )–( 3.4 ), transitivity, and the triangle
inequality give 
 

 
 
 ℙ p , h ( 𝒯 v ) ≤ d + 1 p 𝔼 p [ | H p ​ ( K o ) | | T ⁡ ( K o ) | 1 { | K o | < ∞ , K o ∩ 𝒢 h ≠ ∅ } ] + C d 1 − p p h . \mathbb{P}_{p,h}(\mathscr{T}_{v})\leq\frac{d+1}{p}\,\mathbb{E}_{p}\left[\frac{|H_{p}(K_{o})|}{|T(K_{o})|}1_{\{|K_{o}|<\infty,\ K_{o}\cap\mathcal{G}_{h}\neq\varnothing\}}\right]+C_{d}\frac{1-p}{p}h. 
 
 (3.5) 
 
 
 
 Explore  o o  first and, when it is open, successively explore untested
vertices on the external boundary of the discovered cluster. If  T T 
is the number tested when exploration stops, then on a finite nonempty
cluster  T = | T ⁡ ( K o ) | T=|T(K_{o})| . The process 
 

 
 
 Z n = ∑ i = 1 n ∧ T [ ( 1 − p ) ​ 1 { i ​ th site open } − p ​ 1 { i ​ th site closed } ] Z_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n\wedge T}\left[(1-p)1_{\{i\text{th site open}\}}-p1_{\{i\text{th site closed}\}}\right] 
 
 
 is a martingale,  Z T = − H p ​ ( K o ) Z_{T}=-H_{p}(K_{o}) , and
 𝔼 ​ Z n 2 ≤ p ⁡ ( 1 − p ) ​ n \mathbb{E}Z_{n}^{2}\leq p(1-p)n . The dyadic stopping-time argument and
Doob’s  L 2 L^{2}  inequality yield 
 

 
 
 𝔼 p [ | H p ​ ( K o ) | | T ⁡ ( K o ) | ( 1 − e − h ​ | K o | ) 1 { | K o | < ∞ } ] ≤ C p ⁡ ( 1 − p ) ​ h . \mathbb{E}_{p}\left[\frac{|H_{p}(K_{o})|}{|T(K_{o})|}(1-e^{-h|K_{o}|})1_{\{|K_{o}|<\infty\}}\right]\leq C\sqrt{p(1-p)h}. 
 
 
 Only the event  { o ​  open , | K o | < ∞ } \{o\text{ open},|K_{o}|<\infty\}  contributes to the
left side. On the closed-root event the quotient is zero by convention,
and on the infinite-cluster event it is removed by the displayed
indicator. Thus no assertion that the exploration stops almost surely is
being used. For each dyadic block below,  T < 2 j + 1 T<2^{j+1}  is a finite
stopping event and  | Z T | ≤ max i ≤ 2 j + 1 ⁡ | Z i | |Z_{T}|\leq\max_{i\leq 2^{j+1}}|Z_{i}| ; all applications
of Doob’s inequality are therefore to a bounded-time martingale.
Indeed,  | K o | ≤ T |K_{o}|\leq T  and
 1 − e − h ​ | K o | ≤ min ⁡ { h ​ T , 1 } 1-e^{-h|K_{o}|}\leq\min\{hT,1\} . Split  T < ∞ T<\infty  into the
events  2 j ≤ T < 2 j + 1 2^{j}\leq T<2^{j+1} . On blocks below  h − 1 h^{-1} , the summand
is at most  h ​ max i ≤ 2 j + 1 ​ | Z i | h\max_{i\leq 2^{j+1}}|Z_{i}| ; on blocks above  h − 1 h^{-1} ,
it is at most  2 − j ​ max i ≤ 2 j + 1 ​ | Z i | 2^{-j}\max_{i\leq 2^{j+1}}|Z_{i}| . Doob’s inequality
gives 
 

 
 
 𝔼 ​ max i ≤ 2 j + 1 ​ | Z i | ≤ 2 ​ p ⁡ ( 1 − p ) ​ 2 j + 1 . \mathbb{E}\max_{i\leq 2^{j+1}}|Z_{i}|\leq 2\sqrt{p(1-p)2^{j+1}}. 
 
 
 The two resulting geometric sums are each at most
 C ​ p ⁡ ( 1 − p ) ​ h C\sqrt{p(1-p)h} . The same conclusion for  h > 1 h>1  follows by
starting the second sum at  j = 0 j=0 .
Conditional on  K o K_{o} , the correction in ( 3.5 ) is bounded by
 ( 1 − p ) ​ h / p (1-p)h/p , since
 ℙ ⁡ ( G ⁡ ( K o ) = 1 ∣ K o ) ≤ h ​ | K o | \mathbb{P}(G(K_{o})=1\mid K_{o})\leq h|K_{o}|  and
the cluster-averaged denominator for this term is  | K o | |K_{o}| . Put
 x = ( 1 − p ) ​ h / p x=(1-p)h/p . If  x ≤ 1 x\leq 1 , then
 x ≤ x x\leq\sqrt{x} ; if  x > 1 x>1 , use the trivial probability bound
 1 ≤ x 1\leq\sqrt{x} . Substitution into ( 3.5 ) proves ( 3.1 ). 
 
 
 On  𝒮 v , m \mathscr{S}_{v,m} , each large cluster meets the ghost field with
conditional probability at least  1 − e − h ​ m 1-e^{-hm} . Positive association of
the ghost variables gives 
 

 
 
 ( 1 − e − h ​ m ) 2 ​ ℙ p ​ ( 𝒮 v , m ) ≤ ℙ p , h ​ ( 𝒯 v ) . (1-e^{-hm})^{2}\mathbb{P}_{p}(\mathscr{S}_{v,m})\leq\mathbb{P}_{p,h}(\mathscr{T}_{v}). 
 
 
 Take  h = 1 / m h=1/m  and absorb  ( 1 − e − 1 ) − 2 (1-e^{-1})^{-2}  into  C d C_{d} .
 □ \square 
 
 
 
 4  Finite-energy path opening 

 
 Lemma 4.1   (Finite-energy path opening) . 
 
 Let  G G  be a unimodular transitive graph of degree at most  d d , let
 0 < p < 1 0<p<1 , and suppose that Bernoulli  p p -site percolation has almost
surely at most one infinite open cluster. Let  m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 . If
 x , y ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) x,y\in V(G)  are joined by a fixed simple path of length  ℓ \ell , then 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( | K o | ≥ m ) 2 ≤ ℙ p ​ ( x ↔ y ) + C d ​ ( ℓ + 1 ) ​ ( 2 / p ) ℓ + 1 ​ 1 − p p ​ m . \mathbb{P}_{p}(|K_{o}|\geq m)^{2}\leq\mathbb{P}_{p}(x\leftrightarrow y)+C_{d}(\ell+1)(2/p)^{\ell+1}\sqrt{\frac{1-p}{pm}}. 
 
 (4.1) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By site FKG and transitivity, 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( | K x | ≥ m , | K y | ≥ m ) ≥ ℙ p ​ ( | K o | ≥ m ) 2 . \mathbb{P}_{p}(|K_{x}|\geq m,|K_{y}|\geq m)\geq\mathbb{P}_{p}(|K_{o}|\geq m)^{2}. 
 
 
 The part of this event on which  x ↔ y x\leftrightarrow y  has probability
at most  ℙ p ​ ( x ↔ y ) \mathbb{P}_{p}(x\leftrightarrow y) . On the complementary event,
scan the fixed path from  x x  to  y y , changing a closed path site to
open whenever it is encountered, and stop immediately before the first
change that would join the two clusters containing the original clusters
of  x x  and  y y . Such a step exists because opening the entire path
joins them. Let  z z  be the still-closed site at that step and let  S S 
be the set of earlier path sites changed from closed to open. In the
current configuration,  z z  touches two distinct clusters, each
containing one of the original clusters and hence each of size at least
 m m . At least one of these two clusters is finite. Indeed, outside a
null event the original configuration has at most one infinite cluster,
and opening the finite set  S S  cannot increase the number of infinite
clusters: every infinite cluster after the modification must contain an
infinite cluster from the original configuration, since  S S  and its
set of incident original clusters are finite. Thus the current
configuration belongs to  𝒮 z , m \mathscr{S}_{z,m} . 
 
 
 Map the original configuration to the triple consisting of this current
configuration,  z z , and  S S . For a fixed triple the original
configuration is recovered by closing the sites of  S S , so the map has
at most one preimage. There are at most  ℓ + 1 \ell+1  choices for  z z  and
at most  2 ℓ + 1 2^{\ell+1}  choices for  S S . If  | S | = s |S|=s , the ratio of the
Bernoulli weight of the original configuration to that of its image is
 ( ( 1 − p ) / p ) s ≤ p − ( ℓ + 1 ) ((1-p)/p)^{s}\leq p^{-(\ell+1)} . Integrating the unchanged coordinates,
summing the recorded data, using transitivity, and applying
Lemma  3.1  gives 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ( | K x | ≥ m , | K y | ≥ m , x ↮ y ) ≤ ( ℓ + 1 ) ( 2 / p ) ℓ + 1 ℙ p ( 𝒮 o , m ) ≤ C d ( ℓ + 1 ) ( 2 / p ) ℓ + 1 1 − p p ​ m . \mathbb{P}_{p}(|K_{x}|\geq m,|K_{y}|\geq m,x\not\leftrightarrow y)\leq(\ell+1)(2/p)^{\ell+1}\mathbb{P}_{p}(\mathscr{S}_{o,m})\leq C_{d}(\ell+1)(2/p)^{\ell+1}\sqrt{\frac{1-p}{pm}}. 
 
 
 Combining the connected and disconnected cases proves
( 4.1 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 5  Nonunimodular site percolation 

 
 The ordinary mass-transport estimate above is not available for a
nonunimodular transitive action. Here the correct substitute is the
tilted height analysis of Hutchcroft. 
 
 
 Throughout this section, let  G G  be infinite, connected, locally finite,
and vertex-transitive, and suppose that its canonical action is
nonunimodular. Set  Γ = Aut ⁡ ( G ) \Gamma=\operatorname{Aut}(G) . All modular quantities
below refer to this full automorphism action; the subscript  Γ \Gamma  is
suppressed only to keep the formulas readable. Let
 Δ = Δ Γ \Delta=\Delta_{\Gamma}  be the modular cocycle, put
 t 0 = max x ∼ y ⁡ | log ⁡ Δ ⁡ ( x , y ) | > 0 t_{0}=\max_{x\sim y}|\log\Delta(x,y)|>0 , and reverse an edge attaining
the maximum if necessary. Transitivity and invariance of the modular
cocycle then give, for every  z z , a neighbor  z + z^{+}  such that
 log ⁡ Δ ⁡ ( z , z + ) = t 0 \log\Delta(z,z^{+})=t_{0} . Fix one such choice for each  z z ; equivariance
of this auxiliary choice will not be used. Likewise, fix for each  z z 
a neighbor  z − z^{-}  satisfying  log ⁡ Δ ⁡ ( z , z − ) = − t 0 \log\Delta(z,z^{-})=-t_{0} . Let
 H t = { v : log ⁡ Δ ⁡ ( o , v ) ≥ t } H_{t}=\{v:\log\Delta(o,v)\geq t\} . Define 
 

 
 
 A p s ​ ( t ) = ℙ p ​ ( o ↔ H t ​  inside  ​ H 0 ) , A ^ p s ​ ( t ) = A p s ​ ( t ) p . A_{p}^{\rm s}(t)=\mathbb{P}_{p}(o\leftrightarrow H_{t}\text{ inside }H_{0}),\qquad\widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(t)=\frac{A_{p}^{\rm s}(t)}{p}. 
 
 
 The division by  p p  conditions the shared starting site to be open.
There is a necessary one-layer buffer in the site setting. Namely, 
 

 
 
 A ^ p s ​ ( t + s + t 0 ) ≥ p ​ A ^ p s ​ ( t ) ​ A ^ p s ​ ( s ) ( s , t ≥ 0 ) . \widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(t+s+t_{0})\geq p\,\widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(t)\widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(s)\qquad(s,t\geq 0). 
 
 (5.1) 
 
 To prove ( 5.1 ), explore the open-root cluster below raw height  t t ,
testing sites in  H t H_{t}  only when first reached from below and never
continuing the exploration from such a site. Every tested first-hit site
has height strictly less than  t + t 0 t+t_{0} . On the event of a hit, choose an
open first-hit site  z z  measurably and let  z + z^{+}  be a neighbor with
 log ⁡ Δ ⁡ ( z , z + ) = t 0 \log\Delta(z,z^{+})=t_{0} . Then  z + z^{+}  has height at least  t + t 0 t+t_{0} , so
its label and every label in  H 0 ​ ( z + ) ∖ { z + } H_{0}(z^{+})\setminus\{z^{+}\}  are untested.
Let  ℱ t \mathcal{F}_{t}  be the sigma-field generated by this stopped
exploration, including the selected first-hit site. If
 y ∈ H 0 ​ ( z + ) y\in H_{0}(z^{+}) , the cocycle identity gives 
 

 
 
 log ⁡ Δ ⁡ ( o , y ) = log ⁡ Δ ⁡ ( o , z + ) + log ⁡ Δ ⁡ ( z + , y ) ≥ t + t 0 . \log\Delta(o,y)=\log\Delta(o,z^{+})+\log\Delta(z^{+},y)\geq t+t_{0}. 
 
 
 Thus none of the coordinates used by a continuation inside
 H 0 ​ ( z + ) H_{0}(z^{+})  has been queried. The product exploration property implies
that, conditional on  ℱ t \mathcal{F}_{t} , these coordinates are independent
Bernoulli variables of parameter  p p . Hence the conditional probability
that  z + z^{+}  is open and connects inside  H 0 ​ ( z + ) H_{0}(z^{+})  to relative raw
height  s s  is exactly 
 

 
 
 A p s ​ ( s ) = p ​ A ^ p s ​ ( s ) . A_{p}^{\rm s}(s)=p\widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(s). 
 
 
 Under the open-root law, the probability that the first exploration hits
 H t H_{t}  is  A ^ p s ​ ( t ) \widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(t) . On the intersection of these two
events, concatenate the first-hit path, the edge  z ​ z + zz^{+} , and the
continuation. The resulting path stays in  H 0 ​ ( o ) H_{0}(o)  and ends at raw
height at least  t + t 0 + s t+t_{0}+s . Taking the conditional expectation first
over  ℱ t \mathcal{F}_{t}  and then over the initial exploration gives 
 

 
 
 A ^ p s ​ ( t + s + t 0 ) ≥ A ^ p s ​ ( t ) ​ p ​ A ^ p s ​ ( s ) , \widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(t+s+t_{0})\geq\widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(t)p\widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(s), 
 
 
 which is ( 5.1 ). In particular, no site tested closed on the first
interface is used by the continuation. 
 
 
 Put  c p = − log ⁡ p c_{p}=-\log p  and
 f p ​ ( t ) = − log ⁡ A ^ p s ​ ( t ) f_{p}(t)=-\log\widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(t) . Equation ( 5.1 ) says 
 

 
 
 f p ​ ( t + s + t 0 ) ≤ f p ​ ( t ) + f p ​ ( s ) + c p . f_{p}(t+s+t_{0})\leq f_{p}(t)+f_{p}(s)+c_{p}. 
 
 
 Consequently  b p ​ ( n ) := f p ​ ( ( n − 1 ) ​ t 0 ) + c p b_{p}(n):=f_{p}((n-1)t_{0})+c_{p} ,  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , is subadditive.
Fekete’s lemma and monotonicity of  f p f_{p}  between consecutive multiples
of  t 0 t_{0}  show that 
 

 
 
 α p s := − lim t → ∞ t − 1 log A ^ p s ( t ) \alpha_{p}^{\rm s}:=-\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\log\widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(t) 
 
 
 exists and that 
 

 
 
 h v ​ ( z ) := log ⁡ Δ ⁡ ( v , z ) t 0 h_{v}(z):=\frac{\log\Delta(v,z)}{t_{0}} 
 
 
 is the normalized layer height used in the source. Thus raw height
 t = t 0 ​ r t=t_{0}r  corresponds to normalized height  r r , and the source’s
reference scale is
 exp ¯ ​ ( r ) = e t 0 ​ r \overline{\exp}(r)=e^{t_{0}r} . Consequently 
 

 
 
 − log ⁡ A ^ p s ​ ( t 0 ​ r ) log ⁡ exp ¯ ​ ( r ) = − log ⁡ A ^ p s ​ ( t 0 ​ r ) t 0 ​ r ⟶ α p s . -\frac{\log\widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(t_{0}r)}{\log\overline{\exp}(r)}=-\frac{\log\widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(t_{0}r)}{t_{0}r}\longrightarrow\alpha_{p}^{\rm s}. 
 
 (5.2) 
 
 Equivalently, the decay exponent per layer  r r , with denominator  r r 
rather than  log ⁡ exp ¯ ​ ( r ) \log\overline{\exp}(r) , is
 t 0 ​ α p s t_{0}\alpha_{p}^{\rm s} . The dimensionless exponent used by the source
and by Theorem  5.1  is therefore exactly the
raw-height exponent above, with neither a missing nor an additional
factor of  t 0 t_{0} . 
 
 
 The subadditive formula is 
 

 
 
 α p s = 1 t 0 ​ inf n ≥ 1 f p ​ ( ( n − 1 ) ​ t 0 ) + c p n . \alpha_{p}^{\rm s}=\frac{1}{t_{0}}\inf_{n\geq 1}\frac{f_{p}((n-1)t_{0})+c_{p}}{n}. 
 
 (5.3) 
 
 In particular,  b p ​ ( n ) ≥ n ​ t 0 ​ α p s b_{p}(n)\geq nt_{0}\alpha_{p}^{\rm s} , and hence 
 

 
 
 A ^ p s ​ ( t ) ≤ p − 1 ​ e t 0 ​ α p s ​ e − α p s ​ t ( t ≥ 0 ) . \widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(t)\leq p^{-1}e^{t_{0}\alpha_{p}^{\rm s}}e^{-\alpha_{p}^{\rm s}t}\qquad(t\geq 0). 
 
 (5.4) 
 
 Since division by  p p  is independent of  t t , this is also the
exponential rate obtained from  A p s A_{p}^{\rm s} . 
 
 
 We will use the following continuity fact. For fixed  t t , the event in
 A p s ​ ( t ) A_{p}^{\rm s}(t)  is a union of finite open-path cylinder events. Under
the uniform-label coupling it follows that
 A p n s ​ ( t ) ↑ A p s ​ ( t ) A_{p_{n}}^{\rm s}(t)\uparrow A_{p}^{\rm s}(t)  whenever  p n ↑ p p_{n}\uparrow p .
Thus each numerator in ( 5.3 ) is left-continuous and decreasing on
 ( 0 , 1 ] (0,1] . An infimum of upper-semicontinuous functions is
upper-semicontinuous.
Since  p ↦ α p s p\mapsto\alpha_{p}^{\rm s}  is decreasing, this is exactly left
continuity of  α p s \alpha_{p}^{\rm s} . 
 
 
 Theorem 5.1   (Canonical nonunimodular site theorem) . 
 
 Let  G G  be an infinite, connected, locally
finite, vertex-transitive graph whose canonical action
 Aut ⁡ ( G ) ↷ G \operatorname{Aut}(G)\curvearrowright G  is nonunimodular. The height
exponent defined using the modular cocycle of  Aut ⁡ ( G ) \operatorname{Aut}(G) 
satisfies 
 

 
 
 α p c s s = 1 . \alpha_{p_{c}^{\rm s}}^{\rm s}=1. 
 
 (5.5) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof.  We use the canonical modular cocycle and tilted
mass-transport principle
 [ 15 , Proposition 2.2] , finite vertex Menger
 [ 9 , Theorem 3.3.1] , site sharpness
 [ 2 , Theorem 2 and Section 6] , and the critical
cluster results of  [ 23 , Corollary 5.7 and Remark 5.11] .
All BK, Reimer, exploration, finite-energy, and fractional-moment
estimates required below are proved directly in site coordinates. 
 
 
 First separate the two kinds of conditioning. The source uses an
independent uniform offset  R o ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) R_{o}\in[0,1)  to define Timar’s separating
layers. Transport it equivariantly by 
 
 
 

 
 
 R v = R o − log ⁡ Δ ⁡ ( o , v ) t 0 ( mod 1 ) , L j ​ ( v ) = { x : j + R v − 1 < log ⁡ Δ ⁡ ( v , x ) t 0 ≤ j + R v } , R_{v}=R_{o}-\frac{\log\Delta(o,v)}{t_{0}}\pmod{1},\qquad L_{j}(v)=\left\{x:j+R_{v}-1<\frac{\log\Delta(v,x)}{t_{0}}\leq j+R_{v}\right\}, 
 
 (5.6) 
 
 
 
 where  t 0 t_{0}  and the maximal-increment neighbors are as above. In
particular, if  u ∈ L k ​ ( v ) u\in L_{k}(v) , then 
 

 
 
 k + R v − 1 < log ⁡ Δ ⁡ ( v , u ) t 0 ≤ k + R v ⟹ u + ∈ L k + 1 ​ ( v ) . k+R_{v}-1<\frac{\log\Delta(v,u)}{t_{0}}\leq k+R_{v}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad u^{+}\in L_{k+1}(v). 
 
 (5.7) 
 
 The strict and weak endpoints in ( 5.7 ) are exactly those in ( 5.6 ), so
the conclusion also holds at every boundary offset. This layer field is
independent of site percolation. Thus the conditional-root law used here is 
 
 
 

 
 
 ℙ ^ p v ( ⋅ ) := ℙ p ( ⋅ ∣ v  open ) , τ ^ p ( x , y ) = ℙ p ​ ( x ↔ y ) p . \widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{p}^{v}(\,\cdot\,):=\mathbb{P}_{p}(\,\cdot\mid v\text{ open}),\qquad\widehat{\tau}_{p}(x,y)=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{p}(x\leftrightarrow y)}{p}. 
 
 
 The conversion between the source’s normalized layer coordinate and the
raw height used here is the identity
( 5.2 ). It applies unchanged after conditioning
the root open, because the factor  p − 1 p^{-1}  is independent of height. 
 
 
 When a source formula is conditional on  R v = x R_{v}=x , its site version is
conditional on both  R v = x R_{v}=x  and  v v  being open; we denote this regular
conditional law by  ℙ ^ p v , x \widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{p}^{v,x} . All suprema and
infima over  x ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) x\in[0,1)  are retained. Conditioning on  R v = x R_{v}=x  only
fixes deterministic slabs and therefore does not alter any product-measure
inequality. This distinction prevents the layer offset from being
mistaken for a percolation uniform label.
The half-open convention makes the layers a partition for every fixed
offset, including exceptional offsets of probability zero, and the
cocycle gives the exact shift rule
 u ∈ L k ​ ( v ) ⟹ L j ​ ( u ) = L j + k ​ ( v ) u\in L_{k}(v)\Longrightarrow L_{j}(u)=L_{j+k}(v) .
If two disjoint-occurrence witnesses share a prescribed junction site
 z z , conditioning  z z  open gives 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( ( x ↔ z ) ∘ z ( z ↔ y ) ) ≤ ℙ p ​ ( x ↔ z ) ​ ℙ p ​ ( z ↔ y ) p . \mathbb{P}_{p}\bigl((x\leftrightarrow z)\circ_{z}(z\leftrightarrow y)\bigr)\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}_{p}(x\leftrightarrow z)\mathbb{P}_{p}(z\leftrightarrow y)}{p}. 
 
 (5.8) 
 
 The general form needed here was proved before its first use as
Lemma  2.3 . In the present notation, if  T T  is a
finite rooted tree, its vertices map injectively to
 ( x a ) a ∈ V ⁡ ( T ) (x_{a})_{a\in V(T)} , and  𝒜 T \mathscr{A}_{T}  is the event that its edges
are realized by open paths disjoint apart from their prescribed common
endpoints, that lemma gives 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( 𝒜 T ∣ x r ​  open ) ≤ ∏ a ​ b ∈ E ⁡ ( T ) τ ^ p ​ ( x a , x b ) . \mathbb{P}_{p}(\mathscr{A}_{T}\mid x_{r}\text{ open})\leq\prod_{ab\in E(T)}\widehat{\tau}_{p}(x_{a},x_{b}). 
 
 (5.9) 
 
 A zero-length arm is deleted and its endpoint condition is retained as a
separate coordinate event. Thus ( 5.9 ) also covers the two- and
three-arm decompositions. 
 
 
 This accounts for every path-decomposition BK use in
 [ 15 ] .
The path decompositions in its Lemmas 5.6 and 5.13 are respectively the
path and three-arm cases of ( 5.9 ), and their fractional-moment versions
use the same containments before Holder’s inequality. The blue-edge
argument proving its tilted mean-field bound is replaced by the following
vertex-coordinate calculation. 
 
 
 Write 
 

 
 
 p c s ​ ( λ ) := sup { p : χ p , λ ​ ( v ) < ∞ } . p_{c}^{\rm s}(\lambda):=\sup\{p:\chi_{p,\lambda}(v)<\infty\}. 
 
 
 For every  λ ∈ ℝ \lambda\in\mathbb{R} , this threshold is positive. Indeed,
put 
 

 
 
 M λ = max x ∼ y ⁡ Δ ​ ( x , y ) | λ | < ∞ . M_{\lambda}=\max_{x\sim y}\Delta(x,y)^{|\lambda|}<\infty. 
 
 
 Every vertex connected to  v v  has a simple open path from  v v .
There are at most  d ​ ( d − 1 ) r − 1 d(d-1)^{r-1}  such paths of length  r ≥ 1 r\geq 1 ,
each is open with probability  p r + 1 p^{r+1} , and the cocycle weight of its
endpoint is at most  M λ r M_{\lambda}^{r} . The union bound therefore gives 
 

 
 
 χ p , λ ​ ( v ) ≤ p + ∑ r ≥ 1 d ​ ( d − 1 ) r − 1 ​ p r + 1 ​ M λ r < ∞ \chi_{p,\lambda}(v)\leq p+\sum_{r\geq 1}d(d-1)^{r-1}p^{r+1}M_{\lambda}^{r}<\infty 
 
 
 whenever  p ​ M λ ​ ( d − 1 ) < 1 pM_{\lambda}(d-1)<1 . Hence
 p c s ​ ( λ ) > 0 p_{c}^{\rm s}(\lambda)>0 . For every
 0 ≤ λ < 1 / 2 0\leq\lambda<1/2 , this threshold is also strictly below one.
For  λ = 0 \lambda=0 , this is  p c s < 1 p_{c}^{\rm s}<1 , proved directly below.
For  λ > 0 \lambda>0 , choose  γ ∈ Γ \gamma\in\Gamma  with
 a = Δ ⁡ ( o , γ ​ o ) > 1 a=\Delta(o,\gamma o)>1 , and a path of length  L L  from  o o  to
 γ ​ o \gamma o . The union of its first  n n  translates under  γ \gamma 
contains at most  n ​ L + 1 nL+1  sites and connects  o o  to  γ n ​ o \gamma^{n}o .
Hence 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( o ↔ γ n ​ o ) ​ Δ ​ ( o , γ n ​ o ) λ ≥ p n ​ L + 1 ​ a λ ​ n . \mathbb{P}_{p}(o\leftrightarrow\gamma^{n}o)\Delta(o,\gamma^{n}o)^{\lambda}\geq p^{nL+1}a^{\lambda n}. 
 
 
 The corresponding terms of the susceptibility have a divergent sum as
soon as  p L ​ a λ > 1 p^{L}a^{\lambda}>1 , which holds for some  p < 1 p<1 . 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 5.A   (Russo–convolution) . 
 
 Fix  λ ∈ ℝ \lambda\in\mathbb{R}  and a compact
interval  I ⊂ ( 0 , 1 ) I\subset(0,1) . With 
 

 
 
 χ p , λ ​ ( v ) = ∑ u ℙ p ​ ( v ↔ u ) ​ Δ ​ ( v , u ) λ , \chi_{p,\lambda}(v)=\sum_{u}\mathbb{P}_{p}(v\leftrightarrow u)\Delta(v,u)^{\lambda}, 
 
 
 there is  C = C ⁡ ( G , λ , I ) C=C(G,\lambda,I)  such that, whenever the right side is
finite, 
 

 
 
 d d ​ p ​ χ p , λ ​ ( v ) ≤ C ​ χ p , λ ​ ( v ) 2 \frac{d}{dp}\chi_{p,\lambda}(v)\leq C\chi_{p,\lambda}(v)^{2} 
 
 (5.10) 
 
 for almost every  p ∈ I p\in I . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First work in a finite ball. If a site  z ∉ { u , v } z\notin\{u,v\}  is
pivotal for  v ↔ u v\leftrightarrow u , then in the configuration with  z z 
closed there are neighbors  x , y x,y  of  z z  such that the connections
 v ↔ x v\leftrightarrow x  and  y ↔ u y\leftrightarrow u  occur on disjoint
vertex sets. The site BK inequality and a union bound over at most
 d 2 d^{2}  ordered pairs give 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( z ​  pivotal ) ≤ ∑ x , y ∼ z ℙ p ​ ( v ↔ x ) ​ ℙ p ​ ( y ↔ u ) . \mathbb{P}_{p}(z\text{ pivotal})\leq\sum_{x,y\sim z}\mathbb{P}_{p}(v\leftrightarrow x)\mathbb{P}_{p}(y\leftrightarrow u). 
 
 (5.11) 
 
 For  z = v z=v  or  z = u z=u , the same argument has one arm and contributes
only  O G , λ ​ ( χ p , λ ) O_{G,\lambda}(\chi_{p,\lambda}) . More explicitly, pivotality
of the root coordinate  v v  is the event that the configuration with
 v v  forced open connects  v v  to  u u . Since the event
 { v ↔ u } \{v\leftrightarrow u\}  itself forces  v v  open, 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( v ​  pivotal for  ​ v ↔ u ) = p − 1 ​ ℙ p ​ ( v ↔ u ) . \mathbb{P}_{p}(v\text{ pivotal for }v\leftrightarrow u)=p^{-1}\mathbb{P}_{p}(v\leftrightarrow u). 
 
 
 The same identity holds at  u u . After multiplication by
 Δ ​ ( v , u ) λ \Delta(v,u)^{\lambda}  and summation over  u u , the two endpoint
contributions are at most  2 ​ p − 1 ​ χ p , λ ​ ( v ) 2p^{-1}\chi_{p,\lambda}(v) . Applying
Russo’s formula
 [ 20 , Section 4, Lemma 3, equation (4.2)]  (see also
 [ 12 , Section 2.4] ), the cocycle identity 
 

 
 
 Δ ⁡ ( v , u ) = Δ ⁡ ( v , x ) ​ Δ ​ ( x , z ) ​ Δ ​ ( z , y ) ​ Δ ​ ( y , u ) , \Delta(v,u)=\Delta(v,x)\Delta(x,z)\Delta(z,y)\Delta(y,u), 
 
 
 then turns the sum of ( 5.11 ) over  u , z u,z  into at most
 d 2 ​ M λ 2 ​ χ p , λ ​ ( v ) 2 d^{2}M_{\lambda}^{2}\chi_{p,\lambda}(v)^{2} , where
 M λ = max a ∼ b ⁡ Δ ​ ( a , b ) | λ | M_{\lambda}=\max_{a\sim b}\Delta(a,b)^{|\lambda|} . Since
 χ p , λ ​ ( v ) ≥ p \chi_{p,\lambda}(v)\geq p  and  p p  is bounded away from zero on
 I I , the endpoint terms are absorbed into the same bound. For the
convolution itself, the cocycle display gives 
 

 
 
 ∑ u , z ∑ x , y ∼ z ℙ p ​ ( v ↔ x ) ​ ℙ p ​ ( y ↔ u ) ​ Δ ​ ( v , u ) λ ≤ d 2 ​ M λ 2 ​ ( ∑ x ℙ p ​ ( v ↔ x ) ​ Δ ​ ( v , x ) λ ) ​ sup y ( ∑ u ℙ p ​ ( y ↔ u ) ​ Δ ​ ( y , u ) λ ) . \begin{split}&\sum_{u,z}\sum_{x,y\sim z}\mathbb{P}_{p}(v\leftrightarrow x)\mathbb{P}_{p}(y\leftrightarrow u)\Delta(v,u)^{\lambda}\\
&\quad\leq d^{2}M_{\lambda}^{2}\left(\sum_{x}\mathbb{P}_{p}(v\leftrightarrow x)\Delta(v,x)^{\lambda}\right)\sup_{y}\left(\sum_{u}\mathbb{P}_{p}(y\leftrightarrow u)\Delta(y,u)^{\lambda}\right).\end{split} 
 
 
 Transitivity makes the supremum equal to
 χ p , λ ​ ( v ) \chi_{p,\lambda}(v) , proving the claimed square bound. 
 
 
 For the finite-volume passage, let  Λ \Lambda  be finite, write
 χ Λ , p , λ ​ ( a ) \chi_{\Lambda,p,\lambda}(a)  for the corresponding restricted
susceptibility rooted at  a a , and put
 Y Λ ​ ( p ) = max a ∈ Λ ⁡ χ Λ , p , λ ​ ( a ) Y_{\Lambda}(p)=\max_{a\in\Lambda}\chi_{\Lambda,p,\lambda}(a) .
The same convolution calculation, without using transitivity of
 Λ \Lambda , bounds the derivative at every root by 
 

 
 
 d d ​ p ​ χ Λ , p , λ ​ ( a ) ≤ C ​ Y Λ ​ ( p ) 2 . \frac{d}{dp}\chi_{\Lambda,p,\lambda}(a)\leq CY_{\Lambda}(p)^{2}. 
 
 
 Indeed, after the branch site is summed, each of the two arm sums is at
most  Y Λ ​ ( p ) Y_{\Lambda}(p) , with the adjacent cocycle factors absorbed into
 C C . Since  Y Λ Y_{\Lambda}  is the maximum of finitely many differentiable
functions, its upper right Dini derivative satisfies 
 

 
 
 D + ​ Y Λ ​ ( p ) ≤ C ​ Y Λ ​ ( p ) 2 . D^{+}Y_{\Lambda}(p)\leq CY_{\Lambda}(p)^{2}. 
 
 
 Consequently the usual comparison with the solution of  y ′ = C ​ y 2 y^{\prime}=Cy^{2} 
applies uniformly in  Λ \Lambda . 
 
 
 Now exhaust  G G  by finite sets  Λ R \Lambda_{R} . Transitivity of the
infinite graph gives
 Y Λ R ​ ( s ) ≤ χ s , λ ​ ( v ) Y_{\Lambda_{R}}(s)\leq\chi_{s,\lambda}(v)  for every  s s , because
each restricted rooted susceptibility is bounded by its full-space
counterpart. Integrating the root inequality before taking
 R → ∞ R\to\infty , and using monotone convergence, gives 
 

 
 
 χ q , λ ​ ( v ) − χ p , λ ​ ( v ) ≤ C ​ ∫ p q χ s , λ ​ ( v ) 2 ​ 𝑑 s \chi_{q,\lambda}(v)-\chi_{p,\lambda}(v)\leq C\int_{p}^{q}\chi_{s,\lambda}(v)^{2}\,ds 
 
 
 whenever the right side is finite. It follows that  χ \chi  is locally
absolutely continuous there and satisfies ( 5.10 ) almost
everywhere. Since the thresholds used below are strictly below
one, the same integrated inequality shows that
susceptibility diverges as  p ↑ p c s ​ ( λ ) p\uparrow p_{c}^{\rm s}(\lambda) . Indeed,
if its value at the threshold were  M < ∞ M<\infty , every finite-volume
susceptibility would be at most  M M  there. Solving
 y ′ ≤ C ​ y 2 y^{\prime}\leq Cy^{2}  forward gives
 y ⁡ ( q + s ) ≤ M / ( 1 − C ​ M ​ s ) y(q+s)\leq M/(1-CMs)  for  0 < s < ( C ​ M ) − 1 0<s<(CM)^{-1} , uniformly in the
volume. Monotone convergence would then make the infinite-volume
susceptibility finite above the defining supremum, a contradiction. 
 
 
 If  q = p c s ​ ( λ ) q=p_{c}^{\rm s}(\lambda) , solve the same inequality forward from
 q − ε q-\varepsilon . Were
 χ q − ε , λ < 1 / ( C ​ ε ) \chi_{q-\varepsilon,\lambda}<1/(C\varepsilon) , the finite-volume
bound would stay uniform beyond  q q , contradicting the definition of
 q q . After dividing by the conditioned-root factor this gives 
 

 
 
 χ ^ q − ε , λ ​ ( v ) ≥ c G , λ ​ ε − 1 \widehat{\chi}_{q-\varepsilon,\lambda}(v)\geq c_{G,\lambda}\varepsilon^{-1} 
 
 (5.12) 
 
 for  0 < ε < q / 2 0<\varepsilon<q/2 ; decreasing the constant covers the remaining
 ε \varepsilon . This proves the site form of the tilted mean-field estimate
corresponding to  [ 15 , Proposition 3.1] .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Second, define the conditional tilted susceptibility 
 

 
 
 χ ^ p , λ ​ ( v ) = 1 p ​ ∑ x ℙ p ​ ( v ↔ x ) ​ Δ ​ ( v , x ) λ . \widehat{\chi}_{p,\lambda}(v)=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{x}\mathbb{P}_{p}(v\leftrightarrow x)\Delta(v,x)^{\lambda}. 
 
 (5.13) 
 
 The tilted mass-transport symmetry
 [ 15 , Proposition 2.2] ,
 χ ^ p , λ = χ ^ p , 1 − λ \widehat{\chi}_{p,\lambda}=\widehat{\chi}_{p,1-\lambda} , gives the
tiltability threshold
 p t s := p c s ​ ( 1 / 2 ) p_{t}^{\rm s}:=p_{c}^{\rm s}(1/2) .
For the ghost argument, let  𝒢 v , h ​ ( x ) \mathcal{G}_{v,h}(x)  be independent
Poisson variables of mean  h ​ Δ ​ ( v , x ) λ h\Delta(v,x)^{\lambda} , and set 
 

 
 
 | K | v , λ = ∑ x ∈ K Δ ​ ( v , x ) λ , M ^ p , λ , h ​ ( v ) = 𝔼 ^ p v ​ [ 1 − e − h ​ | K v | v , λ ] . |K|_{v,\lambda}=\sum_{x\in K}\Delta(v,x)^{\lambda},\qquad\widehat{M}_{p,\lambda,h}(v)=\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{p}^{v}[1-e^{-h|K_{v}|_{v,\lambda}}]. 
 
 
 We write  ℙ ^ p , λ , h v \widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{p,\lambda,h}^{v}  and
 𝔼 ^ p , λ , h v \widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{p,\lambda,h}^{v}  for the resulting joint law and
expectation of the site variables, conditioned on  v v  being open, and
the independent ghost variables. Thus  𝔼 ^ p v \widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{p}^{v}  is
reserved for expectation over the site variables alone.
By the symmetry  λ ↔ 1 − λ \lambda\leftrightarrow 1-\lambda , it is enough in the
magnetization argument to take  0 ≤ λ < 1 / 2 0\leq\lambda<1/2 . Put
 q = p c s ​ ( λ ) q=p_{c}^{\rm s}(\lambda) , temporarily suppose that 
 

 
 
 𝔼 ^ q v ​ [ | K v | v , λ ( 1 + η ) / 2 ] < ∞ \widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{q}^{v}[|K_{v}|_{v,\lambda}^{(1+\eta)/2}]<\infty 
 
 
 for some  η > 0 \eta>0 . Replacing  η \eta  by
 min ⁡ { η , 1 / 2 } \min\{\eta,1/2\}  only weakens this finite-moment assumption, so we
may and do suppose  0 < η ≤ 1 / 2 0<\eta\leq 1/2 . Fix
 0 < δ ≤ min ⁡ { η , ( 1 − 2 ​ λ ) / λ } 0<\delta\leq\min\{\eta,(1-2\lambda)/\lambda\} , with the second
bound omitted when  λ = 0 \lambda=0 . The estimates ( 5.15 )–( 5.22 ) below are
at  p = q p=q  and depend on this temporary moment hypothesis.
The tilted mass-transport identity is unchanged. The magnetization
argument uses the following vertex version of its separator step. 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 5.B   (Finite-terminal Menger compactness) . 
 
 Let  H H  be a
countable graph, let  A , B ⊆ V ⁡ ( H ) A,B\subseteq V(H)  be finite, and let
 N ∈ { 0 , 1 , … } N\in\{0,1,\ldots\} . An  A A -to- B B  path is allowed to have
length zero when its sole vertex belongs to  A ∩ B A\cap B .
If  H H  does not contain  N + 1 N+1  pairwise vertex-disjoint finite
 A A -to- B B  paths, then some set of at most  N N  vertices meets every
finite  A A -to- B B  path. The analogous rooted-fan statement also
holds: if there are not  N + 1 N+1  paths from a fixed root  v v  to distinct
members of a finite terminal set
 B ⊆ V ⁡ ( H ) ∖ { v } B\subseteq V(H)\setminus\{v\} , internally vertex-disjoint away from
 v v , then at most  N N  vertices other than  v v  meet every such
positive-length path.
Throughout this lemma, separators are allowed to contain terminal
vertices: in the first statement they may meet  A ∪ B A\cup B , and in the
rooted-fan statement they may contain members of the terminal set; only
the distinguished root  v v  is forbidden from the separator. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First suppose that  A ∩ B = ∅ A\cap B=\varnothing . Identify a candidate
separator with a point of the compact
space  { 0 , 1 } V ⁡ ( H ) \{0,1\}^{V(H)} . The condition that it have size at most  N N 
is closed, and, for each finite terminal path  P P , the condition that
it meet  P P  is clopen. Every finite family of these conditions is
satisfiable: apply the finite vertex-Menger theorem
 [ 9 , Theorem 3.3.1] , or its finite
rooted-fan form, to the finite union of the listed paths. The finite
intersection property therefore gives a separator meeting every finite
terminal path. This proves the disjoint-terminal assertion without
requiring separators from an exhaustion to stabilize. 
 
 
 For arbitrary  A , B A,B , put  I = A ∩ B I=A\cap B . The singleton paths at the
vertices of  I I  are pairwise vertex-disjoint, so the hypothesis implies
 | I | ≤ N |I|\leq N . The graph  H ∖ I H\setminus I  cannot contain
 N − | I | + 1 N-|I|+1  pairwise vertex-disjoint paths from  A ∖ I A\setminus I  to
 B ∖ I B\setminus I : together with the singleton paths indexed by  I I ,
they would give  N + 1 N+1  disjoint  A A -to- B B  paths in  H H .
The disjoint-terminal assertion in  H ∖ I H\setminus I  therefore gives a
separator  S ′ S^{\prime}  of size at most  N − | I | N-|I| . Every  A A -to- B B  path
that meets  I I  is met by  I I , while every such path avoiding  I I 
is met by  S ′ S^{\prime} . Thus  I ∪ S ′ I\cup S^{\prime}  is a separator of size at most
 N N . 
 
 
 For the rooted-fan statement, repeat the compactness argument in the
first paragraph using the finite rooted-fan form of Menger quoted there.
The distinguished root is excluded from every finite separator, and
this exclusion is a closed coordinate condition. Hence the resulting
separator contains at most  N N  vertices other than the root and meets
every positive-length rooted terminal path.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 5.C   (Two-fan separator) . 
 
 Let  K K  be a locally finite connected graph rooted at
 v v , with a finite set of at least two marked tokens. If there are not two paths from
 v v  to distinct tokens, internally vertex-disjoint away from  v v ,
then there is  z ≠ v z\neq v  such that the component of  v v  in
 K ∖ { z } K\setminus\{z\}  has no token and two such paths start from  z z 
outside that component. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Attach a separate leaf for every token and call the
resulting graph  K + K^{+} . Apply the finite-terminal version of vertex
Menger between  v v  and the token leaves. Under the stated hypothesis
there is a one-vertex cut separating  v v  from every token leaf. There
are only finitely many possible such cut vertices: each must lie on any
one fixed finite path from  v v  to a fixed token leaf. Choose one
 z ≠ v z\neq v  for which the component  R z R_{z}  of  v v  in
 K + ∖ { z } K^{+}\setminus\{z\}  is maximal under inclusion. It is token-free by
definition. In the induced graph on
 V ⁡ ( K + ) ∖ R z V(K^{+})\setminus R_{z} , suppose there were not two internally
vertex-disjoint paths from  z z  to distinct token leaves. Menger would
give a vertex  w ≠ z w\neq z  separating  z z  from every token leaf in that
induced graph. The component of  v v  in  K + ∖ { w } K^{+}\setminus\{w\}  contains
 R z ∪ { z } R_{z}\cup\{z\} , strictly contradicting maximality. Thus the required
two-fan exists. Deleting the attached final edges gives the asserted
paths in  K K , with a length-zero arm when its token is carried by
 z z .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Here every ghost token is represented by a distinct attached leaf. Two
tokens at the same graph vertex are distinct targets, but their paths
still share the carrier site’s percolation coordinate. If that carrier is
not the junction it is therefore a separator, not a disjoint occurrence.
If it is the junction, the two length-zero arms consume distinct ghost
coordinates and no unconditioned site coordinate.
Under the temporary finite-moment hypothesis, the total ghost intensity
on the root cluster is  h ​ | K v | v , λ < ∞ h|K_{v}|_{v,\lambda}<\infty , so the token set is
finite almost surely even if the cluster itself is infinite. Thus the
stated finite-terminal form is exactly the one used below. 
 
 
 We record the calculation replacing
 [ 15 , Lemma 4.4] . In the
rest of this paragraph probabilities are under the conditional law
 ℙ ^ p v \widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{p}^{v} . Let
 𝒟 z \mathscr{D}_{z}  be the event that there are two distinct ghost tokens and
open paths from  z z  to their carriers whose sets of graph vertices are
disjoint outside  z z ; a length-zero path is allowed when its carrier is
 z z . Thus coincident carriers are permitted only when both equal  z z .
Here is a product-space justification for applying BK to the
Poisson ghost field. Fix a finite connected set  W ∋ z W\ni z , require the
two paths to lie in  W W , and, for each  x ∈ W x\in W , replace the
Poisson variable of mean
 μ x = h ​ Δ ​ ( z , x ) λ \mu_{x}=h\Delta(z,x)^{\lambda}  by  N N  independent Bernoulli token
slots, each open with probability  1 − e − μ x / N 1-e^{-\mu_{x}/N} . Conditional on
 z z  being open, the remaining site coordinates and all token slots
form a finite product space. There are three terminal cases. For distinct
carriers, the event  𝒟 z \mathscr{D}_{z}  has witnesses using disjoint site
coordinates away from  z z  and distinct token-slot coordinates. If two
tokens have the same carrier  x ≠ z x\neq z , both arms use the site coordinate
of  x x , so this configuration is excluded from  𝒟 z \mathscr{D}_{z}  and is
handled by the separator case below. If both tokens are carried by  z z ,
the site coordinate of  z z  is already conditioned open and the two
attached ghost leaves use distinct token-slot coordinates. Ordinary BK
therefore bounds the two-arm probability by the square of the corresponding
one-arm probability. For fixed  W W , the vector of binomial token counts
converges in law as  N → ∞ N\to\infty  to the independent Poisson counts.
Letting finite connected  W W  increase to  V V , monotone convergence
applies because every one-arm or two-arm witness consists of finite
paths and finitely many tokens. Consequently, 
 

 
 
 ℙ ^ p , λ , h z ​ ( 𝒟 z ) ≤ M ^ p , λ , h ​ ( z ) 2 . \widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{p,\lambda,h}^{z}(\mathscr{D}_{z})\leq\widehat{M}_{p,\lambda,h}(z)^{2}. 
 
 (5.14) 
 
 Coincident terminals away from  z z  lie in the complementary separator
case. Coincident tokens carried by  z z  are covered by ( 5.14 ), since the
site  z z  is conditioned open and their attached leaves are distinct
ghost coordinates. 
 
 
 Now suppose that the cluster of  v v  contains at least two tokens. If
 𝒟 v \mathscr{D}_{v}  fails, Auxiliary Lemma  5.C  gives
an open cut site
 z ≠ v z\neq v . Close  z z , and let  K v ′ K^{\prime}_{v}  be the root-side cluster. It
contains no token, while  𝒟 z \mathscr{D}_{z}  occurs off  K v ′ K^{\prime}_{v} . Assign
 z z  to any neighbor  u ∈ K v ′ u\in K^{\prime}_{v} , and sum over the at most  d d 
choices. Fix deterministic rules for choosing the separator and its
root-side neighbor, and let  𝒜 ⁡ ( v , u , z ) \mathscr{A}(v,u,z)  be the part of this
separator event on which those rules return  ( u , z ) (u,z) .
For fixed  u , z u,z , we bound this event after discarding the selector
condition and retaining only  u ∈ K v ′ u\in K^{\prime}_{v} , absence of ghost tokens in
 K v ′ K^{\prime}_{v} , and the off- K v ′ K^{\prime}_{v}  occurrence of  𝒟 z \mathscr{D}_{z} . Thus the
choice of  z z , which may use the ghost configuration, plays no role in
the conditional law below. 
 
 
 We justify the conditioning used here even when  K v ′ K^{\prime}_{v}  is infinite.
Let 
 

 
 
 ℋ v , z := σ ( U x : x ≠ z ) ∨ σ ( 𝒢 v , h ( x ) : x ∈ V ) , \mathscr{H}_{v,z}:=\sigma(U_{x}:x\neq z)\vee\sigma(\mathcal{G}_{v,h}(x):x\in V), 
 
 
 and let  ℚ v , z \mathbb{Q}_{v,z}  be the marginal law of
 ℙ ^ p , λ , h v \widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{p,\lambda,h}^{v}  on  ℋ v , z \mathscr{H}_{v,z} . Thus
 ℚ v , z \mathbb{Q}_{v,z}  governs every site and ghost coordinate except
 U z U_{z} . For a residual coordinate assignment  ω \omega , write
 ω z , o \omega^{z,\mathrm{o}}  and  ω z , c \omega^{z,\mathrm{c}}  for its extensions
with  z z  declared open and closed, respectively, and define  K v ′ K^{\prime}_{v}  to
be the cluster of  v v  in  ω z , c \omega^{z,\mathrm{c}} . Explore this cluster
breadth first. At stage  n n , let
 A n A_{n}  and  D n D_{n}  be respectively the finite sets found open and
closed, and let  ℱ n \mathcal{F}_{n}  be the sigma-field generated by all site
labels tested up to that stage. Use a fixed enumeration to break ties, so
every boundary site is eventually tested. Then 
 

 
 
 A n ↑ K v ′ , D n ↑ ∂ V K v ′ ∖ { z } , ℱ ∞ = σ ⁡ ( ⋃ n ℱ n ) . A_{n}\uparrow K^{\prime}_{v},\qquad D_{n}\uparrow\partial_{V}K^{\prime}_{v}\setminus\{z\},\qquad\mathcal{F}_{\infty}=\sigma\!\left(\bigcup_{n}\mathcal{F}_{n}\right). 
 
 
 Conditionally on  ℱ n \mathcal{F}_{n} , the untested site labels are independent
Bernoulli variables and all ghost variables retain their original
independent Poisson laws. For every event depending on finitely many of
these residual coordinates this product-kernel identity passes to
 ℱ ∞ \mathcal{F}_{\infty}  by martingale convergence. A monotone-class argument,
followed by increasing finite path exhaustions, extends it to the off-cluster
two-fan event. Thus this is a statement about the terminal exploration
sigma-field, not conditioning on an atom  { K v ′ = K } \{K^{\prime}_{v}=K\} . Denote this
terminal residual product kernel, with  z z  subsequently declared open,
by  𝖪 v , z ( ⋅ ∣ ℱ ∞ ) \mathsf{K}_{v,z}(\,\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{\infty}) . 
 
 
 Set  S = | K v ′ | v , λ S=|K^{\prime}_{v}|_{v,\lambda} . Conditionally on  ℱ ∞ \mathcal{F}_{\infty} ,
the probability that  K v ′ K^{\prime}_{v}  contains no ghost token is  e − h ​ S e^{-hS} ,
interpreted as zero when  S = ∞ S=\infty . This event uses only ghost
coordinates carried by  K v ′ K^{\prime}_{v} . The event that  𝒟 z \mathscr{D}_{z}  occurs
off  K v ′ K^{\prime}_{v}  uses only the residual coordinates. Its conditional
probability is at most the
unrestricted two-fan probability: the tested closed boundary and the
prohibition on entering  K v ′ K^{\prime}_{v}  can only suppress this increasing event.
The cocycle identity gives 
 

 
 
 Δ ​ ( v , x ) λ = Δ ​ ( v , z ) λ ​ Δ ​ ( z , x ) λ . \Delta(v,x)^{\lambda}=\Delta(v,z)^{\lambda}\Delta(z,x)^{\lambda}. 
 
 
 Thus changing the root of the ghost weights from  v v  to  z z  changes
the ghost intensity by the factor  Δ ​ ( v , z ) λ \Delta(v,z)^{\lambda} . Applying
( 5.14 ) now gives the terminal conditional estimate 
 

 
 
 𝖪 v , z ( K v ′ ∩ 𝒢 v , h = ∅ , 𝒟 z  occurs off  K v ′ ∣ ℱ ∞ ) ≤ e − h ​ | K v ′ | v , λ M ^ p , λ , Δ ​ ( v , z ) λ ​ h ( z ) 2 . \mathsf{K}_{v,z}\!\left(K^{\prime}_{v}\cap\mathcal{G}_{v,h}=\varnothing,\ \mathscr{D}_{z}\text{ occurs off }K^{\prime}_{v}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\infty}\right)\leq e^{-h|K^{\prime}_{v}|_{v,\lambda}}\widehat{M}_{p,\lambda,\Delta(v,z)^{\lambda}h}(z)^{2}. 
 
 
 Put 
 

 
 
 F u , z := { u ∈ K v ′ , K v ′ ∩ 𝒢 v , h = ∅ } , F_{u,z}:=\{u\in K^{\prime}_{v},\ K^{\prime}_{v}\cap\mathcal{G}_{v,h}=\varnothing\}, 
 
 
 an event of  ℋ v , z \mathscr{H}_{v,z} , and let 
 

 
 
 R v , u , z := { ω : ω z , o ∈ 𝒜 ⁡ ( v , u , z ) } . R_{v,u,z}:=\{\omega:\omega^{z,\mathrm{o}}\in\mathscr{A}(v,u,z)\}. 
 
 
 Since  z ≠ v z\neq v , conditioning  v v  open does not change the Bernoulli
law of  U z U_{z} . Therefore, for every  B ∈ ℋ v , z B\in\mathscr{H}_{v,z} , 
 

 
 
 ℙ ^ p , λ , h v ​ ( B , U z ≤ p ) = p ​ ℚ v , z ​ ( B ) , ℙ ^ p , λ , h v ​ ( B , U z > p ) = ( 1 − p ) ​ ℚ v , z ​ ( B ) . \widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{p,\lambda,h}^{v}(B,U_{z}\leq p)=p\mathbb{Q}_{v,z}(B),\qquad\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{p,\lambda,h}^{v}(B,U_{z}>p)=(1-p)\mathbb{Q}_{v,z}(B). 
 
 
 In particular, 
 

 
 
 ℙ ^ p , λ , h v ​ ( 𝒜 ⁡ ( v , u , z ) ) = p ​ ℚ v , z ​ ( R v , u , z ) . \widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{p,\lambda,h}^{v}(\mathscr{A}(v,u,z))=p\mathbb{Q}_{v,z}(R_{v,u,z}). 
 
 
 On  R v , u , z R_{v,u,z} , closing  z z  produces the root-side cluster used in
( 5 ). Discarding the deterministic selector condition,
integrating that conditional estimate, and inserting
 𝟏 { u ∈ K ′ v } \mathbf{1}_{\{u\in K^{\prime}_{v}\}}  give

 Combining the last two displays gives 
 

 
 
 ℙ ^ p , λ , h v ​ ( 𝒜 ⁡ ( v , u , z ) ) ≤ C d ​ p ​ ℚ v , z ​ ( F u , z ) ​ M ^ p , λ , Δ ​ ( v , z ) λ ​ h ​ ( z ) 2 . \widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{p,\lambda,h}^{v}(\mathscr{A}(v,u,z))\leq C_{d}p\,\mathbb{Q}_{v,z}(F_{u,z})\widehat{M}_{p,\lambda,\Delta(v,z)^{\lambda}h}(z)^{2}. 
 
 (5.15) 
 
 Thus  p p , rather than  p / ( 1 − p ) p/(1-p) , is the switching coefficient while
the probability on the right is evaluated under  ℚ v , z \mathbb{Q}_{v,z} .
This finite-stage derivation also shows that ( 5.15 ) does not
require  K v ′ K^{\prime}_{v}  to be finite. We now state and prove
the power bound used at this point. This removes any appeal to the bond
magnetization calculation. 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 5.D   (Magnetization power bound) .

 (5.26) 
 
 Removing the conditioning on the root only multiplies the nonzero
