 Proposition 5.5   (Tilted-threshold separation) . 
 
 Assume the site two-fan inverse-magnetization estimate
( 5.23 )–( 5.26 ), the critical fractional estimate
( 5.34 ), the first-moment identities
( 5.59 )–( 5.60 ), and the lower bound
( 5.73 ). Then 
 

 
 
 p c s < p c s ​ ( λ ) ≤ p t s ( 0 < λ < 1 ) . p_{c}^{\rm s}<p_{c}^{\rm s}(\lambda)\leq p_{t}^{\rm s}\qquad(0<\lambda<1). 
 
 
 It also implies  p c s ∈ I h s p_{c}^{\rm s}\in I_{h}^{\rm s}  and, together with the
first-moment estimates already proved,
 α p c s s = β p c s s = 1 \alpha_{p_{c}^{\rm s}}^{\rm s}=\beta_{p_{c}^{\rm s}}^{\rm s}=1 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The site two-fan
differential inequality ( 5.22 ), through the inverse-magnetization
calculation ( 5.23 )–( 5.26 ), gives for
 0 ≤ λ < 1 / 2 0\leq\lambda<1/2 , every  η > 0 \eta>0 , and every root  v v , 
 

 
 
 𝔼 p c s ​ ( λ ) ​ [ | K v | v , λ ( 1 + η ) / 2 ] = ∞ . \mathbb{E}_{p_{c}^{\rm s}(\lambda)}[|K_{v}|_{v,\lambda}^{(1+\eta)/2}]=\infty. 
 
 (5.126) 
 
 Suppose that  p c s ​ ( λ ) = p c s p_{c}^{\rm s}(\lambda)=p_{c}^{\rm s}  for some
 0 < λ < 1 / 2 0<\lambda<1/2 . If  z ∈ L k x ​ ( v ) z\in L_{k}^{x}(v) , then 
 

 
 
 e t 0 ​ λ ​ ( k + x − 1 ) < Δ ​ ( v , z ) λ ≤ e t 0 ​ λ ​ ( k + x ) . e^{t_{0}\lambda(k+x-1)}<\Delta(v,z)^{\lambda}\leq e^{t_{0}\lambda(k+x)}. 
 
 
 Since  x ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) x\in[0,1) , summing over the cluster gives, with constants
independent of the offset, 
 

 
 
 | K v | v , λ ≍ ∑ k ∈ ℤ G , λ ⁡ e t 0 ​ λ ​ k ​ X k − ∞ , ∞ ​ ( v ) . |K_{v}|_{v,\lambda}\asymp_{G,\lambda}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{t_{0}\lambda k}X_{k}^{-\infty,\infty}(v). 
 
 
 For  a = 3 / 4 a=3/4 , subadditivity gives 
 

 
 
 ( ∑ k e t 0 ​ λ ​ k ​ X k ) a ≤ ∑ k e a ​ t 0 ​ λ ​ k ​ X k a . \left(\sum_{k}e^{t_{0}\lambda k}X_{k}\right)^{a}\leq\sum_{k}e^{at_{0}\lambda k}X_{k}^{a}. 
 
 
 Apply clause 2 with  1 − ε = a 1-\varepsilon=a . For  k ≥ 0 k\geq 0 , its summand is
at most
 C ​ exp ⁡ [ − ( t 0 − ζ 0 − 3 ​ t 0 ​ λ / 4 ) ​ k ] C\exp[-(t_{0}-\zeta_{0}-3t_{0}\lambda/4)k] ; for  k = − r < 0 k=-r<0 , it is at most
 C ​ exp ⁡ [ − ( 3 ​ t 0 ​ λ / 4 − ζ 0 ) ​ r ] C\exp[-(3t_{0}\lambda/4-\zeta_{0})r] . Therefore 
 

 
 
 𝔼 ^ p c s v ​ [ | K v | v , λ 3 / 4 ] ≤ C ​ ∑ k ≥ 0 exp ⁡ [ − ( t 0 − ζ 0 − 3 ​ t 0 ​ λ / 4 ) ​ k ] + C ∑ r > 0 exp [ − ( 3 t 0 λ / 4 − ζ 0 ) r ] < ∞ , \begin{split}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{p_{c}^{\rm s}}^{v}[|K_{v}|_{v,\lambda}^{3/4}]&\leq C\sum_{k\geq 0}\exp[-(t_{0}-\zeta_{0}-3t_{0}\lambda/4)k]\\
&\quad+C\sum_{r>0}\exp[-(3t_{0}\lambda/4-\zeta_{0})r]<\infty,\end{split} 
 
 (5.127) 
 
 where clause 2 is used with 
 

 
 
 0 < ζ 0 < min ⁡ { t 0 ​ ( 1 − 3 ​ λ / 4 ) , 3 ​ t 0 ​ λ / 4 } . 0<\zeta_{0}<\min\{t_{0}(1-3\lambda/4),3t_{0}\lambda/4\}. 
 
 
 Both geometric series converge. Since conditioning the root open changes
this moment only by the factor  p c s p_{c}^{\rm s} , this contradicts
( 5.126 ) with  η = 1 / 2 \eta=1/2 . Hence
 p c s < p c s ​ ( λ ) p_{c}^{\rm s}<p_{c}^{\rm s}(\lambda)  for  0 < λ < 1 / 2 0<\lambda<1/2 .
The conditional tilted MTP gives
 χ ^ p , λ = χ ^ p , 1 − λ \widehat{\chi}_{p,\lambda}=\widehat{\chi}_{p,1-\lambda}  for every
 p , λ p,\lambda , and therefore
 p c s ​ ( λ ) = p c s ​ ( 1 − λ ) p_{c}^{\rm s}(\lambda)=p_{c}^{\rm s}(1-\lambda) . This extends the strict
lower bound to  1 / 2 < λ < 1 1/2<\lambda<1 . For the upper bound, if
 χ ^ p , λ < ∞ \widehat{\chi}_{p,\lambda}<\infty , then the symmetry also gives
 χ ^ p , 1 − λ < ∞ \widehat{\chi}_{p,1-\lambda}<\infty , and Cauchy–Schwarz gives 
 

 
 
 χ ^ p , 1 / 2 ≤ χ ^ p , λ 1 / 2 ​ χ ^ p , 1 − λ 1 / 2 < ∞ . \widehat{\chi}_{p,1/2}\leq\widehat{\chi}_{p,\lambda}^{1/2}\widehat{\chi}_{p,1-\lambda}^{1/2}<\infty. 
 
 
 Thus  p c s ​ ( λ ) ≤ p c s ​ ( 1 / 2 ) = p t s p_{c}^{\rm s}(\lambda)\leq p_{c}^{\rm s}(1/2)=p_{t}^{\rm s} .
Finally, choose any  λ ′ < 1 / 2 \lambda^{\prime}<1/2 . The strict inequality already
proved at  λ ′ \lambda^{\prime} , followed by this upper bound, gives
 p c s < p c s ​ ( λ ′ ) ≤ p t s p_{c}^{\rm s}<p_{c}^{\rm s}(\lambda^{\prime})\leq p_{t}^{\rm s} , which also proves
the strict lower bound at  λ = 1 / 2 \lambda=1/2 . This establishes ( 5.35 ) for
every  0 < λ < 1 0<\lambda<1 . 
 
 
 Finally, applying ( 5.35 ) at any fixed
 λ 0 ∈ ( 0 , 1 / 2 ) \lambda_{0}\in(0,1/2)  gives
 p c s < p c s ​ ( λ 0 ) ≤ p t s p_{c}^{\rm s}<p_{c}^{\rm s}(\lambda_{0})\leq p_{t}^{\rm s} , so
the  λ 0 \lambda_{0} -tilted susceptibility is finite at  p c s p_{c}^{\rm s} .
For fixed  j ≤ n j\leq n , every  x ∈ L j ​ ( v ) x\in L_{j}(v)  has
 Δ ​ ( v , x ) λ 0 ≍ e t 0 ​ λ 0 ​ j \Delta(v,x)^{\lambda_{0}}\asymp e^{t_{0}\lambda_{0}j} , uniformly in the
fixed offset. Hence 
 

 
 
 E ^ p c s − ∞ , n ​ ( j , 1 ) ≤ C ​ e − t 0 ​ λ 0 ​ j ​ χ ^ p c s , λ 0 < ∞ , \widehat{E}_{p_{c}^{\rm s}}^{-\infty,n}(j;1)\leq Ce^{-t_{0}\lambda_{0}j}\widehat{\chi}_{p_{c}^{\rm s},\lambda_{0}}<\infty, 
 
 
 and therefore  p c s ∈ I h s p_{c}^{\rm s}\in I_{h}^{\rm s} . The characterization
( 5.60 ) applies because we now know both
 p c s ∈ I h s p_{c}^{\rm s}\in I_{h}^{\rm s}  and  p c s < p t s p_{c}^{\rm s}<p_{t}^{\rm s} . Since
 p c s < p c s p_{c}^{\rm s}<p_{c}^{\rm s}  is false, its equivalence gives
 β p c s s ≤ 1 \beta_{p_{c}^{\rm s}}^{\rm s}\leq 1 . The lower bound
 α p c s s ≥ 1 \alpha_{p_{c}^{\rm s}}^{\rm s}\geq 1  was already proved, without using
the fractional bootstrap, in ( 5.73 ). Since the preceding fixed-tilt
argument gives  p c s < p t s p_{c}^{\rm s}<p_{t}^{\rm s} , ( 5.59 ) gives
 α p c s s = β p c s s ≤ 1 \alpha_{p_{c}^{\rm s}}^{\rm s}=\beta_{p_{c}^{\rm s}}^{\rm s}\leq 1 .
Together with ( 5.73 ), this proves the asserted equality.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Part 1 and the preliminary inequalities of the lemma were proved before
Proposition  5.3 . Propositions
 5.4  and
 5.5  prove Parts 2–4. This completes
the proof of Lemma  5.2 .  □ \square 
 
 
 Clause 4 of Lemma  5.2  proves
Theorem  5.1 .  □ \square 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 5.H   (Bernoulli comparison) . 
 
 Let  I I  be finite, let  E ⊆ { 0 , 1 } I E\subseteq\{0,1\}^{I}  be increasing, and let
 0 < p < q < 1 0<p<q<1 . Then 
 

 
 
 ℙ q ​ ( E ) ≥ ℙ p ​ ( E ) log ⁡ q / log ⁡ p . \mathbb{P}_{q}(E)\geq\mathbb{P}_{p}(E)^{\log q/\log p}. 
 
 (5.128) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Write  a ​ ( t ) = ℙ t ​ ( E ) a(t)=\mathbb{P}_{t}(E) . The assertion is immediate if
 a ⁡ ( p ) ∈ { 0 , 1 } a(p)\in\{0,1\} , so suppose  0 < a ⁡ ( p ) < 1 0<a(p)<1 . Finite-product entropy
tensorization, which follows by iterating the log-sum inequality, applied
to  f = 𝟏 E f=\mathbf{1}_{E}  gives 
 

 
 
 a ⁡ ( t ) ​ log ⁡ 1 a ⁡ ( t ) ≤ ∑ i ∈ I 𝔼 t ​ [ Ent i ⁡ ( f ) ] . a(t)\log\frac{1}{a(t)}\leq\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{t}[\operatorname{Ent}_{i}(f)]. 
 
 
 After all coordinates other than  i i  are fixed, monotonicity makes
 f f  either constant or the function  f ⁡ ( ω ) = ω i f(\omega)=\omega_{i} . The
conditional entropy in the second case is  t ​ log ⁡ ( 1 / t ) t\log(1/t) . Consequently
Russo’s finite-product formula gives 
 

 
 
 a ⁡ ( t ) ​ log ​ 1 a ⁡ ( t ) ≤ t ​ log ​ 1 t ​ ∑ i ∈ I ℙ t ​ ( i ​  is pivotal ) = t ​ log ​ 1 t ​ a ′ ​ ( t ) . a(t)\log\frac{1}{a(t)}\leq t\log\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{P}_{t}(i\text{ is pivotal})=t\log\frac{1}{t}\,a^{\prime}(t). 
 
 
 It follows that 
 

 
 
 d d ​ t ​ ( log ⁡ a ⁡ ( t ) log ⁡ t ) = ( a ′ ​ ( t ) / a ⁡ ( t ) ) ​ log ⁡ t − ( log ⁡ a ⁡ ( t ) ) / t ( log ⁡ t ) 2 ≤ 0 . \frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\log a(t)}{\log t}\right)=\frac{(a^{\prime}(t)/a(t))\log t-(\log a(t))/t}{(\log t)^{2}}\leq 0. 
 
 
 Integrating from  p p  to  q q , and remembering that  log ⁡ q < 0 \log q<0 ,
gives ( 5.128 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 The remaining analytic input for locality is strict decrease: if
 0 < p < q < 1 0<p<q<1  and  α p s > 0 \alpha_{p}^{\rm s}>0 , then
 α q s < α p s \alpha_{q}^{\rm s}<\alpha_{p}^{\rm s} . Apply Auxiliary
Lemma  5.H  to an increasing cylinder event
 E E , so that 
 

 
 
 ℙ q ​ ( E ) ≥ ℙ p ​ ( E ) log ⁡ q / log ⁡ p . \mathbb{P}_{q}(E)\geq\mathbb{P}_{p}(E)^{\log q/\log p}. 
 
 (5.129) 
 
 Condition the starting vertex open and apply ( 5.129 ) to the remaining
site coordinates of a bounded-length height-crossing event. Increasing
the length cutoff gives, by continuity from below, 
 

 
 
 A ^ q s ​ ( t ) ≥ A ^ p s ​ ( t ) log ⁡ q / log ⁡ p . \widehat{A}_{q}^{\rm s}(t)\geq\widehat{A}_{p}^{\rm s}(t)^{\log q/\log p}. 
 
 
 After taking negative logarithms, dividing by  t t , and passing to the
height exponent, 
 

 
 
 α q s ≤ log ⁡ q log ⁡ p ​ α p s < α p s . \alpha_{q}^{\rm s}\leq\frac{\log q}{\log p}\alpha_{p}^{\rm s}<\alpha_{p}^{\rm s}. 
 
 
 Thus this step is intrinsically a vertex-product argument and requires no
bond pivotal decomposition. 
 
 
 We now apply Theorem  5.1  in the locally
convergent setting. Suppose
that the full automorphism action of  G G  is nonunimodular, write 
 

 
 
 Γ = Aut ⁡ ( G ) , Γ n = Aut ⁡ ( G n ) , \Gamma=\operatorname{Aut}(G),\qquad\Gamma_{n}=\operatorname{Aut}(G_{n}), 
 
 (5.130) 
 
 and discard finitely many  n n  so that every canonical action
 Γ n ↷ G n \Gamma_{n}\curvearrowright G_{n}  is nonunimodular. This is permitted by
the clopen property cited below. In the rest of this section,
 α p s ​ ( H ) \alpha_{p}^{\rm s}(H)  denotes the exponent belonging specifically to
the pair  ( H , Aut ⁡ ( H ) ) (H,\operatorname{Aut}(H)) , not to any other transitive
subgroup that  H H  may admit. 
 
 
 Root each transitive graph arbitrarily and regard its Dirac rooted law as
a stationary random graph. The canonical modular label on an oriented
edge is the Radon–Nikodym derivative of the law of the root-neighbor pair
with its orientation reversed with respect to its forward law. It is the
modular cocycle of the full automorphism action in ( 5.130 ). Stationary
rooted local convergence, together with the
uniform degree bound, implies local convergence of these oriented-edge
labels. For a degree- d d  transitive graph every such label belongs to
the finite set 
 

 
 
 { a / b : 1 ≤ a , b ≤ d } . \{a/b:1\leq a,b\leq d\}. 
 
 
 Consequently convergence of the labels upgrades to the following exact
statement: for every radius  r r , all sufficiently large rooted-ball
isomorphisms may be chosen to preserve  Δ ⁡ ( x , y ) \Delta(x,y)  for every
 x , y x,y  in the ball. This is
 [ 14 , Proposition 5.3 and Corollary 5.4] . The
explicit statement that the nonunimodular locus is open and closed is
 [ 14 , Corollary 5.5] . 
 
 
 For fixed  p , t , r p,t,r , the bounded-length height connection probability
 A p s ​ ( t , r ) A_{p}^{\rm s}(t,r)  is local. Moreover
 A p s ​ ( t , r ) ↑ A p s ​ ( t ) A_{p}^{\rm s}(t,r)\uparrow A_{p}^{\rm s}(t)  as  r → ∞ r\to\infty . Local
convergence preserves the incident modular labels, so it also preserves
 t 0 t_{0}  eventually. Fix an integer  N ≥ 1 N\geq 1  and put
 t = ( N − 1 ) ​ t 0 t=(N-1)t_{0} . The buffered Fekete representation ( 5.3 ) gives 
 

 
 
 α p s ​ ( G n ) ≤ − log ⁡ ( A p s ​ ( t , r , G n ) / p ) − log ⁡ p N ​ t 0 . \alpha_{p}^{\rm s}(G_{n})\leq\frac{-\log(A_{p}^{\rm s}(t,r;G_{n})/p)-\log p}{Nt_{0}}. 
 
 
 Take the graph index to infinity, then  r → ∞ r\to\infty , and finally the
infimum over  N N . By ( 5.3 ), this proves 
 

 
 
 lim sup n α p s ​ ( G n ) ≤ α p s ​ ( G ) . \limsup_{n}\alpha_{p}^{\rm s}(G_{n})\leq\alpha_{p}^{\rm s}(G). 
 
 (5.131) 
 
 If  p > p c s ​ ( G ) p>p_{c}^{\rm s}(G) , strict decrease of the positive height
exponent and ( 5.5 ) give  α p s ​ ( G ) < 1 \alpha_{p}^{\rm s}(G)<1 . Equations
( 5.5 ) and ( 5.131 ) imply  p > p c s ​ ( G n ) p>p_{c}^{\rm s}(G_{n})  eventually, and hence
 p ≥ lim sup n p c s ​ ( G n ) p\geq\limsup_{n}p_{c}^{\rm s}(G_{n}) .
Thus site locality holds when the canonical full automorphism action of
the limit is nonunimodular. This argument makes no assertion for a
canonically unimodular graph equipped with a different nonunimodular
transitive subgroup. 
 
 
 
 6  Site snowballing 

 
 Use the monotone coupling  ω p = { v : U v ≤ p } \omega_{p}=\{v:U_{v}\leq p\} , with independent
uniform  U v U_{v} .
For  0 < p ≤ q < 1 0<p\leq q<1 , write 
 

 
 
 δ ⁡ ( p , q ) = log ⁡ log ⁡ ( 1 − q ) log ⁡ ( 1 − p ) , q = Spr ⁡ ( p ; δ ⁡ ( p , q ) ) , Spr ⁡ ( p ; t ) = 1 − ( 1 − p ) e t . \delta(p,q)=\log\frac{\log(1-q)}{\log(1-p)},\qquad q=\operatorname{Spr}(p;\delta(p,q)),\quad\operatorname{Spr}(p;t)=1-(1-p)^{e^{t}}. 
 
 
 
 
 6.1  Finite-volume two-arm bound 

 
 Let  Piv s ⁡ [ m , n ] \operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[m,n]  be the event that two distinct
site clusters in  B n B_{n}  both meet  S m S_{m}  and  S n S_{n} . 
 
 
 Lemma 6.1   (Site AKN) . 
 
 Compare  [ 1 , Sections 2–3] . For
 0 < ε < 1 / 2 0<\varepsilon<1/2 ,  0 < η < 1 0<\eta<1 , and  d < ∞ d<\infty , there is
 C = C ⁡ ( ε , η , d ) C=C(\varepsilon,\eta,d)  such that 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( Piv s ⁡ [ 1 , n ] ) ≤ C ​ [ log ⁡ | B n | n ] 1 / 2 − ε \mathbb{P}_{p}(\operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[1,n])\leq C\left[\frac{\log|B_{n}|}{n}\right]^{1/2-\varepsilon} 
 
 (6.1) 
 
 for every transitive degree- d d  graph,  p ∈ [ η , 1 ] p\in[\eta,1] , and
 n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 . 
 
 
 
 Proof.  It is enough to prove the estimate with  4 ​ n 4n  in place of
 n n . Indeed, for a target radius  N ≥ 4 N\geq 4 , take
 n = ⌊ N / 4 ⌋ n=\lfloor N/4\rfloor . Then
 Piv s ⁡ [ 1 , N ] ⊆ Piv s ⁡ [ 1 , 4 ​ n ] \operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[1,N]\subseteq\operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[1,4n] : truncate each witnessing connection
at its first visit to  S 4 ​ n S_{4n} . Moreover,
 | B 2 ​ n | ≤ | B N | |B_{2n}|\leq|B_{N}|  and  n ≥ N / 8 n\geq N/8 ; the finitely many cases
 N < 4 N<4  are absorbed by increasing  C C . Choose  m ∈ [ n , 2 ​ n ) m\in[n,2n)  such
that, for  W = B m W=B_{m}  and its internal
vertex boundary  ∂ in W \partial_{\rm in}W , 
 

 
 
 q := | ∂ in W | | W | ≤ C d ​ log ⁡ | B 2 ​ n | n . q:=\frac{|\partial_{\rm in}W|}{|W|}\leq C_{d}\frac{\log|B_{2n}|}{n}. 
 
 (6.2) 
 
 This is the usual ball-boundary averaging lemma: multiply the ratios
 | B j + 1 | / | B j | |B_{j+1}|/|B_{j}|  for  n ≤ j < 2 ​ n n\leq j<2n , and use
 | ∂ in B j | ≤ | B j ∖ B j − 1 | |\partial_{\rm in}B_{j}|\leq|B_{j}\setminus B_{j-1}| , after shifting
the index by one. Transitivity also gives
 | B 2 ​ n | ≤ | B n | 2 |B_{2n}|\leq|B_{n}|^{2} . 
 
 
 Let  ℭ \mathfrak{C}  be the collection of open clusters of the induced
percolation on  W W  that meet  ∂ in W \partial_{\rm in}W , and put
 U = ⋃ C ∈ ℭ C U=\bigcup_{C\in\mathfrak{C}}C . For  z ∈ W ∖ U z\in W\setminus U , let
 k ⁡ ( z ) k(z)  be the number of clusters in  ℭ \mathfrak{C}  adjacent to
 z z , and define 
 

 
 
 J = ∑ z ∈ W ∖ U ( k ⁡ ( z ) − 1 ) + . J=\sum_{z\in W\setminus U}(k(z)-1)_{+}. 
 
 
 Every such adjacent site is closed. For each  x ∈ B m − 1 x\in B_{m-1} , the
event  Piv x s ⁡ [ 1 , 4 ​ n ] \operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}_{x}[1,4n]  forces  x x  to be closed:
if  x x  were open, all its open neighbors would lie in one cluster.
The two clusters in that event both leave  W W , since  m < 2 ​ n m<2n , and
therefore give two distinct members of  ℭ \mathfrak{C}  adjacent to
 x x . Consequently, by transitivity, 
 

 
 
 | B m − 1 | ​ ℙ p ​ ( Piv s ⁡ [ 1 , 4 ​ n ] ) ≤ 𝔼 p ​ J . |B_{m-1}|\,\mathbb{P}_{p}(\operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[1,4n])\leq\mathbb{E}_{p}J. 
 
 (6.3) 
 
 
 
 Write  ∂ V W C \partial^{W}_{V}C  for the external vertex boundary of  C C 
inside  W W , and set 
 

 
 
 H p ​ ( C ) = p ​ | ∂ V W C | − ( 1 − p ) ​ | C | . H_{p}(C)=p|\partial^{W}_{V}C|-(1-p)|C|. 
 
 
 Boundary multiplicities give the deterministic identities 
 

 
 
 ∑ C ∈ ℭ | C | = | U | , ∑ C ∈ ℭ | ∂ V W C | = | ∂ V W U | + J . \sum_{C\in\mathfrak{C}}|C|=|U|,\qquad\sum_{C\in\mathfrak{C}}|\partial^{W}_{V}C|=|\partial^{W}_{V}U|+J. 
 
 (6.4) 
 
 There is also the switching estimate 
 

 
 
 | p ​ 𝔼 ​ | ∂ V W U ​ | − ( 1 − p ) ​ 𝔼 | ​ U | | ≤ | ∂ in W | . \left|p\,\mathbb{E}|\partial^{W}_{V}U|-(1-p)\mathbb{E}|U|\right|\leq|\partial_{\rm in}W|. 
 
 (6.5) 
 
 Indeed, for each  z ∉ ∂ in W z\notin\partial_{\rm in}W , delete the coordinate
of  z z . The event that a neighbor of  z z  is connected to
 ∂ in W \partial_{\rm in}W  in  W ∖ { z } W\setminus\{z\}  is independent of that
coordinate. When  z z  is closed it says  z ∈ ∂ V W U z\in\partial^{W}_{V}U , and
when  z z  is open it says  z ∈ U z\in U . Multiplication by  p ⁡ ( 1 − p ) p(1-p) 
cancels the two contributions. The uncancelled boundary sites give
( 6.5 ). It follows from ( 6.4 )–( 6.5 ) that 
 

 
 
 p ​ 𝔼 ​ J ≤ 𝔼 ​ ∑ C ∈ ℭ | H p ​ ( C ) | + | ∂ in W | . p\,\mathbb{E}J\leq\mathbb{E}\sum_{C\in\mathfrak{C}}|H_{p}(C)|+|\partial_{\rm in}W|. 
 
 (6.6) 
 
 
 
 For  x ∈ W x\in W , let  C x C_{x}  be its open cluster in  W W , interpreted
as empty when  x x  is closed. Rooting every cluster at each of its
vertices and applying Cauchy–Schwarz gives, for  0 < ρ < 1 0<\rho<1 , 
 
 
 
 
 𝔼 ​ ∑ C ∈ ℭ | H p ​ ( C ) | \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sum_{C\in\mathfrak{C}}|H_{p}(C)| 
 ≤ [ ∑ x ∈ W 𝔼 ​ H p ​ ( C x ) 2 | C x | 1 + ρ ] 1 / 2 \displaystyle\leq\left[\sum_{x\in W}\mathbb{E}\frac{H_{p}(C_{x})^{2}}{|C_{x}|^{1+\rho}}\right]^{1/2} 
 
 (6.7) 
 
 
 
 × [ ∑ x ∈ W 𝔼 ​ 𝟏 { C x ∩ ∂ in W ≠ ∅ } | C x | 1 − ρ ] 1 / 2 . \displaystyle\times\left[\sum_{x\in W}\mathbb{E}\frac{\mathbf{1}\{C_{x}\cap\partial_{\rm in}W\neq\varnothing\}}{|C_{x}|^{1-\rho}}\right]^{1/2}. 
 
 
 The second bracket before taking its square root equals
 𝔼 ​ ∑ C ∈ ℭ | C | ρ \mathbb{E}\sum_{C\in\mathfrak{C}}|C|^{\rho} , and hence is at most
 | W | ρ ​ | ∂ in W | 1 − ρ |W|^{\rho}|\partial_{\rm in}W|^{1-\rho} , because distinct clusters
contain distinct boundary vertices. 
 
 
 For the first bracket the summand is defined to be zero on
 { x ​  closed } \{x\text{ closed}\} ; we work conditionally on  x x  being open.
Start with the already open root and explore  C x C_{x}  one further site at
a time, always testing an untested neighbor of the discovered open set.
Give an open test increment  1 − p 1-p  and a closed test increment  − p -p .
The resulting process  ( S t ) (S_{t})  is a martingale with increments bounded
by one and conditional variance  p ⁡ ( 1 − p ) ≤ 1 / 4 p(1-p)\leq 1/4 . At the stopping time 
 

 
 
 T = | C x | − 1 + | ∂ V W C x | T=|C_{x}|-1+|\partial^{W}_{V}C_{x}| 
 
 
 every non-root open site and every external boundary site has been
tested, and therefore 
 

 
 
 S T = ( 1 − p ) ​ ( | C x | − 1 ) − p ​ | ∂ V W C x | = − H p ​ ( C x ) − ( 1 − p ) , | C x | ≤ T + 1 ≤ ( d + 1 ) ​ | C x | . S_{T}=(1-p)(|C_{x}|-1)-p|\partial^{W}_{V}C_{x}|=-H_{p}(C_{x})-(1-p),\qquad|C_{x}|\leq T+1\leq(d+1)|C_{x}|. 
 
 
 This also covers  C x = W C_{x}=W , when  T = | C x | − 1 T=|C_{x}|-1 . Splitting according to
 2 j ≤ T + 1 < 2 j + 1 2^{j}\leq T+1<2^{j+1} , using the stopped square-martingale identity,
and using
 | H p ​ ( C x ) | 2 ≤ 2 ​ S T 2 + 2 |H_{p}(C_{x})|^{2}\leq 2S_{T}^{2}+2 , yields the following calculation. On
the  j j -th event, 
 

 
 
 | C x | ≥ T + 1 d + 1 ≥ 2 j d + 1 . |C_{x}|\geq\frac{T+1}{d+1}\geq\frac{2^{j}}{d+1}. 
 
 
 The stopped square-martingale identity and the conditional variance
bound give 
 

 
 
 𝔼 p ​ [ S T ∧ ( 2 j + 1 − 1 ) 2 ∣ x ​  open ] = 𝔼 p ​ [ ∑ t < T ∧ ( 2 j + 1 − 1 ) 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( S t + 1 − S t ) 2 ∣ ℱ t ] | x ​  open ] ≤ 2 j − 1 . \mathbb{E}_{p}[S_{T\wedge(2^{j+1}-1)}^{2}\mid x\text{ open}]=\mathbb{E}_{p}\left[\sum_{t<T\wedge(2^{j+1}-1)}\mathbb{E}[(S_{t+1}-S_{t})^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}]\,\middle|\,x\text{ open}\right]\leq 2^{j-1}. 
 
 
 On  { T + 1 < 2 j + 1 } \{T+1<2^{j+1}\} , the stopped variable in this display is
 S T S_{T} . Therefore the contribution of the  j j -th event is at most 
 

 
 
 C ( d , ρ ) 2 − j ⁡ ( 1 + ρ ) ( 𝔼 p [ S T 2 ; T + 1 < 2 j + 1 ∣ x  open ] + 1 ) ≤ C ( d , ρ ) ( 2 − j ​ ρ + 2 − j ⁡ ( 1 + ρ ) ) . C(d,\rho)2^{-j(1+\rho)}\left(\mathbb{E}_{p}[S_{T}^{2};\,T+1<2^{j+1}\mid x\text{ open}]+1\right)\leq C(d,\rho)(2^{-j\rho}+2^{-j(1+\rho)}). 
 
 
 Summing this geometric bound over  j ≥ 0 j\geq 0  proves 
 

 
 
 𝔼 ​ H p ​ ( C x ) 2 | C x | 1 + ρ ≤ C ⁡ ( d , ρ ) . \mathbb{E}\frac{H_{p}(C_{x})^{2}}{|C_{x}|^{1+\rho}}\leq C(d,\rho). 
 
 (6.8) 
 
 The unconditional expectation is  p p  times the conditional one, and
the closed-root contribution is zero, so no first-test term is missing.
Equations ( 6.7 )–( 6.8 ) now imply 
 

 
 
 𝔼 ​ ∑ C ∈ ℭ | H p ​ ( C ) | ≤ C ⁡ ( d , ρ ) ​ | W | ​ q ( 1 − ρ ) / 2 . \mathbb{E}\sum_{C\in\mathfrak{C}}|H_{p}(C)|\leq C(d,\rho)|W|q^{(1-\rho)/2}. 
 
 (6.9) 
 
 Since  W = B m W=B_{m} , every vertex of  W W  either belongs to  B m − 1 B_{m-1} 
or is joined by an edge to a vertex of  B m − 1 B_{m-1} . Consequently, 
 

 
 
 | W | = | B m | ≤ ( d + 1 ) ​ | B m − 1 | , |W|=|B_{m}|\leq(d+1)|B_{m-1}|, 
 
 
 including when  m = 1 m=1 . Combining ( 6.2 ), ( 6.3 ), ( 6.6 ), and
( 6.9 ), using  p ≥ η p\geq\eta , and taking  ρ = 2 ​ ε \rho=2\varepsilon  proves
( 6.1 ), after changing constants and treating bounded  n n  trivially.
 □ \square 
 
 
 The form of the a priori uniqueness zone used below requires an explicit
low-growth hypothesis. More precisely, for every  K ≥ 1 K\geq 1  and
 0 < ε < 1 / 2 0<\varepsilon<1/2  there are
 c = c ⁡ ( d , η , ε ) > 0 c=c(d,\eta,\varepsilon)>0  and
 C = C ⁡ ( d , η , ε , K ) < ∞ C=C(d,\eta,\varepsilon,K)<\infty  such that 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( Piv s ⁡ [ r , n ] ) ≤ C ​ [ log ⁡ | B n | n ] 1 / 2 − ε . \mathbb{P}_{p}\bigl(\operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[r,n]\bigr)\leq C\left[\frac{\log|B_{n}|}{n}\right]^{1/2-\varepsilon}. 
 
 (6.10) 
 
 whenever  n ≥ 3 n\geq 3 ,  p ∈ [ η , 1 ] p\in[\eta,1] ,  1 ≤ r ≤ c ​ log ⁡ n 1\leq r\leq c\log n , and 
 

 
 
 log ⁡ | B n | ≤ ( log ⁡ n ) K . \log|B_{n}|\leq(\log n)^{K}. 
 
 (6.11) 
 
 Here is the site version of the Cerf comparison, including the point that
would otherwise cause a factor  ( 1 − p ) − 1 (1-p)^{-1} . On
 Piv s ⁡ [ r , n ] \operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[r,n] , choose canonically two crossing
clusters, vertices  a , b ∈ S r a,b\in S_{r}  in those clusters, and a path from
 a a  to  b b  through  o o  of length at most  2 ​ r 2r . Successively open
the closed sites of this path in a fixed order. Immediately before the
first opening that joins the two chosen clusters, its site  z z  is
already closed and touches two distinct open clusters, each containing
one of the original crossing clusters. Since  z ∈ B r z\in B_{r} , both clusters
reach distance at least  n − r n-r  from  z z . Thus the modified
configuration lies in the translate of
 Piv s ⁡ [ 1 , n − r ] \operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[1,n-r]  centered at  z z . 
 
 
 The map opens at most  2 ​ r 2r  sites and never closes a site. Recording
 z z , the path, and the modified subset gives the following explicit
fibre bound. Let  S S  be the set of path sites changed from closed to
open before the joining step. Given the image and  S S , the original
configuration is recovered by closing precisely the sites of  S S .
There are at most  | B r | 2 ≤ d 2 ​ r + 2 |B_{r}|^{2}\leq d^{2r+2}  choices for  a , b a,b , at most
 d 2 ​ r d^{2r}  paths of length at most  2 ​ r 2r  through  o o , at most
 2 ​ r + 1 2r+1  choices for  z z , and at most  2 2 ​ r + 1 2^{2r+1}  choices for  S S .
After increasing a degree-dependent base, their product is at most
 C d r C_{d}^{r} . If  | S | = s |S|=s , the original-to-image Bernoulli weight ratio is
 ( ( 1 − p ) / p ) s ≤ p − 2 ​ r ≤ η − 2 ​ r ((1-p)/p)^{s}\leq p^{-2r}\leq\eta^{-2r} . Integrating all unchanged
coordinates and summing the recorded data therefore gives, for
 2 ​ r ≤ n 2r\leq n , 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( Piv s ⁡ [ r , n ] ) ≤ ( C d / η ) 2 ​ r ​ ℙ p ​ ( Piv s ⁡ [ 1 , n − r ] ) . \mathbb{P}_{p}(\operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[r,n])\leq(C_{d}/\eta)^{2r}\mathbb{P}_{p}(\operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[1,n-r]). 
 
 (6.12) 
 
 Let  x n = log ⁡ | B n | / n x_{n}=\log|B_{n}|/n , apply Lemma 6.1 with parameter
 ε / 2 \varepsilon/2 , and take any integer  1 ≤ r ≤ c ​ log ⁡ n 1\leq r\leq c\log n . For large  n n ,
 r ≤ n / 2 r\leq n/2 ,  | B n − r | ≤ | B n | |B_{n-r}|\leq|B_{n}| , and ( 6.12 ) gives, for a constant
 A = A ⁡ ( d , η ) A=A(d,\eta) , 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( Piv s ⁡ [ r , n ] ) ≤ C ​ n A ​ c ​ x n 1 / 2 − ε / 2 = C ​ x n 1 / 2 − ε ​ ( n A ​ c ​ x n ε / 2 ) . \mathbb{P}_{p}(\operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[r,n])\leq Cn^{Ac}x_{n}^{1/2-\varepsilon/2}=Cx_{n}^{1/2-\varepsilon}\bigl(n^{Ac}x_{n}^{\varepsilon/2}\bigr). 
 
 
 Under ( 6.11 ), 
 

 
 
 n A ​ c ​ x n ε / 2 ≤ n A ​ c − ε / 2 ​ ( log ⁡ n ) K ​ ε / 2 . n^{Ac}x_{n}^{\varepsilon/2}\leq n^{Ac-\varepsilon/2}(\log n)^{K\varepsilon/2}. 
 
 
 Choose  c > 0 c>0  so that  A ​ c < ε / 4 Ac<\varepsilon/4 . The last display is then
bounded, and the finitely many remaining  n n  are absorbed into  C C .
This proves ( 6.10 ). No lower bound on  log ⁡ | B n | \log|B_{n}|  is used. 
 
 
 We will also need the sharper form of the Cerf comparison when a good
local two-point bound is available. 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 6.A   (Site nearby-clusters comparison) . 
 
 If
 0 < p ≤ 1 0<p\leq 1  and  1 < r ≤ m ≤ n / 2 − 2 1<r\leq m\leq n/2-2 , then 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( Piv s ⁡ [ r , n ] ) ≤ ℙ p ​ ( Piv s ⁡ [ 1 , n / 2 ] ) ​ | S r | 2 ​ | B m + 1 | min a , b ∈ S r ⁡ ℙ p ​ ( a ↔ b ​  in  ​ B m ) . \mathbb{P}_{p}(\operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[r,n])\leq\mathbb{P}_{p}(\operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[1,n/2])\frac{|S_{r}|^{2}\,|B_{m+1}|}{\displaystyle\min_{a,b\in S_{r}}\mathbb{P}_{p}(a\leftrightarrow b\text{ in }B_{m})}. 
 
 (6.13) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For  p > 0 p>0 , the denominator is strictly positive: a
geodesic from  a a  to  o o  followed by one from  o o  to  b b  lies in
 B m B_{m} , and the event that every site of this walk is open has positive
probability. The case  p = 1 p=1  is immediate, so below we may take
 0 < p < 1 0<p<1 . On the pivotal event choose  a , b ∈ S r a,b\in S_{r}  in two different
clusters crossing  B n B_{n} , and let  C C  be the cluster of  a a  in
 B n B_{n} . Write  T ⁡ ( C ) = C ∪ ∂ V B n C T(C)=C\cup\partial_{V}^{B_{n}}C . The exact-cluster event
 { K a B n = C } \{K_{a}^{B_{n}}=C\}  fixes  C C  open and its external vertex boundary
closed. Conditional on it, all sites of  B n ∖ T ⁡ ( C ) B_{n}\setminus T(C)  are still
independent Bernoulli sites. Since  b b  belongs to another open cluster,
 b ∉ T ⁡ ( C ) b\notin T(C) , and that cluster connects  b b  to  S n S_{n}  inside
 B n ∖ T ⁡ ( C ) B_{n}\setminus T(C) . 
 
 
 For fixed  a , b ∈ S r a,b\in S_{r} , define the admissible exact-cluster class 
 

 
 
 𝒞 a , b := { C ⊆ B n : a ∈ C , ℙ p ( K a B n = C ) > 0 , C ∩ S n ≠ ∅ , b ∉ T ( C ) } . \mathcal{C}_{a,b}:=\bigl\{C\subseteq B_{n}:a\in C,\ \mathbb{P}_{p}(K_{a}^{B_{n}}=C)>0,\ C\cap S_{n}\neq\varnothing,\ b\notin T(C)\bigr\}. 
 
 
 Thus every  C ∈ 𝒞 a , b C\in\mathcal{C}_{a,b}  crosses from  a ∈ S r a\in S_{r}  to
 S n S_{n} , and the coordinate of  b b  belongs to the residual product
space. If  a , b a,b  witness the pivotal event and  C = K a B n C=K_{a}^{B_{n}} , then
 C ∈ 𝒞 a , b C\in\mathcal{C}_{a,b} : the crossing property gives
 C ∩ S n ≠ ∅ C\cap S_{n}\neq\varnothing , while the fact that  b b  lies in a
different open cluster implies  b ∉ C ∪ ∂ V B n C b\notin C\cup\partial_{V}^{B_{n}}C .
For  C ∈ 𝒞 a , b C\in\mathcal{C}_{a,b} , let  D C D_{C}  be the latter connection event and
let  F C F_{C}  be the event that, inside  B m ∖ T ⁡ ( C ) B_{m}\setminus T(C) ,  b b  is
connected to the external vertex boundary of  T ⁡ ( C ) T(C) . Both are increasing
events of the unrevealed coordinates. Conditional on
 { K a B n = C } \{K_{a}^{B_{n}}=C\} , those coordinates have their original product
Bernoulli law. Harris–FKG  [ 12 , Section 2.2]  therefore gives 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( D C ∩ F C ∣ K a B n = C ) ≥ ℙ p ​ ( D C ∣ K a B n = C ) ​ ℙ p ​ ( F C ∣ K a B n = C ) . \mathbb{P}_{p}(D_{C}\cap F_{C}\mid K_{a}^{B_{n}}=C)\geq\mathbb{P}_{p}(D_{C}\mid K_{a}^{B_{n}}=C)\mathbb{P}_{p}(F_{C}\mid K_{a}^{B_{n}}=C). 
 
 
 The second factor is positive by the fresh-path comparison in the next
paragraph. Dividing by it gives, with all probabilities below understood
in this residual product law, 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( D C ) ≤ ℙ p ​ ( D C ∩ F C ) ℙ p ​ ( F C ) . \mathbb{P}_{p}(D_{C})\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}_{p}(D_{C}\cap F_{C})}{\mathbb{P}_{p}(F_{C})}. 
 
 
 To compare with an unconditioned two-point function, sample an auxiliary
fresh Bernoulli configuration on all of  B m B_{m} . Every fresh open path
from  a a  to  b b  has, after its last visit to the deterministic set
 T ⁡ ( C ) T(C) , a suffix using only exterior coordinates and witnessing
 F C F_{C} . Since those exterior coordinates have exactly the conditional
law under the exact-cluster event, this coupling gives 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( F C ) ≥ ℙ p ​ ( a ↔ b ​  in  ​ B m ) . \mathbb{P}_{p}(F_{C})\geq\mathbb{P}_{p}(a\leftrightarrow b\text{ in }B_{m}). 
 
 
 On  D C ∩ F C D_{C}\cap F_{C} , choose the first site  y ∉ T ⁡ ( C ) y\notin T(C)  on the
 F C F_{C} -connection and an adjacent site  z ∈ T ⁡ ( C ) z\in T(C) . Necessarily
 z ∈ ∂ V C z\in\partial_{V}C , so  z z  is closed and touches both the crossing
cluster  C C  and the crossing cluster containing  b b . Moreover
 z ∈ B m + 1 z\in B_{m+1} , and both clusters reach distance at least
 n − m − 1 ≥ n / 2 n-m-1\geq n/2  from  z z . Thus the translate centered at  z z  of
 Piv s ⁡ [ 1 , n / 2 ] \operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[1,n/2]  occurs. 
 
 
 Let 
 

 
 
 θ r = min a , b ∈ S r ⁡ ℙ p ​ ( a ↔ b ​  in  ​ B m ) > 0 . \theta_{r}=\min_{a,b\in S_{r}}\mathbb{P}_{p}(a\leftrightarrow b\text{ in }B_{m})>0. 
 
 
 For fixed  a , b a,b , the exact-cluster atoms are disjoint. Multiplying the
conditional estimate above by  ℙ p ​ ( K a B n = C ) \mathbb{P}_{p}(K_{a}^{B_{n}}=C)  and summing
over the admissible class gives 
 

 
 
 ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 a , b ℙ p ​ ( K a B n = C , D C ) ≤ θ r − 1 ​ ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 a , b ℙ p ​ ( K a B n = C , D C ∩ F C ) ≤ θ r − 1 ​ ∑ z ∈ B m + 1 ℙ p ​ ( Piv z s ⁡ [ 1 , n / 2 ] ) . \begin{split}&\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{a,b}}\mathbb{P}_{p}(K_{a}^{B_{n}}=C,\,D_{C})\\
&\quad\leq\theta_{r}^{-1}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{a,b}}\mathbb{P}_{p}(K_{a}^{B_{n}}=C,\,D_{C}\cap F_{C})\\
&\quad\leq\theta_{r}^{-1}\sum_{z\in B_{m+1}}\mathbb{P}_{p}(\operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}_{z}[1,n/2]).\end{split} 
 
 
 The last inequality uses the pointwise containment proved in the preceding
paragraph; its use here is legitimate precisely because every indexed
cluster satisfies  C ∩ S n ≠ ∅ C\cap S_{n}\neq\varnothing . We then take a union bound
over the selected site  z z ; after the sum over the disjoint admissible
atoms, no conditioning remains. Transitivity
makes each pivotal probability in the last sum equal to the probability
rooted at  o o . Finally sum over the at most  | S r | 2 |S_{r}|^{2}  ordered choices
of  ( a , b ) (a,b) . This is exactly
( 6.13 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 6.2  Influence bound 

 
 For a set  A A , let  𝐆 ρ A \mathbf{G}_{\rho}^{A}  denote a ghost field that marks
each site of  A A  independently with probability  ρ \rho . The following
is the precise site form of the sharp-threshold input. 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 6.B   (Countable-product Russo–Talagrand exhaustion) . 
 
 Let  I I  be countable,
let  ν q \nu_{q}  be Bernoulli- q q  product measure on  { 0 , 1 } I \{0,1\}^{I} , and
let  E E  be an increasing measurable event. Writing
 Piv i ⁡ ( E ) \operatorname{Piv}_{i}(E)  for the event that coordinate  i i  is
pivotal, the Russo–Talagrand bound 
 

 
 
 d d ​ q ​ log ⁡ ν q ​ ( E ) 1 − ν q ​ ( E ) ≥ c q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ​ log ⁡ ( 2 / [ q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ] ) ​ log ⁡ 1 q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ​ sup i ∈ I ν q ​ ( Piv i ⁡ ( E ) ) \frac{d}{dq}\log\frac{\nu_{q}(E)}{1-\nu_{q}(E)}\geq\frac{c}{q(1-q)\log(2/[q(1-q)])}\log\frac{1}{q(1-q)\sup_{i\in I}\nu_{q}(\operatorname{Piv}_{i}(E))} 
 
 (6.14) 
 
 holds for almost every  q q  for which  0 < ν q ​ ( E ) < 1 0<\nu_{q}(E)<1  and the
logarithm on the right is positive. If  E E  is a connection event
between random subsets of fixed finite seed sets, using paths in a
possibly infinite domain  D D , and  W n ↑ V W_{n}\uparrow V  is a finite
vertex exhaustion containing the seeds, then 
 

 
 
 E n := { the required connection has a path in  ​ D ∩ W n } ↑ E , ν q ​ ( E n ) ↑ ν q ​ ( E ) , E_{n}:=\{\text{the required connection has a path in }D\cap W_{n}\}\uparrow E,\quad\nu_{q}(E_{n})\uparrow\nu_{q}(E), 
 
 (6.15) 
 
 and, for every fixed coordinate  i i , 
 

 
 
 𝟏 Piv i ⁡ ( E n ) ⟶ 𝟏 Piv i ⁡ ( E ) pointwise , ν q ​ ( Piv i ⁡ ( E n ) ) ⟶ ν q ​ ( Piv i ⁡ ( E ) ) . \mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{Piv}_{i}(E_{n})}\longrightarrow\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{Piv}_{i}(E)}\quad\text{pointwise},\qquad\nu_{q}(\operatorname{Piv}_{i}(E_{n}))\longrightarrow\nu_{q}(\operatorname{Piv}_{i}(E)). 
 
 (6.16) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Every connection has a finite path witness, which proves
( 6.15 ). Apply this observation after setting coordinate  i i  first to
one and then to zero. The two resulting increasing sequences of
indicators converge to the corresponding indicators for  E E ; their
difference is the pivotal indicator, proving ( 6.16 ) by dominated
convergence. 
 
 
 For completeness, we give the countable-product passage in functional
form. Enumerate  I = { 1 , 2 , … } I=\{1,2,\ldots\} , let  ℱ n \mathcal{F}_{n}  be generated by
the first  n n  coordinates, and put 
 

 
 
 f n = ν q ​ ( 𝟏 E ∣ ℱ n ) . f_{n}=\nu_{q}(\mathbf{1}_{E}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n}). 
 
 
 For a function  f f  on a finite Bernoulli product, write 
 

 
 
 D i ​ f ​ ( ω ) = f ⁡ ( ω i → 1 ) − f ⁡ ( ω i → 0 ) , Q ⁡ ( q ) = q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ​ log ​ 2 q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) . D_{i}f(\omega)=f(\omega^{i\to 1})-f(\omega^{i\to 0}),\qquad Q(q)=q(1-q)\log\frac{2}{q(1-q)}. 
 
 
 The finite functional  L 1 L^{1} – L 2 L^{2}  inequality of
 [ 21 , Theorem 1.5]  is written there using the centered
coordinate operator
 Δ i f = f − ν q ( f ∣ σ ( ω j : j ≠ i ) ) \Delta_{i}f=f-\nu_{q}(f\mid\sigma(\omega_{j}:j\neq i)) . Conditional on
all coordinates other than  i i , a direct two-point calculation gives 
 

 
 
 ‖ Δ i ​ f ‖ 2 2 = q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ​ ‖ D i ​ f ‖ 2 2 , ‖ Δ i ​ f ‖ 1 = 2 ​ q ​ ( 1 − q ) ​ ‖ D i ​ f ‖ 1 . \|\Delta_{i}f\|_{2}^{2}=q(1-q)\|D_{i}f\|_{2}^{2},\qquad\|\Delta_{i}f\|_{1}=2q(1-q)\|D_{i}f\|_{1}. 
 
 
 The ratio of these two norms is
 ‖ D i ​ f ‖ 2 / [ 2 ​ q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ​ ‖ D i ​ f ‖ 1 ] \|D_{i}f\|_{2}/[2\sqrt{q(1-q)}\|D_{i}f\|_{1}] . Since
 2 ​ q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ≤ 1 2\sqrt{q(1-q)}\leq 1 , its logarithmic denominator is no smaller than
the denominator displayed below. Substitution into Talagrand’s biased
inequality and weakening that denominator therefore gives, with a
universal constant  C C , 
 

 
 
 Var ν q ⁡ ( f ) ≤ C ​ Q ​ ( q ) ​ ∑ i ‖ D i ​ f ‖ 2 2 log ⁡ ( e ​ ‖ D i ​ f ‖ 2 / ‖ D i ​ f ‖ 1 ) , \operatorname{Var}_{\nu_{q}}(f)\leq CQ(q)\sum_{i}\frac{\|D_{i}f\|_{2}^{2}}{\log\!\left(e\|D_{i}f\|_{2}/\|D_{i}f\|_{1}\right)}, 
 
 (6.17) 
 
 where a zero summand is interpreted as zero. Apply this to  f n f_{n} .
Monotonicity of  E E  implies  D i ​ f n ≥ 0 D_{i}f_{n}\geq 0 , and, for every  n ≥ i n\geq i , 
 

 
 
 ‖ D i ​ f n ‖ 1 = ν q ​ ( Piv i ⁡ ( E ) ) = : a i . \|D_{i}f_{n}\|_{1}=\nu_{q}(\operatorname{Piv}_{i}(E))=:a_{i}. 
 
 (6.18) 
 
 Indeed, integrating the coordinate difference telescopes to
 ν q ​ ( E ∣ ω i = 1 ) − ν q ​ ( E ∣ ω i = 0 ) \nu_{q}(E\mid\omega_{i}=1)-\nu_{q}(E\mid\omega_{i}=0) , which is  a i a_{i} .
More precisely, 
 

 
 
 D i f n = ν q ( 𝟏 Piv i ⁡ ( E ) ∣ ℱ n ( i ) ) , ℱ n ( i ) = σ ( ω j : 1 ≤ j ≤ n , j ≠ i ) . D_{i}f_{n}=\nu_{q}\!\left(\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{Piv}_{i}(E)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n}^{(i)}\right),\qquad\mathcal{F}_{n}^{(i)}=\sigma(\omega_{j}:1\leq j\leq n,\ j\neq i). 
 
 
 The martingale convergence theorem consequently gives 
 

 
 
 D i ​ f n ⟶ 𝟏 Piv i ⁡ ( E ) in  ​ L 2 , ‖ D i ​ f n ‖ 2 2 ⟶ a i . D_{i}f_{n}\longrightarrow\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{Piv}_{i}(E)}\quad\hbox{in }L^{2},\qquad\|D_{i}f_{n}\|_{2}^{2}\longrightarrow a_{i}. 
 
 (6.19) 
 
 
 
 Let  S = ∑ i a i S=\sum_{i}a_{i}  and  a ∗ = sup i a i a_{*}=\sup_{i}a_{i} . If  S = ∞ S=\infty , the desired
influence lower bound is automatic. Otherwise, since
 0 ≤ D i ​ f n ≤ 1 0\leq D_{i}f_{n}\leq 1 , each summand in ( 6.17 ) is at most
 ‖ D i ​ f n ‖ 2 2 ≤ ‖ D i ​ f n ‖ 1 = a i \|D_{i}f_{n}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|D_{i}f_{n}\|_{1}=a_{i} . Equations
( 6.18 )–( 6.19 ) therefore permit dominated
convergence for the series, while  f n → 𝟏 E f_{n}\to\mathbf{1}_{E}  in  L 2 L^{2} . We get 
 

 
 
 Var ν q ⁡ ( 𝟏 E ) ≤ C ​ Q ​ ( q ) ​ ∑ i a i log ⁡ ( e / a i ) ≤ C ​ Q ​ ( q ) ​ S log ⁡ ( e / a ∗ ) . \operatorname{Var}_{\nu_{q}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})\leq CQ(q)\sum_{i}\frac{a_{i}}{\log(e/\sqrt{a_{i}})}\leq\frac{CQ(q)S}{\log(e/\sqrt{a_{*}})}. 
 
 (6.20) 
 
 For clarity, the second variance estimate is also proved here rather
than imported through a normalization convention. Finite-product
variance tensorization (the Efron–Stein inequality; compare
 [ 4 , Section 3.1] ) follows by iterating the
conditional-variance identity over the independent coordinates and
using conditional Jensen at each step. Applied to  f n f_{n} , it gives 
 

 
 
 Var ν q ⁡ ( f n ) ≤ q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ D i ​ f n ‖ 2 2 ≤ q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ​ ∑ i = 1 n a i ≤ q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ​ S . \operatorname{Var}_{\nu_{q}}(f_{n})\leq q(1-q)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|D_{i}f_{n}\|_{2}^{2}\leq q(1-q)\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\leq q(1-q)S. 
 
 
 The middle inequality follows from
 0 ≤ D i ​ f n ≤ 1 0\leq D_{i}f_{n}\leq 1  and
 ‖ D i ​ f n ‖ 1 = a i \|D_{i}f_{n}\|_{1}=a_{i} . Since
 f n → 𝟏 E f_{n}\to\mathbf{1}_{E}  in  L 2 L^{2} , passage to the limit proves directly
that
 Var ν q ⁡ ( 𝟏 E ) ≤ q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ​ S \operatorname{Var}_{\nu_{q}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})\leq q(1-q)S .
Combining this bound with ( 6.20 ), and using 
 

 
 
 log ⁡ ( e / a ∗ ) + log ⁡ 2 q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ≥ 1 2 ​ log ⁡ 1 q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ​ a ∗ , \log(e/\sqrt{a_{*}})+\log\frac{2}{q(1-q)}\geq\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{1}{q(1-q)a_{*}}, 
 
 
 yields 
 

 
 
 ∑ i ∈ I ν q ​ ( Piv i ⁡ ( E ) ) ≥ c ​ ν q ​ ( E ) ​ ( 1 − ν q ​ ( E ) ) q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ​ log ⁡ ( 2 / [ q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ] ) ​ log ⁡ 1 q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) ​ sup i ∈ I ν q ​ ( Piv i ⁡ ( E ) ) . \sum_{i\in I}\nu_{q}(\operatorname{Piv}_{i}(E))\geq\frac{c\nu_{q}(E)(1-\nu_{q}(E))}{q(1-q)\log(2/[q(1-q)])}\log\frac{1}{q(1-q)\sup_{i\in I}\nu_{q}(\operatorname{Piv}_{i}(E))}. 
 
 
 
 
 For Russo’s step, raise only the first  n n 
coordinates from  q q  to  q + t q+t , keeping all remaining coordinates at
 q q . Finite-dimensional Russo differentiation gives the sum of the
first  n n  pivotal probabilities at  t = 0 t=0 , while monotonicity bounds
this mixed measure above by  ν q + t ​ ( E ) \nu_{q+t}(E) . Let  n → ∞ n\to\infty .
This is the finite-product Russo formula
 [ 20 , Section 4, Lemma 3, equation (4.2)] ; see also
 [ 12 , Section 2.4] . At every differentiability point of
 q ↦ ν q ​ ( E ) q\mapsto\nu_{q}(E) , combining
this derivative bound with the countable influence inequality gives
( 6.14 ). Monotone functions are differentiable almost everywhere, which
is all that is required when the inequality is integrated.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 6.C   (Ghost threshold) . 
 
 For every  2 ≤ d < ∞ 2\leq d<\infty  and  0 < D < ∞ 0<D<\infty  there are
 c = c ⁡ ( d , D ) > 0 c=c(d,D)>0  and  h 0 = h 0 ​ ( d , D ) > 0 h_{0}=h_{0}(d,D)>0  such that the following holds. Let
 G G  be an infinite connected unimodular transitive graph of degree
 d d . Let  Λ ⊆ V ⁡ ( G ) \Lambda\subseteq V(G)  be nonempty and let
 A , B ⊆ Λ A,B\subseteq\Lambda  be nonempty finite sets. Let
 1 / d ≤ p 1 < p 2 < 1 1/d\leq p_{1}<p_{2}<1 , put
 δ = δ ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 ) ≤ D \delta=\delta(p_{1},p_{2})\leq D , and suppose that site percolation has at
most one infinite cluster throughout  [ p 1 , p 2 ] [p_{1},p_{2}] . If  h ≤ h 0 h\leq h_{0} ,
 h ​ r ≥ 1 hr\geq 1 , and 
 

 
 
 sup p ∈ [ p 1 , p 2 ] ℙ p ​ ( Piv s ⁡ [ 1 , h ​ r ] ) < h , \sup_{p\in[p_{1},p_{2}]}\mathbb{P}_{p}(\operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[1,hr])<h, 
 
 
 then 
 

 
 
 ( 𝐆 h A ⊗ 𝐆 h B ⊗ ℙ p 1 ) ​ ( 𝐆 h A ↔ 𝐆 h B ​  in  ​ Λ ) ≥ h c ​ δ ⟹ ( 𝐆 h c A ⊗ 𝐆 h c B ⊗ ℙ p 2 ) ​ ( 𝐆 h c A ↔ 𝐆 h c B ​  in  ​ B r ​ ( Λ ) ) ≥ 1 − h c ​ δ . \begin{split}&(\mathbf{G}_{h}^{A}\otimes\mathbf{G}_{h}^{B}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{p_{1}})(\mathbf{G}_{h}^{A}\leftrightarrow\mathbf{G}_{h}^{B}\text{ in }\Lambda)\geq h^{c\delta}\quad\Longrightarrow\\

 6.3  Chaining and endpoints 

 
 We first prove the site gluing statement used in the chain. For a set
 Z Z , write  K Z D ​ ( p ) K_{Z}^{D}(p)  for the union of the  p p -open components in
 D D  that meet  Z Z .
For nonempty  A , B ⊆ Λ A,B\subseteq\Lambda , set 
 

 
 
 τ p Λ ​ ( A , B ) = min a ∈ A , b ∈ B ⁡ ℙ p ​ ( a ↔ b ​  in  ​ Λ ) , τ p Λ ​ ( A ) = τ p Λ ​ ( A , A ) . \tau_{p}^{\Lambda}(A,B)=\min_{a\in A,b\in B}\mathbb{P}_{p}(a\leftrightarrow b\text{ in }\Lambda),\qquad\tau_{p}^{\Lambda}(A)=\tau_{p}^{\Lambda}(A,A). 
 
 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 6.D   (Ghost gluing) . 
 
 For every  2 ≤ d < ∞ 2\leq d<\infty  and  0 < D < ∞ 0<D<\infty  there are
 a 1 , a 2 > 0 a_{1},a_{2}>0  with the following property. Let  G G  be unimodular and
transitive of degree  d d , let
 1 / d ≤ p 1 < p 2 < 1 1/d\leq p_{1}<p_{2}<1 , put  δ = δ ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 ) ≤ D \delta=\delta(p_{1},p_{2})\leq D , and

 [ p 1 , p 2 ] [p_{1},p_{2}] . Let  h h  and  r r  satisfy the smallness, radius, and
pivotal hypotheses of Auxiliary Lemma  6.C . Then,
for every
nonempty finite  A , Y ⊆ Λ A,Y\subseteq\Lambda  and every  x ∈ Λ x\in\Lambda , under
the common uniform-label coupling of all percolation parameters,

x\not\leftrightarrow Y\text{ at }p_{2}\text{ in }B_{r}(\Lambda)\end{array}\right)\leq 3h^{a_{2}\delta^{4}}.

 Proof.

 For a fixed good pair, take two independent ghost fields on

give the following three-factor calculation. Let  G X , G Y G_{X},G_{Y}  be the

 ℙ p 1 ​ ( a ↔ b ​  in  ​ Λ ) ≥ h a 1 ​ δ . \mathbb{P}_{p_{1}}(a\leftrightarrow b\text{ in }\Lambda)\geq h^{a_{1}\delta}. 
 
 
 Averaging over the selected pair, and multiplying by the two independent

 ( 𝐆 h A ∩ C X ⊗ 𝐆 h A ∩ C Y ⊗ ℙ p 1 ) ​ ( 𝐆 h A ∩ C X ↔ 𝐆 h A ∩ C Y ​  in  ​ Λ ) ≥ h 3 ​ a 1 ​ δ ≥ h c 0 ​ δ / 3 .  Apply Auxiliary Lemma  6.C  from  p 1 p_{1}  to
 p 4 / 3 p_{4/3} . Since
nonconnection of  C X C_{X}  and  C Y C_{Y}  forces nonconnection of the two

 (6.30) 
 
 
 
 For  1 ≤ t ≤ 2 1\leq t\leq 2 , abbreviate 
 

 
 
 p t = Spr ⁡ ( p 1 ; ( t − 1 ) ​ δ ​ ( p 1 , p 2 ) ) , p_{t}=\operatorname{Spr}\bigl(p_{1};(t-1)\delta(p_{1},p_{2})\bigr), 
 
 
 so that  p 1 , p 2 p_{1},p_{2}  are the endpoints and
 p 4 / 3 < p 5 / 3 p_{4/3}<p_{5/3} . 
 
 
 Continue with this fixed good pair and work on the event that
 x ↮ Y x\not\leftrightarrow Y  at  p 2 p_{2} . Put

D_{X}=\partial_{V}^{B_{r}(\Lambda)}C_{X},\qquad D_{Y}^{*}=\partial_{V}^{\Lambda}C_{Y}\cup(Y\setminus C_{Y}),\qquad D_{Y}=\partial_{V}^{B_{r}(\Lambda)}C_{Y}\cup((Y\setminus C_{Y})\setminus D_{X}). 
 
 
 The joint exact-cluster event has the coordinate description 
 

 
 
 { U z ≤ p 2 : z ∈ C X } ∩ { U z > p 2 : z ∈ D X } ∩ { U z ≤ p 1 : z ∈ C Y } ∩ { U z > p 1 : z ∈ D Y ∗ } . \{U_{z}\leq p_{2}:z\in C_{X}\}\cap\{U_{z}>p_{2}:z\in D_{X}\}\cap\{U_{z}\leq p_{1}:z\in C_{Y}\}\cap\{U_{z}>p_{1}:z\in D_{Y}^{*}\}. 
 
 (6.31) 
 
 Redundant conditions on overlaps are understood. Thus it is measurable
in the coordinates of  C X ∪ D X ∪ C Y ∪ D Y ∗ C_{X}\cup D_{X}\cup C_{Y}\cup D_{Y}^{*} , and all other
labels remain independent uniforms by
Lemma  2.1 , applied successively at thresholds
 p 1 p_{1}  and  p 2 p_{2} . On  ℬ \mathcal{B} ,  x x  is open and
belongs to  C X C_{X} . Removing  D X ∩ ( Y ∖ C Y ) D_{X}\cap(Y\setminus C_{Y})  in the
definition of  D Y D_{Y}  is harmless: those sites are closed even at
 p 2 p_{2}  and cannot initiate an open connection from  Y Y . 
 
 
 Call a path  D X D_{X} -to- D Y D_{Y}  admissible if its endpoints are its only
vertices in  C X ∪ D X ∪ C Y ∪ D Y C_{X}\cup D_{X}\cup C_{Y}\cup D_{Y} , and every internal
vertex is  p 5 / 3 p_{5/3} -open. Let  M M  be the maximum number of pairwise
vertex-disjoint admissible paths. Equivalently, form the locally finite auxiliary
graph whose vertices are  D X ∪ D Y D_{X}\cup D_{Y}  together with the
 p 5 / 3 p_{5/3} -open vertices outside the displayed union, retaining the
edges of  B r ​ ( Λ ) B_{r}(\Lambda) , and give every vertex capacity one. Then
 M M  is the maximum integral  D X D_{X} -to- D Y D_{Y}  flow. It is measurable
outside  C X ∪ D X ∪ C Y ∪ D Y C_{X}\cup D_{X}\cup C_{Y}\cup D_{Y} , and hence is independent of
the exact-cluster event ( 6.31 ). 
 
 
 This restriction loses no connection relevant on the failure event.
Given a path from  C X C_{X}  to the  p 5 / 3 p_{5/3} -open cluster union generated
by  Y Y , take the segment after its last visit to  C X ∪ D X C_{X}\cup D_{X}  and
before its first subsequent visit to  C Y ∪ D Y C_{Y}\cup D_{Y} , and then trim at
the last  D X D_{X} -vertex and first following  D Y D_{Y} -vertex. 
 
 
 The terminal sets are finite because the seed sets and all clusters are
finite in the assumed subcritical interval. The finite-terminal Menger
compactness lemma proved in Section 5 applies even when
 D X ∩ D Y ≠ ∅ D_{X}\cap D_{Y}\neq\varnothing , with each vertex of the intersection
viewed as a zero-length path. It therefore says that if  M ≤ N M\leq N 
there is a vertex separator  S S  of size at most  N N  in the auxiliary
graph. Choose the first such set in a fixed enumeration of the finite
subsets of  V V . In particular, neither existence nor measurability of
 S S  relies on literal stabilization of separators in finite
exhaustions. 
 
 
 The comparison with ( 6.30 ) is made on a fresh label space, not under
the exact-cluster conditioning ( 6.31 ). Give the internal vertices fresh
uniform labels, reveal only their  p 5 / 3 p_{5/3} -states, and leave every
terminal label unrevealed. This produces exactly the law of  M M , since
 M M  ignores all terminal states and, by ( 6.31 ), its internal
coordinates are independent unconditioned uniforms under the original
exact-cluster law. Conditional on this fresh  p 5 / 3 p_{5/3} -configuration
and on  M ≤ N M\leq N , close the deterministically chosen separator  S S  at
level  p 4 / 3 p_{4/3} . An internal separator site is known open at
 p 5 / 3 p_{5/3}  and closes with probability
 ( p 5 / 3 − p 4 / 3 ) / p 5 / 3 (p_{5/3}-p_{4/3})/p_{5/3} ; a terminal separator site has not been
tested on the fresh space and closes with probability  1 − p 4 / 3 1-p_{4/3} .
Thus the conditional cost per site is at least 
 

 
 
 β = min ⁡ { p 5 / 3 − p 4 / 3 p 5 / 3 , 1 − p 4 / 3 } . \beta=\min\left\{\frac{p_{5/3}-p_{4/3}}{p_{5/3}},1-p_{4/3}\right\}. 
 
 
 Closing  S S  destroys every admissible path, hence every wired-endpoint
 p 4 / 3 p_{4/3} -connection between  C X C_{X}  and  C Y C_{Y} . Wiring only the
vertices of  C X ∪ C Y C_{X}\cup C_{Y} , while retaining the site states of every
other vertex, can only make connection more likely. Therefore 
 

 
 
 ℙ p 4 / 3 ( C X ↮ C Y ) ≥ ℙ p 4 / 3 ( C X ↮ C Y  with  C X , C Y  wired ) ≥ β N ℙ ( M ≤ N ) . \mathbb{P}_{p_{4/3}}(C_{X}\not\leftrightarrow C_{Y})\geq\mathbb{P}_{p_{4/3}}(C_{X}\not\leftrightarrow C_{Y}\text{ with }C_{X},C_{Y}\text{ wired})\geq\beta^{N}\mathbb{P}(M\leq N). 
 
 
 The first probability is at most the right side of ( 6.30 ). Since the
law of  M M  on the fresh space is its conditional law under ( 6.31 ),
this gives 
 

 
 
 ℙ ⁡ ( M ≤ N ∣ K x ​ ( p 2 ) = C X , K Y ​ ( p 1 ) = C Y ) ≤ β − N ​ h c 0 ​ δ / 3 . \mathbb{P}(M\leq N\mid K_{x}(p_{2})=C_{X},K_{Y}(p_{1})=C_{Y})\leq\beta^{-N}h^{c_{0}\delta/3}. 
 
 (6.32) 
 
 
 
 If  M ≥ N M\geq N , the first  D X D_{X} -sites of the paths are distinct. A
path of length zero already gives a site in  D X ∩ D Y D_{X}\cap D_{Y} . On every
other path, its last  D Y D_{Y} -site either is known closed at  p 1 p_{1} , in
which case it opens by  p 5 / 3 p_{5/3}  with probability  α 1 \alpha_{1} , or is
untested, in which case it is open with probability
 p 5 / 3 ≥ α 1 p_{5/3}\geq\alpha_{1} , where 
 

 
 
 α 1 = p 5 / 3 − p 1 1 − p 1 . \alpha_{1}=\frac{p_{5/3}-p_{1}}{1-p_{1}}. 
 
 
 Then the first  D X D_{X} -site belongs to
 ∂ V K Y B r ​ ( Λ ) ​ ( p 5 / 3 ) \partial_{V}K_{Y}^{B_{r}(\Lambda)}(p_{5/3}) . If the last site also lies
in  D X D_{X} , it is already in  D X ∩ ∂ V C Y D_{X}\cap\partial_{V}C_{Y}  and is counted
without being opened. The paths are vertex-disjoint and their terminal
labels are independent under ( 6.31 ). Consequently the number of
distinct sites in 
 

 
 
 D X ∩ ∂ V K Y B r ​ ( Λ ) ​ ( p 5 / 3 ) D_{X}\cap\partial_{V}K_{Y}^{B_{r}(\Lambda)}(p_{5/3}) 
 
 
 stochastically dominates  Bin ⁡ ( M , α 1 ) \operatorname{Bin}(M,\alpha_{1}) . 
 
 
 For  0 < a < 1 0<a<1 , put 
 

 
 
 Ψ ⁡ ( a ) = ( 1 − a 2 ) ​ log ⁡ 1 − a / 2 1 − a − a 2 ​ log ⁡ 2 . \Psi(a)=\left(1-\frac{a}{2}\right)\log\frac{1-a/2}{1-a}-\frac{a}{2}\log 2.

 The next proposition applies the ghost-threshold and ghost-gluing
estimates established above; through the ghost-threshold lemma it also
uses the countable-product Russo–Talagrand and site two-ghost estimates.
The site AKN and nearby-cluster estimates are not used in this
proposition. They enter later, in Proposition  7.3 ,
when its pivotal hypothesis is verified. 
 
 
 Proposition 6.2   (Site snowballing) . 
 
 For every  2 ≤ d < ∞ 2\leq d<\infty  and  0 < D < ∞ 0<D<\infty

 τ p 2 B 2 ​ r ​ ( Λ ) ​ ( A 1 , A N ) ≥ s 2 ​ τ p 1 Λ ​ ( A 1 ) ​ τ p 1 Λ ​ ( A N ) . \tau_{p_{2}}^{B_{2r}(\Lambda)}(A_{1},A_{N})\geq s_{2}\tau_{p_{1}}^{\Lambda}(A_{1})\tau_{p_{1}}^{\Lambda}(A_{N}). 
 
 (6.43) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof.  The case  N = 1 N=1  follows by taking  s 2 ≤ 1 s_{2}\leq 1 , so assume

 (6.44)

smallness threshold in Auxiliary Lemma  6.D . Take
independent ghost
fields  𝐆 i \mathbf{G}_{i}  of intensity  h ¯ \bar{h}  on  A i A_{i} . 
 
 
 First use auxiliary ghost fields of intensity  h h . Since

 The endpoint probabilities include the event that each endpoint site is

 7  The unrestricted multiscale induction 

 
 We state the consequence of the induction that is used below. Put 
 

 
 
 Spr ⁡ ( p ; δ ) = 1 − ( 1 − p ) e δ , Gr G ⁡ ( r ) = | B r ​ ( G ) | , \operatorname{Spr}(p;\delta)=1-(1-p)^{e^{\delta}},\qquad\operatorname{Gr}_{G}(r)=|B_{r}(G)|, 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 κ p s ​ ( L , R ) = inf γ ​ finite len ⁡ ( γ ) ≤ L ℙ p ​ ( γ 0 ↔ γ len ⁡ ( γ ) ​  in  ​ B R ​ ( γ ) ) , \kappa_{p}^{\rm s}(L,R)=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\ \text{finite}\\
\operatorname{len}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}_{p}\bigl(\gamma_{0}\leftrightarrow\gamma_{\operatorname{len}(\gamma)}\text{ in }B_{R}(\gamma)\bigr), 
 
 
 where  B R ​ ( γ ) = ⋃ v ∈ γ B R ​ ( v ) B_{R}(\gamma)=\bigcup_{v\in\gamma}B_{R}(v)  and
 B ∞ ​ ( γ ) = V B_{\infty}(\gamma)=V . Also put 
 

 
 
 ℒ ⁡ ( G , D ) = { m ∈ [ 3 , ∞ ) : log ⁡ Gr G ⁡ ( s ) ≤ ( log ⁡ s ) D ​  for every real  ​ s ∈ [ m 1 / 3 , m ] } . \mathscr{L}(G,D)=\left\{m\in[3,\infty):\log\operatorname{Gr}_{G}(s)\leq(\log s)^{D}\text{ for every real }s\in[m^{1/3},m]\right\}. 
 
 
 Thus  ℒ ⁡ ( G , D ) \mathscr{L}(G,D)  is a predicate on real outer scales; all ball
radii continue to use the floor convention fixed in Section 1. For a
real  m ≥ 3 m\geq 3  and  p ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] p\in[0,1] , define 
 

 
 
 b s ( m , p ) = max { r ∈ ℕ :  1 ≤ r ≤ ⌊ 1 8 m 1 / 3 ⌋ , ℙ p ( Piv s [ 4 r , m 1 / 3 ] ) ≤ ( log m ) − 1 } , b_{\rm s}(m,p)=\max\left\{r\in\mathbb{N}:\ 1\leq r\leq\left\lfloor\tfrac{1}{8}m^{1/3}\right\rfloor,\quad\mathbb{P}_{p}(\operatorname{Piv}^{\rm s}[4r,m^{1/3}])\leq(\log m)^{-1}\right\}, 
 
 
 with value zero if the set is empty. For every real  n ≥ 3 n\geq 3 , define 
 

 
 
 Burn s ⁡ ( G , n , p ) = sup m ∈ ℒ ⁡ ( G , 20 ) ( log ⁡ n ) 1 / 2 ≤ m ≤ n ( log ⁡ log ⁡ m min ⁡ { log ⁡ m , log ⁡ Gr G ⁡ ( b s ​ ( m , p ) ) } ) 1 / 4 . \operatorname{Burn}^{\rm s}(G,n,p)=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathscr{L}(G,20)\\
(\log n)^{1/2}\leq m\leq n\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\log\log m}{\min\{\log m,\log\operatorname{Gr}_{G}(b_{\rm s}(m,p))\}}\right)^{1/4}. 
 
 (7.1) 
 
 The displayed logarithms are defined throughout the optimizing set. The
maximum defining  b s ​ ( m , p ) b_{\rm s}(m,p)  is over a finite set of
integer radii and hence is attained whenever it is nonempty; no
attainment of the outer supremum is used. As usual, the supremum over
the empty set is zero and the value is
infinite if some relevant  b s ​ ( m , p ) = 0 b_{\rm s}(m,p)=0 . When
 b s ​ ( m , p ) = 1 b_{\rm s}(m,p)=1 , the denominator contains the positive number
 log ⁡ Gr G ⁡ ( 1 ) \log\operatorname{Gr}_{G}(1) , so the corresponding term is finite. 
 
 
 A tube of thickness  r r  around a finite path  γ \gamma  is
 B r ​ ( γ ) = ⋃ z ∈ γ B r ​ ( z ) B_{r}(\gamma)=\bigcup_{z\in\gamma}B_{r}(z) . A set is an
 ( s , 3 ​ s ) (s,3s) -crossing when it contains a path from  S s S_{s}  to  S 3 ​ s S_{3s} .
We say that  G G  has  ( k , r , ℓ ) (k,r,\ell) -plentiful radial tubes at scale
 s s  if it has at least  k k  paths from  S s S_{s}  to  S 4 ​ s S_{4s} , each of
length at most  ℓ \ell , whose thickness- r r  tubes are pairwise
vertex-disjoint. It has  ( k , r , ℓ ) (k,r,\ell) -plentiful annular tubes at scale
 s s  if, for every two  ( s , 3 ​ s ) (s,3s) -crossings  A , B A,B , there are at least
 k k  paths from  A A  to  B B , each of length at most  ℓ \ell , whose
thickness- r r  tubes are pairwise vertex-disjoint. Finally,
 ( c , λ ) (c,\lambda) -polylog-plentiful means both properties with 
 

 
 
 k = ⌈ ( log s ) c ​ λ ⌉ , r = s ( log s ) − λ / c , ℓ = s ( log s ) λ / c . k=\left\lceil(\log s)^{c\lambda}\right\rceil,\qquad r=s(\log s)^{-\lambda/c},\qquad\ell=s(\log s)^{\lambda/c}. 
 
 (7.2) 
 
 The ceiling makes the required number of paths an integer. This does not
alter any later lower-bound comparison: for an integer-valued path count
 N N , the inequalities  N ≥ x N\geq x  and  N ≥ ⌈ x ⌉ N\geq\lceil x\rceil  are
equivalent. 
 
 
 Lemma 7.0   (Plentiful-tube lemma) . 
 
 For every integer
 d ≥ 2 d\geq 2  and  D ≥ 1 D\geq 1 , there is  c 1 = c 1 ​ ( d , D ) ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) c_{1}=c_{1}(d,D)\in(0,1)  such
that, for every  λ ≥ 1 \lambda\geq 1 , there is
 N geo = N geo ​ ( d , D , λ ) N_{\rm geo}=N_{\rm geo}(d,D,\lambda)  with the following property.
If  G G  is infinite, connected, unimodular, transitive, degree  d d ,
and not one-dimensional (that is,  | B r | ≠ O ⁡ ( r ) |B_{r}|\neq O(r) ), and if the real
scale  n ≥ N geo n\geq N_{\rm geo}  belongs to  ℒ ⁡ ( G , D ) \mathscr{L}(G,D) , then there are
integers  m 1 , m 2 m_{1},m_{2}  such that 
 

 
 
 n 1 / 3 ≤ m 1 , m 1 1 + c 1 ≤ m 2 ≤ n , n^{1/3}\leq m_{1},\qquad m_{1}^{1+c_{1}}\leq m_{2}\leq n, 
 
 (7.3) 
 
 and  G G  has  ( c 1 , λ ) (c_{1},\lambda) -polylog-plentiful radial and
annular tubes at every integer scale in  [ m 1 , m 2 ] [m_{1},m_{2}] . 
 
 
 
 Proof of Lemma  7.0 .  We prove the required
statement from quantitative structure theorems and random-walk estimates.
The fast-tripling argument uses
 [ 10 , Theorem 5.11 and Corollary 5.12] 
for the pointwise and maximal Varopoulos–Carne estimates,
 [ 10 , Theorem 5.13]  for the entropy-increment
inequality,  [ 17 , Lemma 10.46]  for the
growth/isoperimetry inequality, and
 [ 18 , Theorem 2]  for the infinite-measure heat-kernel
estimate. The entropy/coupling, all-time heat-kernel, escape,
distant-ball, crease, and intersection estimates are derived below from
these inputs. The slow and fast tripling cases are separated by the
following condition: 
 

 
 
 Gr ( m ) ≤ e ( log ⁡ m ) D , Gr ( 3 m ) ≥ 3 5 Gr ( m ) ( n 1 − ε ≤ m ≤ n 1 + ε ) . \operatorname{Gr}(m)\leq e^{(\log m)^{D}},\qquad\operatorname{Gr}(3m)\geq 3^{5}\operatorname{Gr}(m)\quad(n^{1-\varepsilon}\leq m\leq n^{1+\varepsilon}). 
 
 
 This is the complement of the slow-tripling alternative. 
 
 
 The proof treats slow and fast tripling separately. In the slow branch,
the radial construction transfers connectors between the two halves of a
bi-infinite geodesic through the root, as detailed in the slow-branch
radial paragraph. In the fast branch, the starting points lie
on a finite geodesic from the root to  S s S_{s} , and independent walks are
run from those points before their ironed trajectories are trimmed between
 S s S_{s}  and  S 4 ​ s S_{4s} ; see ( 7 ). Thus the two
radial constructions use different geometric objects. 
 
 
 Short cycles and exposed spheres. 
We need the following quantitative form of the cutset argument rather than
the one-line invocation in  [ 10 , Lemma 5.8] . Let
 X X  be an infinite, connected, one-ended transitive graph. Suppose that
the binary cycle space of  X X  is generated by cycles of length at most
 ρ \rho . Fix a root  o o , and define the exposed sphere by 
 

 
 
 S q ∞ ​ ( X ) := { z ∈ S q ​ ( o ) : there is a ray  ​ z = z 0 , z 1 , … ​  such that z j ∉ B q ​ ( o ) ​  for every  ​ j ≥ 1 } . S_{q}^{\infty}(X):=\left\{z\in S_{q}(o):\begin{array}[]{l}\text{there is a ray }z=z_{0},z_{1},\ldots\text{ such that}\\[-2.0pt]
z_{j}\notin B_{q}(o)\text{ for every }j\geq 1\end{array}\right\}. 
 
 
 Thus a vertex of  S q ​ ( o ) S_{q}(o)  is exposed precisely when it admits a ray
whose remaining vertices stay outside  B q ​ ( o ) B_{q}(o) . If
 ρ ≤ h ≤ q \rho\leq h\leq q  and
 u , v ∈ S q ∞ ​ ( X ) u,v\in S_{q}^{\infty}(X) , then there is a path from  u u  to  v v , of
length at most 
 

 
 
 5 ​ h ​ | B 3 ​ q ​ ( X ) | | B h ​ ( X ) | , 5h\frac{|B_{3q}(X)|}{|B_{h}(X)|}, 
 
 (7.4) 
 
 contained in the  5 ​ h 5h -neighbourhood of  S q ∞ ​ ( X ) S_{q}^{\infty}(X) . 
 
 
 Here are the details. The exposed sphere  S q ∞ ​ ( X ) S_{q}^{\infty}(X)  is a minimal
vertex separator between the root and infinity. Indeed, every ray has a
last visit to the finite ball  B q B_{q} , and that last vertex belongs to
 S q ∞ S_{q}^{\infty} . Conversely, a geodesic from the root to any
 z ∈ S q ∞ z\in S_{q}^{\infty} , followed by the ray witnessing exposure, meets the
exposed sphere only at  z z . 
 
 
 We spell out the vertex-separator form of the parity argument behind
 [ 24 , Theorem 5.1] . Let
 Π = Π 1 ∪ Π 2 \Pi=\Pi_{1}\cup\Pi_{2}  be a nontrivial partition of a finite minimal
separator. Minimality gives rays  P 1 , P 2 P_{1},P_{2}  from the root to infinity such
that  P j ∩ Π P_{j}\cap\Pi  consists of one vertex of  Π j \Pi_{j} . Take a ball
containing  Π \Pi . Since  X X  is one-ended, sufficiently remote points
of  P 1 P_{1}  and  P 2 P_{2}  can be joined by a finite path outside that ball.
Together with the corresponding initial segments of the two rays, this
gives two finite paths  A , B A,B  with the same endpoints such that  A A 
avoids  Π 2 \Pi_{2}  and  B B  avoids  Π 1 \Pi_{1} . 
 
 
 Write the mod-two cycle  A △ B A\mathbin{\triangle}B  as a sum of generating
cycles. If no generating cycle met both  Π 1 \Pi_{1}  and  Π 2 \Pi_{2} , split the
sum according to whether a generating cycle meets  Π 1 \Pi_{1} , and toggle
all cycles in the first class against  A A . The resulting finite subgraph
has the same two odd vertices as  A A . In its representation using  A A 
it has no vertex in  Π 2 \Pi_{2} ; in its equivalent representation using
 B B  it has no vertex in  Π 1 \Pi_{1} . It therefore contains a finite path
between the common endpoints avoiding all of  Π \Pi . Concatenate this path
with the untouched infinite tail of  P 2 P_{2} . The finite prefix meets that
ray tail in only finitely many vertices. Take its last such intersection,
loop-erase the finite prefix up to that vertex, and append the remaining
subray of  P 2 P_{2} . This gives a ray from the root avoiding  Π \Pi ,
contradicting that  Π \Pi  is a separator.
Thus a generating cycle meets both parts, and the two parts contain
vertices at distance at most  ρ \rho . It follows that every finite minimal
vertex separator, in particular  S q ∞ S_{q}^{\infty} , is  ρ \rho -connected. 
 
 
 Let  T ⊆ S q ∞ T\subseteq S_{q}^{\infty}  be maximal among sets that contain  u u  and
satisfy  d ⁡ ( x , y ) > 2 ​ h d(x,y)>2h  for distinct  x , y ∈ T x,y\in T . Such a set exists because
 S q ∞ S_{q}^{\infty}  is finite. The balls  B h ​ ( x ) B_{h}(x) ,  x ∈ T x\in T , are pairwise
disjoint and lie in  B q + h ​ ( o ) ⊆ B 3 ​ q ​ ( o ) B_{q+h}(o)\subseteq B_{3q}(o) . Transitivity therefore
gives 
 

 
 
 | T | ​ | B h ​ ( X ) | ≤ | B 3 ​ q ​ ( X ) | . |T|\,|B_{h}(X)|\leq|B_{3q}(X)|. 
 
 (7.5) 
 
 By maximality, every point of  S q ∞ S_{q}^{\infty}  is within distance  2 ​ h 2h  of
 T T . 
 
 
 Take a  ρ \rho -chain  z 0 = u , z 1 , … , z N = v z_{0}=u,z_{1},\ldots,z_{N}=v  in
 S q ∞ S_{q}^{\infty} . For each  j j , choose  θ ⁡ ( z j ) ∈ T \theta(z_{j})\in T  within distance
 2 ​ h 2h , taking  θ ⁡ ( u ) = u \theta(u)=u . Consecutive images satisfy 
 

 
 
 d ⁡ ( θ ⁡ ( z j ) , θ ⁡ ( z j + 1 ) ) ≤ 2 ​ h + ρ + 2 ​ h ≤ 5 ​ h . d(\theta(z_{j}),\theta(z_{j+1}))\leq 2h+\rho+2h\leq 5h. 
 
 
 Hence the graph on  T T  that joins two points when their distance in
 X X  is at most  5 ​ h 5h  contains a walk from  u u  to a point of  T T 
within  2 ​ h 2h  of  v v . Loop-erase this walk in the finite graph, join its
successive vertices by geodesics in  X X , and finally join its last vertex
to  v v . Loop-erase the resulting walk in  X X ; this preserves its
endpoints and localization and cannot increase its length. The simple path
in the auxiliary graph uses at most  | T | − 1 |T|-1  edges, so the resulting path
has length at most 
 

 
 
 5 ​ h ​ ( | T | − 1 ) + 2 ​ h ≤ 5 ​ h ​ | T | ≤ 5 ​ h ​ | B 3 ​ q ​ ( X ) | | B h ​ ( X ) | 5h(|T|-1)+2h\leq 5h|T|\leq 5h\frac{|B_{3q}(X)|}{|B_{h}(X)|} 
 
 
 by ( 7.5 ). Every joining geodesic stays within  5 ​ h 5h 
of one of its endpoints in  S q ∞ S_{q}^{\infty} , while the final geodesic has
length at most  2 ​ h 2h . This proves both claims in
( 7.4 ) and completes the short-cycle argument. 
 
 
 We first record the presentation fact used below. If
 F S ↠ Γ F_{S}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma  has kernel  R R , put 
 

 
 
 R r = R ∩ { w : | w | S ≤ r } , N r = ⟨ ⟨ R r ⟩ ⟩ , Γ r = F S / N r . R_{r}=R\cap\{w:|w|_{S}\leq r\},\qquad N_{r}=\langle\!\langle R_{r}\rangle\!\rangle,\qquad\Gamma_{r}=F_{S}/N_{r}. 
 
 
 The quotient  Γ r → Γ \Gamma_{r}\to\Gamma  identifies the rooted Cayley balls of
radius  ⌊ r / 2 ⌋ − 1 \lfloor r/2\rfloor-1 : two words of that length with the same
image differ by a word in  R r R_{r} . 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 7.A   (Quantitative path transfer) . 
 
 There is a universal constant
 Q qi = 100 Q_{\rm qi}=100  with the following property. Let
 ϕ : Y → X \phi:Y\to X  be a  ( 1 , δ ) (1,\delta) -quasi-isometry, where  δ ≥ 1 \delta\geq 1 ,
and choose roots  o Y , o X o_{Y},o_{X}  with
 d X ​ ( ϕ ⁡ ( o Y ) , o X ) ≤ δ d_{X}(\phi(o_{Y}),o_{X})\leq\delta . There is a coarse inverse
 ψ : X → Y \psi:X\to Y , chosen with  ψ ⁡ ( o X ) = o Y \psi(o_{X})=o_{Y} , such that 
 

 
 
 d X ​ ( ϕ ​ ψ ​ ( x ) , x ) ≤ δ , d Y ​ ( ψ ​ ϕ ​ ( y ) , y ) ≤ 2 ​ δ , d_{X}(\phi\psi(x),x)\leq\delta,\qquad d_{Y}(\psi\phi(y),y)\leq 2\delta, 
 
 
 and  ψ \psi  is a  ( 1 , 3 ​ δ ) (1,3\delta) -quasi-isometry. Paths may be transferred
in either direction with the following simultaneous bounds. A path
 η \eta  of length  L L  transfers to a path  η ~ \widetilde{\eta} , with
natural coarse-inverse endpoints, such that 
 

 
 
 len ⁡ ( η ~ ) ≤ Q qi ​ ( L + δ ) , F ⁡ ( B s ​ ( η ~ ) ) ⊆ B s + Q qi ​ δ ​ ( η ) , \operatorname{len}(\widetilde{\eta})\leq Q_{\rm qi}(L+\delta),\qquad F(B_{s}(\widetilde{\eta}))\subseteq B_{s+Q_{\rm qi}\delta}(\eta), 
 
 (7.6) 
 
 where  F F  is the quasi-isometry in the direction of transfer. Either
transferred endpoint may instead be moved to a prescribed vertex whose
image under  F F  is within  25 ​ δ 25\delta  of the corresponding endpoint
of  η \eta , without changing these bounds. In particular, if  η \eta 
lies in
 { z : | d ⁡ ( o , z ) − a | ≤ h } \{z:|d(o,z)-a|\leq h\} , then 
 

 
 
 B s ​ ( η ~ ) ⊆ { z : | d ⁡ ( o , z ) − a | ≤ h + s + Q qi ​ δ } . B_{s}(\widetilde{\eta})\subseteq\{z:|d(o,z)-a|\leq h+s+Q_{\rm qi}\delta\}. 
 
 (7.7) 
 
 If the  ( s + Q qi ​ δ ) (s+Q_{\rm qi}\delta) -tubes about several target paths are
pairwise disjoint, their transferred  s s -tubes are pairwise disjoint. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We first prove the one-direction assertion for a
 ( 1 , ε ) (1,\varepsilon) -quasi-isometry  F : Z → W F:Z\to W , with
 ε ≥ 1 \varepsilon\geq 1 . Sample a target path at successive time gaps
 ⌈ ε ⌉ \lceil\varepsilon\rceil , choose inverse images within
 ε \varepsilon , use the prescribed inverse images at its endpoints, and
join consecutive inverse images by geodesics. Consecutive images in  W W 
are at distance at most  4 ​ ε 4\varepsilon , so consecutive inverse images in
 Z Z  are at distance at most  5 ​ ε 5\varepsilon . There are at most
 L / ε + 1 L/\varepsilon+1  pieces. The resulting path therefore has length at
most  10 ​ ( L + ε ) 10(L+\varepsilon) . Every point of its  s s -tube is within
 5 ​ ε + s 5\varepsilon+s  of a sampled inverse image; applying  F F  and then
moving to the sampled target point gives 
 

 
 
 F ⁡ ( B s ​ ( η ~ ) ) ⊆ B s + 7 ​ ε ​ ( η ) . F(B_{s}(\widetilde{\eta}))\subseteq B_{s+7\varepsilon}(\eta). 
 
 (7.8) 
 
 
 
 Choose  ψ ⁡ ( o X ) = o Y \psi(o_{X})=o_{Y} , and for every other  x x  choose  ψ ⁡ ( x ) \psi(x)  so
that  d X ​ ( ϕ ​ ψ ​ ( x ) , x ) ≤ δ d_{X}(\phi\psi(x),x)\leq\delta . The lower quasi-isometry inequality
gives  d Y ​ ( ψ ​ ϕ ​ ( y ) , y ) ≤ 2 ​ δ d_{Y}(\psi\phi(y),y)\leq 2\delta , and the triangle inequality
shows that  ψ \psi  is a  ( 1 , 3 ​ δ ) (1,3\delta) -quasi-isometry. Apply the
one-direction construction to  ϕ \phi  and to  ψ \psi . Since
 10 ​ ( L + 3 ​ δ ) ≤ 30 ​ ( L + δ ) 10(L+3\delta)\leq 30(L+\delta)  and
 s + 7 ​ ( 3 ​ δ ) ≤ s + 21 ​ δ s+7(3\delta)\leq s+21\delta , the natural-endpoint bounds hold with
constant  30 30 . A prescribed endpoint as in the statement is within
 31 ​ δ 31\delta  of the corresponding natural coarse inverse; append a
geodesic of that length at each end. Its image stays within
 59 ​ δ 59\delta  of the appropriate endpoint of  η \eta . Thus the length and
tube bounds both hold with  Q qi = 100 Q_{\rm qi}=100 , proving
( 7.6 ). Comparing distance from the corresponding roots
costs at most another  3 ​ δ 3\delta , which is still covered by that constant;
this gives ( 7.7 ). Finally, a vertex in two transferred
 s s -tubes would have an image in both enlarged target tubes, proving the
last assertion.
∎

 Auxiliary Lemma 7.C   (Slow-tripling plentiful tubes) . 
 
 For every  d d  and  κ < ∞ \kappa<\infty  there are constants

 | B 3 ​ n ​ ( G ) | ≤ 3 κ ​ | B n ​ ( G ) | , n ≥ n 0 , |B_{3n}(G)|\leq 3^{\kappa}|B_{n}(G)|,\qquad n\geq n_{0},

 Proof. 
 
 Put  K gr = 3 κ K_{\mathrm{gr}}=3^{\kappa} , and apply Auxiliary
Lemma  7.B  to  G G 
with this growth constant. It gives a Cayley graph

of  d , κ d,\kappa , there is a  ( 1 , δ qi ) (1,\delta_{\rm qi}) -quasi-isometry between
