 A := B 2 ​ n ​ ( X ) = S 2 ​ n is a  ​ K 0 ​ -approximate group , K 0 := K gr 3 . A:=B_{2n}(X)=S^{2n}\quad\text{is a }K_{0}\text{-approximate group},\qquad K_{0}:=K_{\mathrm{gr}}^{3}. 
 
 (7.14) 
 
 We spell out how ( 7.14 ) supplies the
hypothesis needed for persistence. By definition there is
 F ⊆ Γ F\subseteq\Gamma ,  | F | ≤ K 0 |F|\leq K_{0} , such that  A 2 ⊆ F ​ A A^{2}\subseteq FA .
Consequently  A 3 ⊆ F 2 ​ A A^{3}\subseteq F^{2}A , and, since  S S  contains the identity, 
 

 
 
 | B 4 ​ n + 1 ​ ( X ) | ≤ | B 6 ​ n ​ ( X ) | = | A 3 | ≤ K 0 2 ​ | A | = K 0 2 ​ | B 2 ​ n ​ ( X ) | . |B_{4n+1}(X)|\leq|B_{6n}(X)|=|A^{3}|\leq K_{0}^{2}|A|=K_{0}^{2}|B_{2n}(X)|. 
 
 (7.15) 
 
 After increasing the fixed threshold for  n n , apply the
persistent-growth theorem
 [ 6 , Theorem 1.1]  at the base radius  2 ​ n 2n  to
( 7.15 ). It gives
 Θ = Θ ⁡ ( d , κ ) \Theta=\Theta(d,\kappa)  such that 
 

 
 
 | B 2 ​ m ​ ( X ) | ≤ Θ ​ | B m ​ ( X ) | ( m ≥ 2 ​ n ) . |B_{2m}(X)|\leq\Theta|B_{m}(X)|\qquad(m\geq 2n). 
 
 (7.16) 
 
 In particular, no ball-ratio comparison with an additive  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  shift is
used to establish ( 7.16 ). 
 
 
 We record the dyadic iteration leading to the growth ratio used below.
For  v ≥ u ≥ 2 ​ n v\geq u\geq 2n , put
 j = ⌈ log 2 ⁡ ( v / u ) ⌉ j=\lceil\log_{2}(v/u)\rceil . Monotonicity and  j j  applications of
( 7.16 ) give 
 

 
 
 | B v ​ ( X ) | ≤ | B 2 j ​ u ​ ( X ) | ≤ Θ j ​ | B u ​ ( X ) | ≤ Θ ​ ( v / u ) log 2 ⁡ Θ ​ | B u ​ ( X ) | . |B_{v}(X)|\leq|B_{2^{j}u}(X)|\leq\Theta^{j}|B_{u}(X)|\leq\Theta(v/u)^{\log_{2}\Theta}|B_{u}(X)|. 
 
 
 Increasing  C = C ⁡ ( d , κ ) C=C(d,\kappa)  therefore yields 
 

 
 
 | B v ​ ( X ) | | B u ​ ( X ) | ≤ C ​ ( v / u ) C ( v ≥ u ≥ 2 ​ n ) . \frac{|B_{v}(X)|}{|B_{u}(X)|}\leq C(v/u)^{C}\qquad(v\geq u\geq 2n). 
 
 (7.17) 
 
 In particular, 
 

 
 
 | B 6 ​ n ​ ( X ) | ≤ | B 8 ​ n ​ ( X ) | ≤ Θ 2 ​ | B 2 ​ n ​ ( X ) | , |B_{6n}(X)|\leq|B_{8n}(X)|\leq\Theta^{2}|B_{2n}(X)|, 
 
 (7.18) 
 
 which is the one-scale hypothesis used in the uniform-presentation step.
The group  Γ \Gamma  is infinite and not virtually cyclic: by the
quasi-isometry, either alternative would force  G G  to have bounded or
linear growth, contrary to the hypotheses. 
 
 
 Apply the uniform-presentation theorem, written in the raw-length notation
above,
 [ 11 , Theorem 1.2] , with the one-scale
growth constant  Θ 2 \Theta^{2}  from
( 7.18 ), base scale  2 ​ n 2n , and the generator
bound supplied by the finitary structure theorem. After one final increase
of the fixed threshold for  n n , it gives a constant
 C rel C_{\mathrm{rel}}  such that at most  C rel C_{\mathrm{rel}}  dyadic numbers
 q ≥ 2 ​ n q\geq 2n  satisfy
 N 2 ​ q ≠ N q N_{2q}\neq N_{q} . Fix an integer  J ≥ 8 J\geq 8 , let
 q 0 q_{0}  be the least dyadic number at least  32 ​ n 32n , and define 
 

 
 
 𝒜 = { q ≥ q 0 : q ​  is dyadic and  ​ N 2 J ​ q ≠ N q } . \mathcal{A}=\{q\geq q_{0}:q\text{ is dyadic and }N_{2^{J}q}\neq N_{q}\}. 
 
 (7.19) 
 
 Every member of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  contains a one-step relation change among
 q , 2 ​ q , … , 2 J − 1 ​ q q,2q,\ldots,2^{J-1}q , and each one-step change is charged at most
 J J  times. Hence  | 𝒜 | ≤ J ​ C rel |\mathcal{A}|\leq JC_{\mathrm{rel}} . 
 
 
 Fix  K ≥ 1 K\geq 1 , take  m ≥ C 0 ​ K ​ n m\geq C_{0}Kn  outside
( 7.13 ), and choose a fixed large numerical
constant  M M . If  𝒜 ∩ [ q 0 , m ] ≠ ∅ \mathcal{A}\cap[q_{0},m]\neq\varnothing , let  a a  be
its largest member and put  b = 2 ​ a b=2a . If this set is empty, put  b = q 0 b=q_{0} .
Choose  C ∗ ≥ 8 ​ M C_{*}\geq 8M  and then  C 0 C_{0}  large enough that in both cases 
 

 
 
 b ≤ m 2 ​ M ​ K . b\leq\frac{m}{2MK}. 
 
 (7.20) 
 
 Indeed, in the first case exclusion of  [ a , C ∗ ​ K ​ a ] [a,C_{*}Ka]  gives
 m > C ∗ ​ K ​ a m>C_{*}Ka , while in the second  q 0 < 64 ​ n q_{0}<64n  and  m ≥ C 0 ​ K ​ n m\geq C_{0}Kn .
This explicitly covers the case  𝒜 = ∅ \mathcal{A}=\varnothing . 
 
 
 There is no member of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  in the dyadic interval  [ b , m ] [b,m] .
Let  q ∗ q_{*}  be the largest dyadic number not exceeding  m m . Successively
using  N q = N 2 J ​ q N_{q}=N_{2^{J}q}  at dyadic  q q  between  b b  and  q ∗ q_{*} , and
using monotonicity of  N r N_{r} , gives 
 

 
 
 N b = N 2 J ​ q ∗ = N 64 ​ m . N_{b}=N_{2^{J}q_{*}}=N_{64m}. 
 
 (7.21) 
 
 More explicitly,  N q = N 2 J ​ q N_{q}=N_{2^{J}q}  makes all the groups  N s N_{s}  equal for
 q ≤ s ≤ 2 J ​ q q\leq s\leq 2^{J}q . Iterating these overlapping intervals from  b b 
first gives  N b = N q ∗ N_{b}=N_{q_{*}} , and the interval starting at  q ∗ q_{*}  then
gives  N b = N 2 J ​ q ∗ N_{b}=N_{2^{J}q_{*}} .
For the last equality, note that  q ∗ > m / 2 q_{*}>m/2  and  J ≥ 8 J\geq 8 , so
 2 J ​ q ∗ > 128 ​ m 2^{J}q_{*}>128m , and sandwich  N 64 ​ m N_{64m}  between the equal endpoint
groups. Thus  X b = Cay ⁡ ( Γ b , S ) X_{b}=\operatorname{Cay}(\Gamma_{b},S)  is literally
 Cay ⁡ ( Γ 64 ​ m , S ) \operatorname{Cay}(\Gamma_{64m},S) , and its rooted  ( 32 ​ m − 1 ) (32m-1) -ball
is identical to that of  X X . This is the required later presentation
scale; no conclusion about  Γ a \Gamma_{a}  is used. 
 
 
 The connector lemma applies to  X b X_{b} . To justify its one-ended hypothesis,
observe that  6 ​ n < 32 ​ m − 1 6n<32m-1 , after increasing  C 0 C_{0} . Hence the common
 ( 32 ​ m − 1 ) (32m-1) -ball transfers ( 7.18 ) to give 
 

 
 
 | B 6 ​ n ​ ( X b ) | ≤ Θ 2 ​ | B 2 ​ n ​ ( X b ) | . |B_{6n}(X_{b})|\leq\Theta^{2}|B_{2n}(X_{b})|. 
 
 
 The metric form of the Breuillard–Green–Tao structure theorem
 [ 5 ] , stated explicitly in
 [ 11 , Theorem 1.1] , gives a finite normal
subgroup  Q ⊲ Γ b Q\triangleleft\Gamma_{b}  such that  Γ b / Q \Gamma_{b}/Q  is virtually
nilpotent. Let  H / Q H/Q  be a nilpotent finite-index subgroup of
 Γ b / Q \Gamma_{b}/Q , where  H H  denotes its preimage in  Γ b \Gamma_{b} . Conjugation
on the finite group  Q Q  has finite image, so  C = C H ​ ( Q ) C=C_{H}(Q)  has finite index
in  H H . Moreover,  C / ( C ∩ Q ) C/(C\cap Q)  is a subgroup of the nilpotent group
 H / Q H/Q , while  C ∩ Q ⊆ Z ⁡ ( C ) C\cap Q\subseteq Z(C) . Thus  C C  is a central extension
of a nilpotent group and is itself nilpotent: if the quotient has class
 c c , then the  ( c + 1 ) (c+1) -st term of the lower central series of  C C  lies
in the central subgroup  C ∩ Q C\cap Q , so the next term is trivial.
Hence  Γ b \Gamma_{b}  is virtually nilpotent and therefore has polynomial
word growth. It is infinite because it
surjects onto the infinite group  Γ \Gamma , and it cannot be virtually
cyclic because a quotient of a virtually cyclic group is finite or virtually
cyclic, whereas  Γ \Gamma  is neither. Since virtually nilpotent groups
have polynomial growth, the exact ends alternative
 [ 8 , Theorem 1.2]  implies that  Γ b \Gamma_{b}  is
one-ended. Indeed, a finitely generated group has zero, one, two, or
infinitely many ends, and the two-ended case is exactly the virtually
cyclic case. In the infinitely-ended case that theorem supplies a
nontrivial amalgam or HNN splitting over a finite subgroup. The normal
form theorem for that splitting gives a free semigroup on two generators,
except in the dihedral index-two/index-two case, which is virtually
cyclic. Thus an infinitely-ended, non-virtually-cyclic finitely generated
group has exponential word growth. Both alternatives contradict the
properties of  Γ b \Gamma_{b} , proving the claim without an appeal to an
unstated virtually-nilpotent corollary.
Moreover, its cycle space is generated by translates of relator cycles of
length at most  b b , by the definition of  N b N_{b} . 
 
 
 We now construct the tubes. Put  h = ⌈ m / ( M ​ K ) ⌉ h=\lceil m/(MK)\rceil . Increasing
 C 0 C_{0}  makes 
 

 
 
 Q qi ​ δ qi ≤ h / 100 , h ≥ 2 ​ n , Q_{\rm qi}\delta_{\rm qi}\leq h/100,\qquad h\geq 2n, 
 
 (7.22) 
 
 and ( 7.20 ) gives  b ≤ h b\leq h . Truncate two
paths crossing from  S m ​ ( G ) S_{m}(G)  to  S 3 ​ m ​ ( G ) S_{3m}(G)  at their first visits to
the outer sphere. Apply Auxiliary Lemma  7.A  to the coarse
inverse  X → G X\to G  to obtain two paths in  X X . The original truncated
paths lie in  B 3 ​ m ​ ( G ) B_{3m}(G) , so ( 7.7 ) puts their transfers in
 B 3 ​ m + Q qi ​ δ qi ​ ( X ) B_{3m+Q_{\rm qi}\delta_{\rm qi}}(X) ; hence the common ball lets us
regard them as paths in  X b X_{b} . Use the prescribed-endpoint clause so
that their endpoints are the  ϕ \phi -images of the original endpoints.
Their initial and terminal radial
coordinates differ from  m m  and  3 ​ m 3m , respectively, by at most
 Q qi ​ δ qi Q_{\rm qi}\delta_{\rm qi} . Consequently, for every integer
 i ∈ [ 6 ​ m / 5 , 13 ​ m / 10 ] i\in[6m/5,13m/10] , each transferred path starts in  B i ​ ( X b ) B_{i}(X_{b})  and
reaches outside  B 2 ​ i + 1 ​ ( X b ) B_{2i+1}(X_{b}) : the two margins are at least
 m / 5 − Q qi ​ δ qi m/5-Q_{\rm qi}\delta_{\rm qi}  and
 2 ​ m / 5 − Q qi ​ δ qi − 1 2m/5-Q_{\rm qi}\delta_{\rm qi}-1 , which are positive by
( 7.22 ) after increasing  C 0 C_{0} . We use the standard
fact that every vertex of an infinite, locally finite, vertex-transitive
graph lies on a bi-infinite geodesic. Indeed, choose a geodesic segment of
length  2 ​ N 2N , use transitivity to map its midpoint to the chosen vertex,
and let  N → ∞ N\to\infty ; local finiteness and a diagonal subsequence give the
required bi-infinite geodesic. Such a path meets
 S i ∞ ​ ( X b ) S_{i}^{\infty}(X_{b}) : take its last exit from  B i B_{i} , continue to its
outer endpoint in  S 2 ​ i + 1 S_{2i+1} , and then continue along one of the two
directions of a bi-infinite geodesic through that endpoint that avoids
 B i B_{i} . At least one direction avoids  B i B_{i} , since otherwise two points
of  B i B_{i}  on the geodesic would be more than  2 ​ i 2i  apart. This is
the standard exposed-sphere argument, included here to fix the localization.
For completeness, the finite suffix of the transferred path may meet the
chosen geodesic direction before its endpoint. Loop-erase that finite
suffix and, among its intersections with the chosen geodesic ray, take
the one farthest along the ray. Concatenate the loop-erased suffix up to
that vertex with the remaining geodesic tail. The resulting path is a
ray: its finite prefix is simple, its geodesic tail meets that prefix only
at the joining vertex, and every vertex after the exposed-sphere point
remains outside  B i B_{i} .
Thus the point obtained at the last exit really belongs to
 S i ∞ ​ ( X b ) S_{i}^{\infty}(X_{b}) . 
 
 
 For each  i i , join the two resulting exposed-sphere points by the path
from ( 7.4 ). The common ball identifies every
object involved with its counterpart in  X X , since the connector lies
within  5 ​ h 5h  of  S i S_{i}  and  i + 5 ​ h < 32 ​ m i+5h<32m . Also, by
( 7.17 ), its length is at most 
 

 
 
 5 ​ h ​ | B 3 ​ i ​ ( X ) | | B h ​ ( X ) | ≤ C ​ h ​ ( i / h ) C ≤ C ​ K C ​ m . 5h\frac{|B_{3i}(X)|}{|B_{h}(X)|}\leq Ch(i/h)^{C}\leq CK^{C}m. 
 
 
 Transfer these connectors back to  G G . To verify the endpoint condition,
let  x i x_{i}  be one of their endpoints on a transferred crossing. The two
endpoint-adjustment geodesics used in the first transfer stay within
 31 ​ δ qi 31\delta_{\rm qi}  of radial levels  m m  or  3 ​ m 3m , so the positive
margins displayed above imply that  x i ∈ S i x_{i}\in S_{i}  lies on the natural
coarse-inverse part of the transferred path. The sharper bound
( 7.8 ) therefore puts its coarse inverse within
 21 ​ δ qi 21\delta_{\rm qi}  of an original crossing vertex  u i u_{i} . Hence
 d X ​ ( x i , ϕ ⁡ ( u i ) ) ≤ 23 ​ δ qi < 25 ​ δ qi d_{X}(x_{i},\phi(u_{i}))\leq 23\delta_{\rm qi}<25\delta_{\rm qi} , so the
prescribed-endpoint clause of the transfer lemma makes the returned
connector end exactly at  u i u_{i} ; the same holds at its other end. 
 
 
 The connector in  X b X_{b}  lies within  5 ​ h 5h  of  S i S_{i} . Equations
( 7.6 )–( 7.22 ) show that the image of the
 h / 10 h/10 -tube about its returned path is contained in the
 ( h / 10 + Q qi ​ δ qi ) (h/10+Q_{\rm qi}\delta_{\rm qi}) -tube about the connector, and hence in 
 

 
 
 { x : | d ⁡ ( o , x ) − i | < 6 ​ h } . \{x:|d(o,x)-i|<6h\}. 
 
 (7.23) 
 
 Thus indices separated by at least  12 ​ h 12h  give disjoint  h / 10 h/10 -tubes.
The interval  [ 6 ​ m / 5 , 13 ​ m / 10 ] [6m/5,13m/10]  contains  m / 10 + O ⁡ ( 1 ) m/10+O(1)  integers, so a greedy
selection leaves at least  c ​ K cK  indices, since
 h ≤ 2 ​ m / ( M ​ K ) h\leq 2m/(MK)  for large  m m . Decreasing  c 0 = c 0 ​ ( d , κ ) > 0 c_{0}=c_{0}(d,\kappa)>0  so
that  c 0 ​ m / K ≤ h / 10 c_{0}m/K\leq h/10 , and applying the length bound in
( 7.6 ), gives  c 0 ​ K c_{0}K  returned connectors with disjoint
 c 0 ​ m / K c_{0}m/K -tubes, endpoints on the prescribed crossings, and length at
most  C 0 ​ K C 0 ​ m C_{0}K^{C_{0}}m . This proves the annular assertion with every
quasi-isometry loss accounted for by  Q qi Q_{\rm qi} . 
 
 
 For the radial assertion, choose in  G G  a bi-infinite geodesic through
the root and take its two opposite portions, each continued to radius
 20 ​ m 20m . Repeat the preceding construction with integers
 i ∈ [ 33 ​ m / 4 , 35 ​ m / 4 ] i\in[33m/4,35m/4] . Equation ( 7.7 ) puts the transferred
portions in the common  32 ​ m 32m -ball, and the same endpoint argument returns
each connector to the two original rays. Choose  z ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) z\in V(G)  at distance
 9 ​ m 9m  on the positive ray. If  u i u_{i}  and  v i v_{i}  are the returned
endpoints on the positive and negative rays, respectively, their radial
coordinates differ from  i i  by at most  Q qi ​ δ qi Q_{\rm qi}\delta_{\rm qi} .
The geodesic identities and ( 7.22 ) therefore give 
 

 
 
 d G ​ ( z , u i ) ≤ 3 ​ m 4 + Q qi ​ δ qi < m , d G ​ ( z , v i ) ≥ 9 ​ m + 33 ​ m 4 − Q qi ​ δ qi > 4 ​ m . d_{G}(z,u_{i})\leq\frac{3m}{4}+Q_{\rm qi}\delta_{\rm qi}<m,\qquad d_{G}(z,v_{i})\geq 9m+\frac{33m}{4}-Q_{\rm qi}\delta_{\rm qi}>4m. 
 
 
 The  h / 10 h/10 -tube and length estimates are exactly those in the annular
case, and selecting  12 ​ h 12h -separated indices again leaves at least  c 0 ​ K c_{0}K 
paths. Apply one automorphism sending  z z  to the root and trim every path
between its first visits to  S m S_{m}  and  S 4 ​ m S_{4m} . Trimming preserves
disjointness and the length bound. This proves the radial assertion and
completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Notice that the proof used only  K ≥ 1 K\geq 1  and  m ≥ C 0 ​ K ​ n m\geq C_{0}Kn , not
 K ≥ n K\geq n . It chose the presentation at the later scale  b b , treated
the empty exceptional-set case, used the growth-ratio bound
( 7.17 ) derived above, obtained tube thickness
 c ​ K − 1 ​ m cK^{-1}m , and retained the factor  m m  in the lifted path length.
These are conclusions of Auxiliary
Lemma  7.C . 
 
 
 We now derive the fast-tripling branch. We first prove the coupling
estimate used in its construction. 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 7.D   (Entropy and coupling from growth) . 
 
 For every integer  d ≥ 2 d\geq 2  there is  C = C ⁡ ( d ) < ∞ C=C(d)<\infty  such that the
following holds. Let  G G  be an infinite transitive graph of degree  d d ,
let  X X  be lazy random walk, and write  H t H_{t}  for the Shannon entropy
of  X t X_{t} . For every integer  t ≥ 4 t\geq 4 , with
 n = ⌊ t 1 / 2 ⌋ n=\lfloor t^{1/2}\rfloor , 
 

 
 
 H t ≤ C ​ [ log ⁡ Gr ⁡ ( n ) ] 2 , H_{t}\leq C[\log\operatorname{Gr}(n)]^{2}, 
 
 (7.24) 
 
 and, for all vertices  x , y x,y , 
 

 
 
 ‖ 𝐏 x ( X t ∈ ⋅ ) − 𝐏 y ( X t ∈ ⋅ ) ‖ TV ≤ C log ⁡ Gr ⁡ ( n ) t 1 / 2 d ( x , y ) . \left\|\mathbf{P}_{x}(X_{t}\in\cdot)-\mathbf{P}_{y}(X_{t}\in\cdot)\right\|_{\rm TV}\leq C\frac{\log\operatorname{Gr}(n)}{t^{1/2}}d(x,y). 
 
 (7.25) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The maximal Varopoulos–Carne estimate
 [ 10 , Theorem 5.11 and Corollary 5.12] , entropy
conditioning on  { X t ∈ B r } \{X_{t}\in B_{r}\} , and submultiplicativity give, whenever
 r = q ​ n r=qn  with  q q  a positive integer, 
 

 
 
 H t ≤ log ⁡ 2 + q ​ log ​ Gr ⁡ ( n ) + 2 ​ d ​ ( t + 1 ) 2 ​ exp ​ { q ​ log ​ Gr ⁡ ( n ) − q 2 ​ n 2 2 ​ t } . H_{t}\leq\log 2+q\log\operatorname{Gr}(n)+2d(t+1)^{2}\exp\left\{q\log\operatorname{Gr}(n)-\frac{q^{2}n^{2}}{2t}\right\}. 
 
 (7.26) 
 
 Indeed, the walk is supported on  B t B_{t} , so its conditional entropy
outside  B r B_{r}  is at most
 log ⁡ Gr ⁡ ( t ) ≤ ( t + 1 ) ​ log ⁡ d \log\operatorname{Gr}(t)\leq(t+1)\log d . The maximal estimate bounds
the probability of that event by
 2 ( t + 1 ) Gr ( r ) e − r 2 / ( 2 t ) 2(t+1)\operatorname{Gr}(r)e^{-r^{2}/(2t)} , and
 Gr ⁡ ( r ) ≤ Gr ⁡ ( n ) q \operatorname{Gr}(r)\leq\operatorname{Gr}(n)^{q} . These are exactly the
three terms in ( 7.26 ). 
 
 
 Since  n 2 / t ≥ 1 / 4 n^{2}/t\geq 1/4 , take 
 

 
 
 q = ⌈ C 0 ​ log ⁡ [ t ​ Gr ⁡ ( n ) ] ⌉ q=\left\lceil C_{0}\log[t\operatorname{Gr}(n)]\right\rceil 
 
 
 with  C 0 = C 0 ​ ( d ) C_{0}=C_{0}(d)  sufficiently large. The graph is infinite, so
 Gr ⁡ ( n ) ≥ n + 1 \operatorname{Gr}(n)\geq n+1 , whence
 log ⁡ [ t ​ Gr ⁡ ( n ) ] ≤ 5 ​ log ⁡ Gr ⁡ ( n ) \log[t\operatorname{Gr}(n)]\leq 5\log\operatorname{Gr}(n)  for
 t ≥ 4 t\geq 4 . The first two terms in ( 7.26 ) are therefore
 O d ​ ( [ log ⁡ Gr ⁡ ( n ) ] 2 ) O_{d}([\log\operatorname{Gr}(n)]^{2}) , and the exponent in its last term
is at most 
 

 
 
 q ​ log ⁡ Gr ⁡ ( n ) − q 2 / 8 ≤ − 3 ​ log ⁡ ( t + 1 ) − log ⁡ ( 2 ​ d ) q\log\operatorname{Gr}(n)-q^{2}/8\leq-3\log(t+1)-\log(2d) 
 
 
 after increasing  C 0 ​ ( d ) C_{0}(d) , uniformly in  t ≥ 4 t\geq 4 . (The negative
quadratic term dominates both linear logarithmic terms; the ceiling in
 q q  is absorbed by the same choice of  C 0 C_{0} .) Hence the last term in
( 7.26 ) is bounded by an absolute constant, which proves
( 7.24 ). Notice that the radius used
here is  r = n ​ q r=nq ; the factor  n n  is part of the definition. 
 
 
 We next derive ( 7.25 ). The entropy-increment
inequality  [ 10 , Theorem 5.13]  states that 
 

 
 
 1 d ∑ z ∼ o ∥ 𝐏 o ( X k ∈ ⋅ ) − 𝐏 z ( X k − 1 ∈ ⋅ ) ∥ TV 2 ≤ H k − H k − 1 . \frac{1}{d}\sum_{z\sim o}\|\mathbf{P}_{o}(X_{k}\in\cdot)-\mathbf{P}_{z}(X_{k-1}\in\cdot)\|_{\rm TV}^{2}\leq H_{k}-H_{k-1}. 
 
 
 The entropy increments are nonnegative, and their sum for
 ⌊ t / 2 ⌋ < k ≤ t \lfloor t/2\rfloor<k\leq t  is at most  H t H_{t} . Hence some such  k k 
satisfies  H k − H k − 1 ≤ 3 ​ H t / t H_{k}-H_{k-1}\leq 3H_{t}/t . The total-variation distance between
the laws at times  k − 1 k-1  and  k k , from the same starting vertex, is
at most  C k − 1 / 2 Ck^{-1/2} : condition on the number of non-lazy steps and use
the elementary  O ( k − 1 / 2 ) O(k^{-1/2})  total-variation bound between
 Bin ⁡ ( k − 1 , 1 / 2 ) \operatorname{Bin}(k-1,1/2)  and  Bin ⁡ ( k , 1 / 2 ) \operatorname{Bin}(k,1/2) .
Thus, for adjacent  x , y x,y , transitivity and the triangle inequality give 
 

 
 
 ∥ 𝐏 x ( X k ∈ ⋅ ) − 𝐏 y ( X k ∈ ⋅ ) ∥ TV ≤ C d H t / t + C t − 1 / 2 . \|\mathbf{P}_{x}(X_{k}\in\cdot)-\mathbf{P}_{y}(X_{k}\in\cdot)\|_{\rm TV}\leq C_{d}\sqrt{H_{t}/t}+Ct^{-1/2}. 
 
 
 Applying the common transition kernel for another  t − k t-k  steps can only
decrease total variation. Substitute ( 7.24 ), and
then sum along a geodesic from  x x  to  y y . This proves
( 7.25 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 7.E   (All-time lazy heat-kernel bound) . 
 
 Let  G G  be an infinite unimodular transitive graph of degree  d d ,
let  P P  be the transition kernel of lazy simple random walk, and write
 p n rw ​ ( u , v ) = P n ​ ( u , v ) p_{n}^{\rm rw}(u,v)=P^{n}(u,v) . There are constants
 c hk = c hk ​ ( d ) ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) c_{\rm hk}=c_{\rm hk}(d)\in(0,1)  and
 C hk = C hk ​ ( d ) < ∞ C_{\rm hk}=C_{\rm hk}(d)<\infty  such that, for every integer
 t ≥ 4 t\geq 4  and every  u , v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) u,v\in V(G) , 
 

 
 
 p t rw ​ ( u , v ) ≤ C hk Gr ⁡ ( ρ t ) , ρ t := max ⁡ { 1 , ⌊ c hk ​ t 1 / 2 log ⁡ Gr ⁡ ( ⌊ t 1 / 2 ⌋ ) ⌋ } . p_{t}^{\rm rw}(u,v)\leq\frac{C_{\rm hk}}{\operatorname{Gr}(\rho_{t})},\qquad\rho_{t}:=\max\left\{1,\left\lfloor\frac{c_{\rm hk}t^{1/2}}{\sqrt{\log\operatorname{Gr}(\lfloor t^{1/2}\rfloor)}}\right\rfloor\right\}. 
 
 (7.27) 
 
 Moreover, for every  m ≥ 0 m\geq 0  and  u , v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) u,v\in V(G) , 
 

 
 
 p 2 ​ m + 1 rw ​ ( u , v ) = 1 2 ​ p 2 ​ m rw ​ ( u , v ) + 1 2 ​ d ​ ∑ v ′ ∼ v p 2 ​ m rw ​ ( u , v ′ ) ≤ max w ∈ { v } ∪ N ⁡ ( v ) ⁡ p 2 ​ m rw ​ ( u , w ) . p_{2m+1}^{\rm rw}(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}p_{2m}^{\rm rw}(u,v)+\frac{1}{2d}\sum_{v^{\prime}\sim v}p_{2m}^{\rm rw}(u,v^{\prime})\leq\max_{w\in\{v\}\cup N(v)}p_{2m}^{\rm rw}(u,w). 
 
 (7.28) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We first derive the even-time bound from published inputs. Let 
 

 
 
 Gr − 1 ⁡ ( s ) := inf { r ∈ ℕ : Gr ⁡ ( r ) ≥ s } , \operatorname{Gr}^{-1}(s):=\inf\{r\in\mathbb{N}:\operatorname{Gr}(r)\geq s\}, 
 
 
 and let  Φ ⁡ ( s ) \Phi(s)  be the conductance profile of lazy simple random walk
with respect to counting measure. The growth/isoperimetry inequality
 [ 17 , Lemma 10.46] , with the normalization of the lazy
kernel absorbed into a degree-dependent constant, gives 
 

 
 
 Φ ⁡ ( s ) ≥ c d Gr − 1 ⁡ ( 2 ​ s ) ( s ≥ 1 ) . \Phi(s)\geq\frac{c_{d}}{\operatorname{Gr}^{-1}(2s)}\qquad(s\geq 1). 
 
 (7.29) 
 
 The infinite-stationary-measure case of the evolving-set estimate
 [ 18 , Theorem 2] , applied with counting measure and
holding probability  1 / 2 1/2 , implies that there are
 c d , C d > 0 c_{d},C_{d}>0  such that 
 

 
 
 p 2 ​ m rw ( u , v ) ≤ C d sup { y ≥ 1 : ∫ 1 y d ​ x x ​ Φ ​ ( 4 ​ x ) 2 ≤ c d ​ m } . p_{2m}^{\rm rw}(u,v)\leq\frac{C_{d}}{\displaystyle\sup\left\{y\geq 1:\int_{1}^{y}\frac{dx}{x\Phi(4x)^{2}}\leq c_{d}m\right\}}. 
 
 (7.30) 
 
 Here transitivity makes  G G  regular, so counting measure is stationary;
connectedness gives irreducibility, and the lazy kernel has holding
probability  1 / 2 1/2 . In the notation of that theorem  π ≡ 1 \pi\equiv 1 , and choosing
its error parameter to be  1 / y 1/y  changes its integral from
 [ 4 , 4 ​ y ] [4,4y]  to  [ 1 , y ] [1,y] ; the factor  4 4 , the harmless extra unit of
time, and the passage from time  m m  to  2 ​ m 2m  are absorbed in
 c d , C d c_{d},C_{d} . 
 
 
 By ( 7.29 ), for every  y ≥ 1 y\geq 1 , 
 

 
 
 ∫ 1 y d ​ x x ​ Φ ​ ( 4 ​ x ) 2 ≤ C d ​ [ Gr − 1 ⁡ ( 8 ​ y ) ] 2 ​ log ⁡ y . \int_{1}^{y}\frac{dx}{x\Phi(4x)^{2}}\leq C_{d}[\operatorname{Gr}^{-1}(8y)]^{2}\log y. 
 
 (7.31) 
 
 Put  R = ⌊ m 1 / 2 ⌋ R=\lfloor m^{1/2}\rfloor ,
 L = log ⁡ Gr ⁡ ( R ) L=\log\operatorname{Gr}(R) , and 
 

 
 
 r = max { 1 , ⌊ c d ′ m 1 / 2 L − 1 / 2 ⌋ } , r=\max\left\{1,\left\lfloor c^{\prime}_{d}m^{1/2}L^{-1/2}\right\rfloor\right\}, 
 
 
 where  c d ′ > 0 c^{\prime}_{d}>0  will be chosen sufficiently small. If
 Gr ⁡ ( r ) < 8 \operatorname{Gr}(r)<8 , then
 C d / Gr ⁡ ( r ) ≥ 1 C_{d}/\operatorname{Gr}(r)\geq 1  after enlarging  C d C_{d} , so the
desired estimate follows from  p 2 ​ m rw ≤ 1 p_{2m}^{\rm rw}\leq 1 . Otherwise take
 y = Gr ⁡ ( r ) / 8 ≥ 1 y=\operatorname{Gr}(r)/8\geq 1 . After decreasing  c d ′ c^{\prime}_{d} , and
enlarging the final constant to cover the finitely many bounded values
of  m m , we have  r ≤ R r\leq R . Hence 
 

 
 
 Gr − 1 ⁡ ( 8 ​ y ) ≤ r , log ⁡ y ≤ L . \operatorname{Gr}^{-1}(8y)\leq r,\qquad\log y\leq L. 
 
 
 Consequently ( 7.31 ) is at most
 C d ​ r 2 ​ L ≤ C d ​ ( c d ′ ) 2 ​ m C_{d}r^{2}L\leq C_{d}(c^{\prime}_{d})^{2}m . Choose  c d ′ c^{\prime}_{d}  so that this is at most
the threshold in ( 7.30 ). That estimate,
with this value of  y y , yields 
 

 
 
 p 2 ​ m rw ​ ( u , v ) ≤ C d Gr ⁡ ( r ) . p_{2m}^{\rm rw}(u,v)\leq\frac{C_{d}}{\operatorname{Gr}(r)}. 
 
 (7.32) 
 
 After decreasing  c hk c_{\rm hk} , the radius  ρ 2 ​ m \rho_{2m}  in
( 7.27 ) is at most  r r : the factor
 2 \sqrt{2}  from the time change is absorbed in  c hk c_{\rm hk} , while
 log ⁡ Gr ⁡ ( ⌊ 2 ​ m ⌋ ) ≥ L \log\operatorname{Gr}(\lfloor\sqrt{2m}\rfloor)\geq L .
Thus ( 7.32 ) proves
( 7.27 ) at every even time, after changing
the degree-dependent constants to cover the finitely many small values
of  m m . 
 
 
 The lazy transition probabilities into  v v  are
 P ⁡ ( v , v ) = 1 / 2 P(v,v)=1/2 ,  P ⁡ ( v ′ , v ) = 1 / ( 2 ​ d ) P(v^{\prime},v)=1/(2d)  for  v ′ ∼ v v^{\prime}\sim v , and zero otherwise.
Chapman–Kolmogorov therefore gives the equality in
( 7.28 ); its right side is a convex combination,
which gives the inequality. The even-time bound just proved is uniform in both
the starting and terminal vertices. Hence every term in the maximum in
( 7.28 ) satisfies that same bound. Since  2 ​ m 2m 
and  2 ​ m + 1 2m+1  are comparable for  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 , changing
 c hk c_{\rm hk}  and  C hk C_{\rm hk}  absorbs the change of time and the
integer parts in  ρ t \rho_{t} . The finitely many smaller times follow from
 p t rw ≤ 1 p_{t}^{\rm rw}\leq 1 , after one further enlargement of
 C hk C_{\rm hk} . This proves ( 7.27 ) at
all times and records the corrected odd-time calculation explicitly.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 7.F   (Scale-dependent escape) . 
 
 For every integer  d ≥ 2 d\geq 2  and real  κ ≥ 1 \kappa\geq 1 , there is
 C = C ⁡ ( d , κ ) < ∞ C=C(d,\kappa)<\infty  such that the following holds. Let  G G  be an
infinite connected unimodular transitive graph of degree  d d , and let
 n , t ≥ 1 n,t\geq 1  be integers satisfying 
 

 
 
 Gr ⁡ ( 3 ​ m ) ≥ 3 κ ​ Gr ⁡ ( m ) for every integer  ​ n ≤ m ≤ 1 2 ​ t 1 / 2 . \operatorname{Gr}(3m)\geq 3^{\kappa}\operatorname{Gr}(m)\quad\text{for every integer }n\leq m\leq\tfrac{1}{2}t^{1/2}. 
 
 (7.33) 
 
 Then, for all  u , v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) u,v\in V(G) , 
 

 
 
 𝐏 u ​ ( X t ∈ B n ​ ( v ) ) ≤ C ​ [ log ⁡ max ⁡ { t n 2 , Gr ⁡ ( n ) } ] κ ​ ( n 2 t ) κ / 2 . \mathbf{P}_{u}(X_{t}\in B_{n}(v))\leq C\left[\log\max\left\{\frac{t}{n^{2}},\operatorname{Gr}(n)\right\}\right]^{\kappa}\left(\frac{n^{2}}{t}\right)^{\kappa/2}. 
 
 (7.34) 
 
 An empty interval in ( 7.33 ) imposes no
condition. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We give the complete deduction from Auxiliary
Lemma  7.E . For a fixed set  A A , the
Markov property gives 
 

 
 
 sup x 𝐏 x ​ ( X j + 1 ∈ A ) ≤ sup y 𝐏 y ​ ( X j ∈ A ) , \sup_{x}\mathbf{P}_{x}(X_{j+1}\in A)\leq\sup_{y}\mathbf{P}_{y}(X_{j}\in A), 
 
 
 so the supremum is nonincreasing in time. If  t < 16 ​ n 2 t<16n^{2} , the right
side of ( 7.34 ) is bounded below by a positive constant
depending only on  κ \kappa , and the assertion follows after enlarging
 C C . Otherwise put  T = ⌊ t / 4 ⌋ T=\lfloor t/4\rfloor . Then
 T ≍ t T\asymp t ,  T ≥ 3 ​ n 2 T\geq 3n^{2} , and
 T 1 / 2 ≤ t 1 / 2 / 2 T^{1/2}\leq t^{1/2}/2 . It is therefore enough to prove the claimed
bound at time  T T , under the stronger-range hypothesis 
 

 
 
 Gr ⁡ ( 3 ​ m ) ≥ 3 κ ​ Gr ⁡ ( m ) ( n ≤ m ≤ T 1 / 2 ) , \operatorname{Gr}(3m)\geq 3^{\kappa}\operatorname{Gr}(m)\quad(n\leq m\leq T^{1/2}), 
 
 (7.35) 
 
 because the preceding monotonicity bounds the probability at time  t t 
by the supremum at time  T T , and replacing  T T  by  t t  in the
right side changes only the constant. We now write  T T  for this
reduced time. 
 
 
 Put 
 

 
 
 R = ⌊ T 1 / 2 ⌋ , L = log Gr ( R ) , ρ = max { 1 , ⌊ c hk T 1 / 2 L − 1 / 2 ⌋ } . R=\lfloor T^{1/2}\rfloor,\qquad L=\log\operatorname{Gr}(R),\qquad\rho=\max\left\{1,\left\lfloor c_{\rm hk}T^{1/2}L^{-1/2}\right\rfloor\right\}. 
 
 
 By submultiplicativity of volume growth,
 Gr ⁡ ( a + b ) ≤ Gr ⁡ ( a ) ​ Gr ⁡ ( b ) \operatorname{Gr}(a+b)\leq\operatorname{Gr}(a)\operatorname{Gr}(b) , 
 

 
 
 Gr ⁡ ( R ) ≤ Gr ⁡ ( ρ ) ⌈ R / ρ ⌉ . \operatorname{Gr}(R)\leq\operatorname{Gr}(\rho)^{\lceil R/\rho\rceil}. 
 
 
 Since  L ≤ ( R + 1 ) ​ log ⁡ ( d + 1 ) L\leq(R+1)\log(d+1) , the quantity before the floor in the
definition of  ρ \rho  is at least two once
 T ≥ T 0 ​ ( d ) T\geq T_{0}(d) . For these times,
 ⌈ R / ρ ⌉ ≤ C d ​ L \lceil R/\rho\rceil\leq C_{d}\sqrt{L} , and hence 
 

 
 
 Gr ⁡ ( ρ ) ≥ exp ⁡ ( c d ​ L ) . \operatorname{Gr}(\rho)\geq\exp(c_{d}\sqrt{L}). 
 
 (7.36) 
 
 The finitely many times below  T 0 ​ ( d ) T_{0}(d)  are covered by enlarging
 C ⁡ ( d , κ ) C(d,\kappa) . 
 
 
 Set 
 

 
 
 x = T 1 / 2 n , A = Gr ⁡ ( n ) , B = log ⁡ max ⁡ { x 2 , A } . x=\frac{T^{1/2}}{n},\qquad A=\operatorname{Gr}(n),\qquad B=\log\max\{x^{2},A\}. 
 
 
 Thus  x ≥ 1 x\geq 1  and  B ≥ log ⁡ 2 B\geq\log 2 . If  ρ < n \rho<n , the definition of
 ρ \rho  gives  L ≥ c d ​ x \sqrt{L}\geq c_{d}x . From
( 7.27 ) and
( 7.36 ), followed by a union bound over
 B n ​ ( v ) B_{n}(v) , 
 

 
 
 𝐏 u ​ ( X T ∈ B n ​ ( v ) ) ≤ C d ​ A ​ e − c d ​ x . \mathbf{P}_{u}(X_{T}\in B_{n}(v))\leq C_{d}Ae^{-c_{d}x}. 
 
 (7.37) 
 
 If  log ⁡ A ≥ c d ​ x / 2 \log A\geq c_{d}x/2 , then  B κ ​ x − κ B^{\kappa}x^{-\kappa}  is bounded
below by a positive  ( d , κ ) (d,\kappa) -constant. If
 log ⁡ A < c d ​ x / 2 \log A<c_{d}x/2 , the right side of
( 7.37 ) is at most
 C d e − c d x / 2 ≤ C d , κ B κ x − κ C_{d}e^{-c_{d}x/2}\leq C_{d,\kappa}B^{\kappa}x^{-\kappa} .
This proves ( 7.34 ) in this case. 
 
 
 Suppose instead that  ρ ≥ n \rho\geq n . Iterating
( 7.35 ) at the scales
 n , 3 ​ n , … , 3 ⌊ log 3 ⁡ ( ρ / n ) ⌋ ​ n n,3n,\ldots,3^{\lfloor\log_{3}(\rho/n)\rfloor}n  gives 
 

 
 
 Gr ⁡ ( ρ ) ≥ ( ρ 3 ​ n ) κ ​ A . \operatorname{Gr}(\rho)\geq\left(\frac{\rho}{3n}\right)^{\kappa}A. 
 
 (7.38) 
 
 The heat-kernel bound and a union bound therefore give both 
 

 
 
 𝐏 u ​ ( X T ∈ B n ​ ( v ) ) ≤ C d , κ ​ x − κ ​ L κ / 2 and 𝐏 u ​ ( X T ∈ B n ​ ( v ) ) ≤ C d ​ A ​ e − c d ​ L . \mathbf{P}_{u}(X_{T}\in B_{n}(v))\leq C_{d,\kappa}x^{-\kappa}L^{\kappa/2}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{P}_{u}(X_{T}\in B_{n}(v))\leq C_{d}Ae^{-c_{d}\sqrt{L}}. 
 
 (7.39) 
 
 Here the floor in  ρ \rho  changes only the displayed constants, by the
same lower bound on the unfloored radius used above. 
 
 
 Choose  K = K ⁡ ( d , κ ) K=K(d,\kappa)  so that  c d ​ K ≥ 2 ​ κ + 2 c_{d}K\geq 2\kappa+2 . If
 L ≤ K ​ B \sqrt{L}\leq KB , the first bound in
( 7.39 ) is at most
 C d , κ ​ x − κ ​ B κ C_{d,\kappa}x^{-\kappa}B^{\kappa} . If  L > K ​ B \sqrt{L}>KB , the second is
at most 
 

 
 
 C d ​ exp ⁡ { B − c d ​ K ​ B } ≤ C d , κ ​ x − κ ​ B κ , C_{d}\exp\{B-c_{d}KB\}\leq C_{d,\kappa}x^{-\kappa}B^{\kappa}, 
 
 
 where we used  A ≤ e B A\leq e^{B} ,  B ≥ 2 ​ log ⁡ x B\geq 2\log x , and
 B ≥ log ⁡ 2 B\geq\log 2 . Thus in every case 
 

 
 
 𝐏 u ​ ( X T ∈ B n ​ ( v ) ) ≤ C d , κ ​ B κ ​ x − κ , \mathbf{P}_{u}(X_{T}\in B_{n}(v))\leq C_{d,\kappa}B^{\kappa}x^{-\kappa}, 
 
 
 which is ( 7.34 ) at the reduced time  T T . The initial
time reduction completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 For  t ≥ n 2 t\geq n^{2} , suppose 
 

 
 
 Gr ⁡ ( 3 ​ m ) ≥ 3 κ ​ Gr ⁡ ( m ) ( r ≤ m ≤ t 1 / 2 ) \operatorname{Gr}(3m)\geq 3^{\kappa}\operatorname{Gr}(m)\quad(r\leq m\leq t^{1/2}) 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 sup x , y ∈ B 3 ​ n ∥ 𝐏 x ( X t ∈ ⋅ ) − 𝐏 y ( X t ∈ ⋅ ) ∥ TV ≤ 1 4 . \sup_{x,y\in B_{3n}}\|\mathbf{P}_{x}(X_{t}\in\cdot)-\mathbf{P}_{y}(X_{t}\in\cdot)\|_{\rm TV}\leq\tfrac{1}{4}. 
 
 
 The growth condition ( 7 ) enters through the
local escape, distant-ball, and intersection lemmas proved here. Each
use stops at the scale  t 1 / 2 t^{1/2} , as required by
( 7 ). 
 
 
 We next define the ironing operation and prove the crease estimate used
below. The parameter  a a  is the maximum permitted duration of a short
inter-crease interval. In the application below we choose 
 

 
 
 a = ⌊ r 2 16 ​ log ⁡ max ​ { t , Gr ⁡ ( r ) } ⌋ . a=\left\lfloor\frac{r^{2}}{16\log\max\{t,\operatorname{Gr}(r)\}}\right\rfloor. 
 
 
 For a finite path
 γ = ( γ 0 , … , γ t ) \gamma=(\gamma_{0},\ldots,\gamma_{t})  and an integer  r ≥ 1 r\geq 1 , put
 τ 0 = 0 \tau_{0}=0 . If  τ j < t \tau_{j}<t  and the remaining path leaves
 B r − 1 ​ ( γ τ j ) B_{r-1}(\gamma_{\tau_{j}}) , let  τ j + 1 \tau_{j+1}  be its first exit time;
otherwise put  τ j + 1 = t \tau_{j+1}=t . Stop at the first index  N N  for which
 τ N = t \tau_{N}=t . Write  cr r ⁡ ( γ ) = N \operatorname{cr}_{r}(\gamma)=N , and let
 iron r ⁡ ( γ ) \operatorname{iron}_{r}(\gamma)  be the concatenation of the fixed
geodesics between consecutive crease points. Every nonterminal geodesic
has length exactly  r r , and the terminal one has length at most  r r , so 
 

 
 
 len ⁡ ( iron r ⁡ ( γ ) ) ≤ r ​ cr r ⁡ ( γ ) . \operatorname{len}(\operatorname{iron}_{r}(\gamma))\leq r\operatorname{cr}_{r}(\gamma). 
 
 (7.40) 
 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 7.G   (Crease estimate) . 
 
 Let  X X  be lazy random walk on a
locally finite transitive graph, started at any vertex. For all integers
 r , t , a ≥ 1 r,t,a\geq 1 , 
 

 
 
 𝐏 ⁡ ( cr r ⁡ ( X t ) > 1 + t a ) ≤ 2 ​ ( t + 1 ) ​ ( a + 1 ) ​ Gr ⁡ ( r ) ​ exp ⁡ ( − r 2 2 ​ a ) . \mathbf{P}\left(\operatorname{cr}_{r}(X^{t})>1+\frac{t}{a}\right)\leq 2(t+1)(a+1)\operatorname{Gr}(r)\exp\left(-\frac{r^{2}}{2a}\right). 
 
 (7.41) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 If  N = cr r ⁡ ( X t ) > 1 + t / a N=\operatorname{cr}_{r}(X^{t})>1+t/a , then the
 N − 1 N-1  nonterminal crease intervals have total length at most  t t , so
one has length at most  a a . Consequently, for some  0 ≤ j ≤ t 0\leq j\leq t ,
the walk travels distance at least  r r  from  X j X_{j}  during the next
 a a  steps. Conditional on  X j X_{j} , the Varopoulos–Carne maximal bound
 [ 10 , Corollary 5.12]  bounds this probability by
 2 ( a + 1 ) Gr ( r ) e − r 2 / ( 2 a ) 2(a+1)\operatorname{Gr}(r)e^{-r^{2}/(2a)} , uniformly in  j j .
A union bound over  j j  proves ( 7.41 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 7.H   (Distant-ball estimate) . 
 
 For every integer  d ≥ 2 d\geq 2  and real  κ > 2 \kappa>2 , there is
 C = C ⁡ ( d , κ ) < ∞ C=C(d,\kappa)<\infty  such that the following holds. Let  G G  be an
infinite connected unimodular transitive graph of degree  d d . Suppose
that  s , T ≥ 1 s,T\geq 1  are integers, 
 

 
 
 T ≥ s 2 / 4 , Gr ( 3 j ) ≥ 3 κ Gr ( j ) ( s ≤ j ≤ T 1 / 2 ) . T\geq s^{2}/4,\qquad\operatorname{Gr}(3j)\geq 3^{\kappa}\operatorname{Gr}(j)\quad(s\leq j\leq T^{1/2}). 
 
 
 For vertices  x , w x,w  satisfying  d ⁡ ( x , w ) ≥ 2 ​ s d(x,w)\geq 2s , 
 

 
 
 𝐏 x ​ ( hit  ​ B s ​ ( w ) ​  by time  ​ T ) ≤ C ​ [ log ⁡ max ⁡ { d ⁡ ( x , w ) , Gr ⁡ ( 2 ​ s ) } ] ( 3 ​ κ + 2 ) / 2 ​ ( s d ⁡ ( x , w ) ) κ − 2 . \mathbf{P}_{x}(\text{hit }B_{s}(w)\text{ by time }T)\leq C\left[\log\max\{d(x,w),\operatorname{Gr}(2s)\}\right]^{(3\kappa+2)/2}\left(\frac{s}{d(x,w)}\right)^{\kappa-2}. 
 
 (7.42) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We derive the estimate from the pointwise and maximal
Varopoulos–Carne bounds and Auxiliary Lemma  7.F . Put
 D 0 = d ⁡ ( x , w ) D_{0}=d(x,w)  and 
 

 
 
 ℓ = max ⁡ { 1 , ⌊ s 2 8 ​ log ⁡ Gr ​ ( s ) ⌋ } . \ell=\max\left\{1,\left\lfloor\frac{s^{2}}{8\log\operatorname{Gr}(s)}\right\rfloor\right\}. 
 
 
 Then 
 

 
 
 s 2 16 ​ log ⁡ Gr ​ ( s ) ≤ ℓ ≤ T . \frac{s^{2}}{16\log\operatorname{Gr}(s)}\leq\ell\leq T. 
 
 (7.43) 
 
 Indeed, the lower bound follows by considering separately whether the
quantity inside the floor is smaller than  2 2 ; the upper bound follows
from  T ≥ s 2 / 4 T\geq s^{2}/4 ,  Gr ⁡ ( s ) ≥ 2 \operatorname{Gr}(s)\geq 2 , and  T ≥ 1 T\geq 1 .
If  ℓ = 1 \ell=1 , a walk started in  B s ​ ( w ) B_{s}(w)  remains in  B 2 ​ s ​ ( w ) B_{2s}(w) 
for its next step deterministically. If  ℓ ≥ 2 \ell\geq 2 , Corollary 5.12
and  Gr ⁡ ( s ) ≥ s + 1 \operatorname{Gr}(s)\geq s+1  give, uniformly in
 y ∈ B s ​ ( w ) y\in B_{s}(w) , 
 

 
 
 𝐏 y ​ ( X j ∈ B 2 ​ s ​ ( w ) ​  for  ​ 0 ≤ j ≤ ℓ ) ≥ 1 − 2 ( ℓ + 1 ) Gr ( s ) e − s 2 / ( 2 ℓ ) ≥ 1 2 . \begin{split}\mathbf{P}_{y}\left(X_{j}\in B_{2s}(w)\text{ for }0\leq j\leq\ell\right)&\geq 1-2(\ell+1)\operatorname{Gr}(s)e^{-s^{2}/(2\ell)}\geq\frac{1}{2}.\end{split} 
 
 (7.44) 
 
 The last numerical inequality follows from
 ℓ ≤ s 2 / [ 8 ​ log ⁡ Gr ⁡ ( s ) ] \ell\leq s^{2}/[8\log\operatorname{Gr}(s)] :
its error term is at most 
 

 
 
 3 ​ s 2 8 ​ log ⁡ Gr ⁡ ( s ) ​ Gr ​ ( s ) 3 ≤ 1 2 . \frac{3s^{2}}{8\log\operatorname{Gr}(s)\operatorname{Gr}(s)^{3}}\leq\frac{1}{2}. 
 
 
 
 
 Fix an integer  1 ≤ b ≤ T 1\leq b\leq T , let 
 

 
 
 A b = { X j ∈ B s ( w )  for some  b ≤ j ≤ T } , Z b = ∑ j = b 2 ​ T 𝟏 { X j ∈ B 2 ​ s ( w ) } . A_{b}=\{X_{j}\in B_{s}(w)\text{ for some }b\leq j\leq T\},\qquad Z_{b}=\sum_{j=b}^{2T}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{j}\in B_{2s}(w)\}}. 
 
 
 Apply the strong Markov property at the first time in
 [ b , T ] [b,T]  at which  X X  enters  B s ​ ( w ) B_{s}(w) . Since  ℓ ≤ T \ell\leq T ,
( 7.43 )–( 7.44 ) yield 
 

 
 
 𝐄 x ​ [ Z b ∣ A b ] ≥ ℓ 2 ≥ s 2 32 ​ log ⁡ Gr ​ ( s ) . \mathbf{E}_{x}[Z_{b}\mid A_{b}]\geq\frac{\ell}{2}\geq\frac{s^{2}}{32\log\operatorname{Gr}(s)}. 
 
 (7.45) 
 
 
 
 We next bound the same occupation variable without conditioning.
Auxiliary Lemma  7.F , with its constants absorbing the
replacement of  s s  by  2 ​ s 2s , gives 
 

 
 
 𝐄 x ​ Z b ≤ C ​ ∑ j = b 2 ​ T [ log ⁡ max ⁡ { j / s 2 , Gr ⁡ ( 2 ​ s ) } ] κ ​ ( s 2 j ) κ / 2 . \mathbf{E}_{x}Z_{b}\leq C\sum_{j=b}^{2T}\left[\log\max\{j/s^{2},\operatorname{Gr}(2s)\}\right]^{\kappa}\left(\frac{s^{2}}{j}\right)^{\kappa/2}. 
 
 (7.46) 
 
 When the scale interval in Auxiliary Lemma  7.F  is
empty, the corresponding summand is bounded instead by the trivial
probability one; the displayed right-hand side still dominates it after
changing  C C . Every
nonvacuous application has lower scale at least  s s  and upper scale at
most  ( 2 ​ T ) 1 / 2 / 2 ≤ T 1 / 2 (2T)^{1/2}/2\leq T^{1/2} , so the stated tripling range suffices. 
 
 
 Put 
 

 
 
 L b = log ⁡ max ⁡ { b / s 2 , Gr ⁡ ( 2 ​ s ) } ≥ 1 . L_{b}=\log\max\{b/s^{2},\operatorname{Gr}(2s)\}\geq 1. 
 
 
 On the dyadic block  2 r ​ b ≤ j < 2 r + 1 ​ b 2^{r}b\leq j<2^{r+1}b , the contribution to
( 7.46 ) is at most 
 

 
 
 C ​ s κ ​ b 1 − κ / 2 ​ 2 − r ⁡ ( κ / 2 − 1 ) ​ [ L b + ( r + 1 ) ​ log ⁡ 2 ] κ . Cs^{\kappa}b^{1-\kappa/2}2^{-r(\kappa/2-1)}[L_{b}+(r+1)\log 2]^{\kappa}. 
 
 
 Because  κ > 2 \kappa>2 , summing these bounds over  r ≥ 0 r\geq 0  gives 
 

 
 
 𝐄 x ​ Z b ≤ C κ ​ L b κ ​ s κ b ( κ − 2 ) / 2 . \mathbf{E}_{x}Z_{b}\leq C_{\kappa}L_{b}^{\kappa}\frac{s^{\kappa}}{b^{(\kappa-2)/2}}. 
 
 (7.47) 
 
 Since
 𝐏 x ​ ( A b ) ​ 𝐄 x ​ [ Z b ∣ A b ] ≤ 𝐄 x ​ Z b \mathbf{P}_{x}(A_{b})\mathbf{E}_{x}[Z_{b}\mid A_{b}]\leq\mathbf{E}_{x}Z_{b} ,
( 7.45 )–( 7.47 ),
 log ⁡ Gr ⁡ ( s ) ≤ L b \log\operatorname{Gr}(s)\leq L_{b} , and  L b ≥ 1 L_{b}\geq 1  imply 
 

 
 
 𝐏 x ​ ( A b ) ≤ C ​ L b κ + 2 ​ s κ − 2 b ( κ − 2 ) / 2 . \mathbf{P}_{x}(A_{b})\leq CL_{b}^{\kappa+2}\frac{s^{\kappa-2}}{b^{(\kappa-2)/2}}. 
 
 (7.48) 
 
 
 
 It remains to add the probability of hitting  B s ​ ( w ) B_{s}(w)  before time
 b b . The pointwise Varopoulos–Carne estimate
 [ 10 , Theorem 5.11]  gives, for
 1 ≤ j ≤ b 1\leq j\leq b  and  y ∈ B s ​ ( w ) y\in B_{s}(w) , 
 

 
 
 𝐏 x ​ ( X j = y ) ≤ 2 ​ exp ⁡ ( − ( D 0 − s ) 2 2 ​ j ) ≤ 2 ​ exp ⁡ ( − D 0 2 8 ​ b ) . \mathbf{P}_{x}(X_{j}=y)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{(D_{0}-s)^{2}}{2j}\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{D_{0}^{2}}{8b}\right). 
 
 
 At time zero the event is empty because  x ∉ B s ​ ( w ) x\notin B_{s}(w) .
Union bounds over the  b + 1 b+1  times and at most
 Gr ⁡ ( s ) \operatorname{Gr}(s)  target vertices, followed by
( 7.48 ), give 
 

 
 
 𝐏 x ​ ( hit  ​ B s ​ ( w ) ​  by time  ​ T ) ≤ C ​ [ log ⁡ max ⁡ { b / s 2 , Gr ⁡ ( 2 ​ s ) } ] κ + 2 ​ s κ − 2 b ( κ − 2 ) / 2 + 2 ​ ( b + 1 ) ​ Gr ⁡ ( s ) ​ exp ⁡ ( − D 0 2 8 ​ b ) . \begin{split}\mathbf{P}_{x}(\text{hit }B_{s}(w)\text{ by time }T)&\leq C\left[\log\max\{b/s^{2},\operatorname{Gr}(2s)\}\right]^{\kappa+2}\frac{s^{\kappa-2}}{b^{(\kappa-2)/2}}\\
&\quad+2(b+1)\operatorname{Gr}(s)\exp\left(-\frac{D_{0}^{2}}{8b}\right).\end{split} 
 
 (7.49) 
 
 
 
 Let  L 0 = log ⁡ max ⁡ { D 0 , Gr ⁡ ( 2 ​ s ) } L_{0}=\log\max\{D_{0},\operatorname{Gr}(2s)\} , choose a sufficiently
small  c 0 = c 0 ​ ( κ ) > 0 c_{0}=c_{0}(\kappa)>0 , and put  b ∗ = c 0 ​ D 0 2 / L 0 b_{*}=c_{0}D_{0}^{2}/L_{0} . If  b ∗ < 2 b_{*}<2 ,
then 
 

 
 
 L 0 ( 3 ​ κ + 2 ) / 2 ​ ( s / D 0 ) κ − 2 ≥ c κ , L_{0}^{(3\kappa+2)/2}(s/D_{0})^{\kappa-2}\geq c_{\kappa}, 
 
 
 because  L 0 > c 0 ​ D 0 2 / 2 L_{0}>c_{0}D_{0}^{2}/2 ,  s ≥ 1 s\geq 1 , and
 2 ​ ( 3 ​ κ + 2 ) / 2 − ( κ − 2 ) = 2 ​ κ + 4 > 0 2(3\kappa+2)/2-(\kappa-2)=2\kappa+4>0 . Thus
( 7.42 ) follows from the trivial bound one.
If  2 ≤ b ∗ ≤ T 2\leq b_{*}\leq T , take  b = ⌊ b ∗ ⌋ b=\lfloor b_{*}\rfloor  in
( 7.49 ). Since  b ∗ / 2 ≤ b ≤ b ∗ ≤ D 0 2 b_{*}/2\leq b\leq b_{*}\leq D_{0}^{2} ,
the first term is at most 
 

 
 
 C κ ​ L 0 κ + 2 + ( κ − 2 ) / 2 ​ ( s / D 0 ) κ − 2 = C κ ​ L 0 ( 3 ​ κ + 2 ) / 2 ​ ( s / D 0 ) κ − 2 . C_{\kappa}L_{0}^{\kappa+2+(\kappa-2)/2}(s/D_{0})^{\kappa-2}=C_{\kappa}L_{0}^{(3\kappa+2)/2}(s/D_{0})^{\kappa-2}. 
 
 
 The second term is at most
 C ​ D 0 2 ​ e L 0 − L 0 / ( 8 ​ c 0 ) CD_{0}^{2}e^{L_{0}-L_{0}/(8c_{0})} . Since  D 0 2 ≤ e 2 ​ L 0 D_{0}^{2}\leq e^{2L_{0}} , choosing
 c 0 c_{0}  so that  1 / ( 8 ​ c 0 ) − 3 ≥ κ − 2 1/(8c_{0})-3\geq\kappa-2  bounds it by the same target. 
 
 
 It remains to treat  b ∗ > T b_{*}>T , the case omitted in the cited optimization.
The same pointwise estimate, followed by a union bound over
 0 ≤ j ≤ T 0\leq j\leq T  and  y ∈ B s ​ ( w ) y\in B_{s}(w) , gives directly 
 

 
 
 𝐏 x ​ ( hit  ​ B s ​ ( w ) ​  by time  ​ T ) ≤ 2 ​ ( T + 1 ) ​ Gr ⁡ ( s ) ​ exp ⁡ ( − D 0 2 8 ​ T ) . \mathbf{P}_{x}(\text{hit }B_{s}(w)\text{ by time }T)\leq 2(T+1)\operatorname{Gr}(s)\exp\left(-\frac{D_{0}^{2}}{8T}\right). 
 
 (7.50) 
 
 Writing  q 0 = D 0 2 / T q_{0}=D_{0}^{2}/T , the inequality  b ∗ > T b_{*}>T  gives
 q 0 > L 0 / c 0 q_{0}>L_{0}/c_{0} . Since  T + 1 ≤ 2 ​ T T+1\leq 2T ,  D 0 2 ≤ e 2 ​ L 0 D_{0}^{2}\leq e^{2L_{0}} , and
 Gr ⁡ ( s ) ≤ e L 0 \operatorname{Gr}(s)\leq e^{L_{0}} , the right-hand side of
( 7.50 ) is at most 
 

 
 
 C ​ e 3 ​ L 0 − q 0 / 8 ≤ C ​ e − ( κ − 2 ) ​ L 0 ≤ C ​ L 0 ( 3 ​ κ + 2 ) / 2 ​ ( s / D 0 ) κ − 2 , Ce^{3L_{0}-q_{0}/8}\leq Ce^{-(\kappa-2)L_{0}}\leq CL_{0}^{(3\kappa+2)/2}(s/D_{0})^{\kappa-2}, 
 
 
 after decreasing  c 0 c_{0}  once more. This proves
( 7.42 ) in all cases.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 7.I   (Intersection estimate) . 
 
 Let  G G  be an infinite,
connected, unimodular transitive graph of degree  d d , let
 κ > 4 \kappa>4 , and let  R , t ≥ 1 R,t\geq 1  be integers. Suppose that 
 

 
 
 t ≥ R 2 , Gr ( 3 j ) ≥ 3 κ Gr ( j ) ( R ≤ j ≤ t 1 / 2 ) . t\geq R^{2},\qquad\operatorname{Gr}(3j)\geq 3^{\kappa}\operatorname{Gr}(j)\quad(R\leq j\leq t^{1/2}). 
 
 
 If  X X  and  Y Y  are independent lazy random walks started at  x x  and
 y y , respectively, and  D = d ⁡ ( x , y ) ≥ 4 ​ R D=d(x,y)\geq 4R , then 
 

 
 
 ( 𝐏 x ⊗ 𝐏 y ) ( d ( X i , Y j ) ≤ R  for some  0 ≤ i , j ≤ t ) ≤ C ⁡ ( d , κ ) ​ t D 2 ​ [ log ⁡ max ⁡ { D , Gr ⁡ ( 4 ​ R ) } ] ( 3 ​ κ + 4 ) / 2 ​ ( R D ) κ − 4 . \begin{split}&(\mathbf{P}_{x}\otimes\mathbf{P}_{y})\bigl(d(X_{i},Y_{j})\leq R\text{ for some }0\leq i,j\leq t\bigr)\\
&\quad\leq C(d,\kappa)\frac{t}{D^{2}}\left[\log\max\{D,\operatorname{Gr}(4R)\}\right]^{(3\kappa+4)/2}\left(\frac{R}{D}\right)^{\kappa-4}.\end{split} 
 
 (7.51) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Write 
 

 
 
 L = log ⁡ max ⁡ { D , Gr ⁡ ( 4 ​ R ) } , Ψ = t D 2 ​ L ( 3 ​ κ + 4 ) / 2 ​ ( R D ) κ − 4 . L=\log\max\{D,\operatorname{Gr}(4R)\},\qquad\Psi=\frac{t}{D^{2}}L^{(3\kappa+4)/2}\left(\frac{R}{D}\right)^{\kappa-4}. 
 
 
 If  4 ​ R ≤ D < 16 ​ R 4R\leq D<16R , then  t ≥ R 2 t\geq R^{2}  implies that  Ψ \Psi  is bounded
below by a positive constant depending only on  κ \kappa . Increasing the
constant proves the claim in this case, so suppose that  D ≥ 16 ​ R D\geq 16R . 
 
 
 We first handle intersections involving time zero. If  i = 0 i=0 ,
then  Y Y  hits  B R ​ ( x ) B_{R}(x)  by time  t t ; the case  j = 0 j=0  is symmetric.
Applying ( 7.42 ) with radius  R R  gives a bound
of order 
 

 
 
 L ( 3 ​ κ + 2 ) / 2 ​ ( R / D ) κ − 2 ≤ Ψ , L^{(3\kappa+2)/2}(R/D)^{\kappa-2}\leq\Psi, 
 
 
 because  t / R 2 ≥ 1 t/R^{2}\geq 1  and 
 

 
 
 Ψ = t R 2 ​ L ( 3 ​ κ + 4 ) / 2 ​ ( R / D ) κ − 2 . \Psi=\frac{t}{R^{2}}L^{(3\kappa+4)/2}(R/D)^{\kappa-2}. 
 
 (7.52) 
 
 
 
 It remains to consider  1 ≤ i , j ≤ t 1\leq i,j\leq t . On
 d ⁡ ( X i , Y j ) ≤ R d(X_{i},Y_{j})\leq R , the triangle inequality gives 
 

 
 
 D ≤ d ⁡ ( x , X i ) + R + d ⁡ ( Y j , y ) . D\leq d(x,X_{i})+R+d(Y_{j},y). 
 
 
 Thus either
 d ⁡ ( x , X i ) ≥ ( D − R ) / 2 d(x,X_{i})\geq(D-R)/2  or
 d ⁡ ( y , Y j ) ≥ ( D − R ) / 2 d(y,Y_{j})\geq(D-R)/2 . The two cases are symmetric; we treat the first.
For each  j j , let  Y τ k Y_{\tau_{k}}  be a crease point preceding  Y j Y_{j} ,
with the terminal crease point used when  j = t j=t . By construction,
 d ⁡ ( Y j , Y τ k ) ≤ R d(Y_{j},Y_{\tau_{k}})\leq R . Hence 
 

 
 
 d ⁡ ( X i , Y τ k ) ≤ 2 ​ R , d ⁡ ( x , Y τ k ) ≥ D − R 2 − 2 ​ R ≥ D 4 . d(X_{i},Y_{\tau_{k}})\leq 2R,\qquad d(x,Y_{\tau_{k}})\geq\frac{D-R}{2}-2R\geq\frac{D}{4}. 
 
 (7.53) 
 
 This is the required weakened separation; no interchange of  x x  and
 y y  is being made. 
 
 
 Choose  c ∗ = 1 / [ 4 ​ ( κ + 2 ) ] c_{*}=1/[4(\kappa+2)]  and put 
 

 
 
 a = max ⁡ { 1 , ⌊ c ∗ ​ R 2 L ⌋ } . a=\max\left\{1,\left\lfloor\frac{c_{*}R^{2}}{L}\right\rfloor\right\}. 
 
 
 If  a = 1 a=1 , use the deterministic bound
 cr R ⁡ ( Y t ) + 1 ≤ t + 2 \operatorname{cr}_{R}(Y^{t})+1\leq t+2 . Since then
 c ∗ ​ R 2 / L < 2 c_{*}R^{2}/L<2 , this is at most  C κ ​ t ​ L / R 2 C_{\kappa}tL/R^{2} . If  a ≥ 2 a\geq 2 , let
 ℬ = { cr R ( Y t ) > 1 + t / a } \mathcal{B}=\{\operatorname{cr}_{R}(Y^{t})>1+t/a\} . On
 ℬ c \mathcal{B}^{c} , the number of crease points is at most 
 

 
 
 2 + t / a ≤ 3 ​ t / a ≤ C κ ​ t ​ L / R 2 ; 2+t/a\leq 3t/a\leq C_{\kappa}tL/R^{2}; 
 
 (7.54) 
 
 here  t / a ≥ 1 t/a\geq 1 . The crease lemma gives 
 

 
 
 𝐏 y ( ℬ ) ≤ 2 ( t + 1 ) ( a + 1 ) Gr ( R ) e − R 2 / ( 2 a ) ≤ 8 t R 2 e L − L / ( 2 ​ c ∗ ) . \mathbf{P}_{y}(\mathcal{B})\leq 2(t+1)(a+1)\operatorname{Gr}(R)e^{-R^{2}/(2a)}\leq 8tR^{2}e^{L-L/(2c_{*})}. 
 
 
 Since  R 2 ≤ e 2 ​ L R^{2}\leq e^{2L} , our choice of  c ∗ c_{*}  makes the last expression
at most  8 ​ t ​ e − ( κ − 2 ) ​ L 8te^{-(\kappa-2)L} . On the other hand,
 R κ − 4 ≥ 1 R^{\kappa-4}\geq 1 ,  D ≤ e L D\leq e^{L} , and  L ≥ 1 L\geq 1 , so
 Ψ ≥ t ​ e − ( κ − 2 ) ​ L \Psi\geq te^{-(\kappa-2)L} . Consequently 
 

 
 
 𝐏 y ​ ( ℬ ) ≤ 8 ​ Ψ . \mathbf{P}_{y}(\mathcal{B})\leq 8\Psi. 
 
 (7.55) 
 
 
 
 We record the uniformity in the location of a crease center. If
 p = ( 3 ​ κ + 2 ) / 2 p=(3\kappa+2)/2  and  A , D ≥ e A,D\geq e , then 
 

 
 
 sup z ≥ D / 4 [ log ⁡ max ​ { z , A } ] p z κ − 2 ≤ C κ ​ [ log ⁡ max ​ { D , A } ] p D κ − 2 . \sup_{z\geq D/4}\frac{[\log\max\{z,A\}]^{p}}{z^{\kappa-2}}\leq C_{\kappa}\frac{[\log\max\{D,A\}]^{p}}{D^{\kappa-2}}. 
 
 (7.56) 
 
 Indeed, write  z = D ​ y / 4 z=Dy/4 , where  y ≥ 1 y\geq 1 , and put
 L A = log ⁡ max ⁡ { D , A } L_{A}=\log\max\{D,A\} . Then 
 

 
 
 log ⁡ max ⁡ { z , A } ≤ L A + log ⁡ max ⁡ { y , 1 } , \log\max\{z,A\}\leq L_{A}+\log\max\{y,1\}, 
 
 
 and  L A ≥ 1 L_{A}\geq 1 . After division by the right-hand side of
( 7.56 ), the resulting expression is bounded by a
constant times
 y − ( κ − 2 ) ​ ( 1 + log ⁡ y ) p y^{-(\kappa-2)}(1+\log y)^{p} , whose supremum over  y ≥ 1 y\geq 1  is finite. 
 
 
 Condition on  Y Y  and on  ℬ c \mathcal{B}^{c}  (with
 ℬ = ∅ \mathcal{B}=\varnothing  in the case  a = 1 a=1 ). By
( 7.53 ), a relevant intersection forces

For  c > 0 c>0 , write  k c ​ ( s ) k_{c}(s) ,  r c ​ ( s ) r_{c}(s) , and  ℓ c ​ ( s ) \ell_{c}(s)  for the

 k c ′ ​ ( s ) ≤ k c ​ ( s ) , r c ′ ​ ( s ) ≤ r c ​ ( s ) , ℓ c ′ ​ ( s ) ≥ ℓ c ​ ( s ) . k_{c^{\prime}}(s)\leq k_{c}(s),\qquad r_{c^{\prime}}(s)\leq r_{c}(s),\qquad\ell_{c^{\prime}}(s)\geq\ell_{c}(s). 
 
 
 Consequently, every family witnessing plentiful tubes with parameters
 ( c , λ ) (c,\lambda)  also witnesses plentiful tubes with parameters
 ( c ′ , λ ) (c^{\prime},\lambda) : the same family contains at least the required number of
paths, its smaller-radius tube neighbourhoods remain disjoint, and its paths
obey the larger permitted length bound. The accompanying scale inequality
also becomes weaker, since  m 1 1 + c ′ ≤ m 1 1 + c m_{1}^{1+c^{\prime}}\leq m_{1}^{1+c} . Thus the single
choice  c 1 = min ⁡ { c str , c rw } c_{1}=\min\{c_{\rm str},c_{\rm rw}\}  proves both conclusions,
independently of  λ \lambda . Thus the displayed minimum is a common
constant for both branches. 
 
 
 Finally, suppose that  n ∈ ℒ ⁡ ( G , D ) n\in\mathscr{L}(G,D) . If some
 s ′ ∈ [ n 1 / 3 , n 10 / 11 ] s^{\prime}\in[n^{1/3},n^{10/11}]  satisfies
 Gr ⁡ ( 3 ​ s ′ ) ≤ 3 5 ​ Gr ⁡ ( s ′ ) \operatorname{Gr}(3s^{\prime})\leq 3^{5}\operatorname{Gr}(s^{\prime}) , the structure
branch and the logarithmic selection ( 7.62 ) give the
required interval. Otherwise, for every
 s ∈ [ n 2 / 5 , n 4 / 5 ] s\in[n^{2/5},n^{4/5}] , 
 

 
 
 [ s 9 / 10 , s 11 / 10 ] ⊆ [ n 1 / 3 , n 10 / 11 ] ⊆ [ n 1 / 3 , n ] . [s^{9/10},s^{11/10}]\subseteq[n^{1/3},n^{10/11}]\subseteq[n^{1/3},n]. 
 
 
 The failure of the slow inequality supplies the second condition in
( 7 ) throughout the first interval, while
 n ∈ ℒ ⁡ ( G , D ) n\in\mathscr{L}(G,D)  supplies the first. The random-walk construction
proved above therefore applies at every such  s s . These two cases
are exhaustive, and the preceding common-constant choice gives exactly
( 7.3 ); in particular, it implies
 m 1 ≤ n 1 / ( 1 + c 1 ) m_{1}\leq n^{1/(1+c_{1})} .  □ \square 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 7.K   (Rounded scale selection) . 
 
 Assume ( 7.3 ), set 
 

 
 
 𝒮 ⁡ ( n ) = [ m 1 1 + c 1 / 4 , m 1 1 + 3 ​ c 1 / 4 ] ∩ ℕ , \mathscr{S}(n)=[m_{1}^{1+c_{1}/4},m_{1}^{1+3c_{1}/4}]\cap\mathbb{N}, 
 
 
 and let  f f  be nondecreasing on the integers with
 1 ≤ f ⁡ ( s ) ≤ s 1\leq f(s)\leq s . For each fixed  R > 0 R>0 , if  n n  is sufficiently
large in terms of  c 1 , R c_{1},R , there are  m − , m ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( n ) m_{-},m\in\mathscr{S}(n)  with 
 

 
 
 1 2 ​ m ​ ( log ⁡ n ) − R ≤ m − ≤ 2 ​ m ​ ( log ⁡ n ) − R , f ⁡ ( m ) f ⁡ ( m − ) ≤ ( log ⁡ n ) 8 ​ R / c 1 . \tfrac{1}{2}m(\log n)^{-R}\leq m_{-}\leq 2m(\log n)^{-R},\qquad\frac{f(m)}{f(m_{-})}\leq(\log n)^{8R/c_{1}}. 
 
 (7.63) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Starting with the least integer in  𝒮 ⁡ ( n ) \mathscr{S}(n) , take
successive ceilings after multiplication by  ( log ⁡ n ) R (\log n)^{R} , stopping
before the right endpoint. Since  m 1 ≥ n 1 / 3 m_{1}\geq n^{1/3} , the logarithmic
width of  𝒮 ⁡ ( n ) \mathscr{S}(n)  is at least  ( c 1 / 6 ) ​ log ⁡ n + O ⁡ ( 1 ) (c_{1}/6)\log n+O(1) .
Hence there are at least
 c 1 ​ log ⁡ n / ( 7 ​ R ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) c_{1}\log n/(7R\log\log n)  consecutive pairs for large  n n .
If the second inequality in ( 7.63 ) failed for all pairs, telescoping
would give 
 

 
 
 f ⁡ ( s last ) > ( log ⁡ n ) ( 8 ​ R / c 1 ) ​ c 1 ​ log ⁡ n / ( 7 ​ R ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) > n , f(s_{\rm last})>(\log n)^{(8R/c_{1})c_{1}\log n/(7R\log\log n)}>n, 
 
 
 contrary to  f ⁡ ( s last ) ≤ s last ≤ n f(s_{\rm last})\leq s_{\rm last}\leq n . Ceilings change
the reciprocal scale relation by a factor at most two.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proposition 7.1   (Site multiscale criterion) . 
 
 Fix
 d < ∞ d<\infty  and  a , b > 0 a,b>0 . There are  K , N < ∞ K,N<\infty  such that the
following holds. Let  G G  be an infinite connected unimodular
transitive degree- d d  graph that is not one-dimensional. Let
 p ∈ [ a , 1 − b ] p\in[a,1-b] , let  n ≥ N n\geq N  be a real scale, and set 
 

 
 
 δ 0 = ( log log n ) − 1 / 2 + K Burn s ( G , n , p ) . \delta_{0}=(\log\log n)^{-1/2}+K\operatorname{Burn}^{\rm s}(G,n,p). 
 
 
 Suppose  δ 0 ≤ 1 \delta_{0}\leq 1 ,
 Spr ( p ; 2 ( log log n ) − 1 / 2 + K Burn s ( G , n , p ) ) ≤ 1 − b \operatorname{Spr}(p;2(\log\log n)^{-1/2}+K\operatorname{Burn}^{\rm s}(G,n,p))\leq 1-b , and 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( u ↔ v ) ≥ exp ⁡ [ − log ⁡ log ⁡ ( ( log ⁡ n ) 1 / 2 ) ] for every  ​ u , v ∈ B n . \mathbb{P}_{p}(u\leftrightarrow v)\geq\exp\!\left[-\sqrt{\log\log((\log n)^{1/2})}\right]\quad\text{for every }u,v\in B_{n}. 
 
 
 Then 
 

 
 
 p c s ( G ) ≤ Spr ( p ; 2 ( log log n ) − 1 / 2 + K Burn s ( G , n , p ) ) . p_{c}^{\rm s}(G)\leq\operatorname{Spr}\left(p;2(\log\log n)^{-1/2}+K\operatorname{Burn}^{\rm s}(G,n,p)\right). 
 
 (7.64) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof.  The premise forces  p ≥ 1 / d p\geq 1/d  once  N = N ⁡ ( d ) N=N(d)  is large.
Indeed, if  p < 1 / d p<1/d , put  N n = ⌊ n ⌋ N_{n}=\lfloor n\rfloor  and choose
 y ∈ S N n ​ ( o ) y\in S_{N_{n}}(o) . (The graph is infinite and connected, so this
sphere is nonempty.) The simple-path union bound gives 
 

 
 
 ℙ p ​ ( o ↔ y ) ≤ d d − 1 ​ p ​ [ p ⁡ ( d − 1 ) ] N n 1 − p ⁡ ( d − 1 ) ≤ C d ​ ( d − 1 d ) ⌊ n ⌋ ≤ C d ′ ​ ( d − 1 d ) n . \mathbb{P}_{p}(o\leftrightarrow y)\leq\frac{d}{d-1}\frac{p[p(d-1)]^{N_{n}}}{1-p(d-1)}\leq C_{d}\left(\frac{d-1}{d}\right)^{\lfloor n\rfloor}\leq C^{\prime}_{d}\left(\frac{d-1}{d}\right)^{n}. 
 
 
 Since  y ∈ B n ​ ( o ) y\in B_{n}(o) , this contradicts the displayed two-point premise
at radius  n n  for all sufficiently large  n n . Thus we
may use the lower parameter bound required by
Propositions  6.2  and
 7.3 . 
 
 
 We first isolate the two product-space facts used below. 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 7.L   (Site Hamming) . 
 
 Let  D D  be a finite graph, let  A , B ⊆ V ⁡ ( D ) A,B\subseteq V(D)  be nonempty,
and let  0 < p < 1 0<p<1 ,  0 < θ ≤ 1 0<\theta\leq 1 , and  t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 . Adopt the
convention 
 

 
 
 max x , y ∈ A x ≠ y ⁡ ℙ p ​ ( x ↔ y ) = 0 when  ​ | A | = 1 . \max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in A\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}_{p}(x\leftrightarrow y)=0\quad\text{when }|A|=1. 
 
 
 If 
 

 
 
 min x ∈ A ⁡ ℙ p ​ ( x ↔ B ) ≥ θ ≥ 2 ​ | A | ​ max x , y ∈ A x ≠ y ​ ℙ p ​ ( x ↔ y ) , \min_{x\in A}\mathbb{P}_{p}(x\leftrightarrow B)\geq\theta\geq 2|A|\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in A\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}_{p}(x\leftrightarrow y), 
 
 
 then 
 

 
 
 ℙ Spr ⁡ ( p , t ) ( A ↔ B ) ≥ 1 − e − t θ | A | / 2 . \mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{Spr}(p;t)}(A\leftrightarrow B)\geq 1-e^{-t\theta|A|/2}. 
 
 (7.65) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We first prove the finite-product differential Hamming inequality needed
here, so Proposition 6.7 of  [ 10 ]  is not an input.
Let  I = V ⁡ ( D ) I=V(D) , let  F ⊆ { 0 , 1 } I F\subseteq\{0,1\}^{I}  be decreasing, and write
 H F ​ ( ω ) H_{F}(\omega)  for the minimum number of open coordinates that must be
closed to obtain a configuration in  F F , with  H F = 0 H_{F}=0  on  F F .
Harris–FKG implies that  ℙ q ( ⋅ ∣ F ) \mathbb{P}_{q}(\,\cdot\mid F)  is stochastically
dominated by  ℙ q \mathbb{P}_{q} . Since the product space is finite, the
monotone coupling theorem gives a coupling  ( Ω , Ξ ) (\Omega,\Xi)  with
 Ω ∼ ℙ q \Omega\sim\mathbb{P}_{q} ,
 Ξ ∼ ℙ q ( ⋅ ∣ F ) \Xi\sim\mathbb{P}_{q}(\,\cdot\mid F) , and  Ξ ≤ Ω \Xi\leq\Omega 
coordinatewise. Consequently, 
 

 
 
 H F ​ ( Ω ) ≤ | Ω | − | Ξ | , 𝔼 q ​ H F ≤ | I | ​ q − 𝔼 q ​ [ | ω | ∣ F ] . H_{F}(\Omega)\leq|\Omega|-|\Xi|,\qquad\mathbb{E}_{q}H_{F}\leq|I|q-\mathbb{E}_{q}[|\omega|\mid F]. 
 
 (7.66) 
 
 Direct differentiation of the finite Bernoulli polynomial gives 
 

 
 
 d d ​ q ​ log ⁡ ℙ q ​ ( F ) = 𝔼 q ​ [ | ω | ∣ F ] − | I | ​ q q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) . \frac{d}{dq}\log\mathbb{P}_{q}(F)=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{q}[|\omega|\mid F]-|I|q}{q(1-q)}. 
 
 
 Together with ( 7.66 ), this proves 
 

 
 
 d d ​ q ​ [ − log ⁡ ℙ q ​ ( F ) ] ≥ 𝔼 q ​ H F q ⁡ ( 1 − q ) \frac{d}{dq}[-\log\mathbb{P}_{q}(F)]\geq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{q}H_{F}}{q(1-q)} 
 
 (7.67) 
 
 for  0 < q < 1 0<q<1 . This derivation also proves the endpoint cases needed by
continuity whenever the probabilities are nonzero. 
 
 
 Apply ( 7.67 ) to
 F = { A ↮ B } F=\{A\not\leftrightarrow B\} . For a site configuration, the
distance to  F F  is at least the
number of distinct open clusters that meet both  A A  and  B B : at least
one open vertex must be closed in each such cluster, and these vertex sets
are disjoint. That number is at least 
 

 
 
 ∑ x ∈ A 𝟏 { x ↔ B } − ∑ x , y ∈ A x ≠ y 𝟏 { x ↔ y } . \sum_{x\in A}\mathbf{1}_{\{x\leftrightarrow B\}}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in A\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{\{x\leftrightarrow y\}}. 
 
 
 Taking expectations and using the two hypotheses gives expected Hamming
distance at least  θ ​ | A | / 2 \theta|A|/2  at density  p p . The Hamming distance
to the decreasing event  F F  is an increasing function of the
configuration, so stochastic domination gives the same lower bound at
every density  q ≥ p q\geq p . If
 q ⁡ ( t ) = Spr ⁡ ( p , t ) q(t)=\operatorname{Spr}(p;t) , the chain rule applied to
( 7.67 ), together with
 q ′ ​ ( t ) = − ( 1 − q ⁡ ( t ) ) ​ log ⁡ ( 1 − q ⁡ ( t ) ) q^{\prime}(t)=-(1-q(t))\log(1-q(t)) , gives 
 

 
 
 d d ​ t ​ [ − log ⁡ ℙ q ⁡ ( t ) ​ ( A ↮ B ) ] ≥ − log ⁡ ( 1 − q ⁡ ( t ) ) q ⁡ ( t ) ​ θ ​ | A | 2 ≥ θ ​ | A | 2 . \frac{d}{dt}[-\log\mathbb{P}_{q(t)}(A\not\leftrightarrow B)]\geq\frac{-\log(1-q(t))}{q(t)}\frac{\theta|A|}{2}\geq\frac{\theta|A|}{2}. 
 
 
 Integration proves ( 7.65 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Auxiliary Lemma 7.M   (Fixed-cluster tube gluing) . 
 
 Couple site percolation by uniform labels,
let  0 < p < q < 1 0<p<q<1 , let  H ⊆ V ⁡ ( G ) H\subseteq V(G)  be finite, and condition on two
distinct  p p -open clusters  C , D C,D  of the configuration induced on
 H H . More precisely, condition on the exact event that they are two
distinct induced clusters with these vertex sets. If  F = C ∪ D F=C\cup D , the
conditional label law on  H H  is the product law 
 

 
 
 z ∈ F U z ∼ Unif ⁡ [ 0 , p ] , z ∈ ∂ V H F U z ∼ Unif ⁡ ( p , 1 ] , z ∈ H ∖ ( F ∪ ∂ V H F ) U z ∼ Unif ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] , \begin{array}[]{c|c}z\in F&U_{z}\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,p],\\
z\in\partial_{V}^{H}F&U_{z}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(p,1],\\
z\in H\setminus(F\cup\partial_{V}^{H}F)&U_{z}\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,1],\end{array} 
 
 (7.68) 
 
 with all displayed coordinates independent; no condition outside  H H 
is asserted. Let  T 1 , … , T k ⊆ H T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}\subseteq H  be tubes whose sets of
vertices outside  F F  are pairwise disjoint. If ordinary
 p p -site percolation on  T j ∖ F T_{j}\setminus F , with every site of  F F 
declared open but with no vertex identifications, connects  C C  to
 D D  in the induced graph  G ⁡ [ T j ∪ F ] G[T_{j}\cup F]  with probability at least
 ξ \xi  for every  1 ≤ j ≤ k 1\leq j\leq k , then, conditionally on  C , D C,D ,
the events 
 

 
 
 { C ↔ D ​  in  ​ G ​ [ T j ∪ F ] ​  at parameter  ​ q } , 1 ≤ j ≤ k , \{C\leftrightarrow D\text{ in }G[T_{j}\cup F]\text{ at parameter }q\},\qquad 1\leq j\leq k, 
 
 
 are independent and each has probability at least 
 

 
 
 ξ ​ min ⁡ { 1 , q − p p ⁡ ( 1 − p ) } 2 . \xi\min\left\{1,\frac{q-p}{p(1-p)}\right\}^{2}. 
 
 (7.69) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The conditional product law in ( 7.68 ) is
Lemma  2.1  for the breadth-first cluster
exploration restricted to  H H , with external boundary taken relative
to  H H , and the partition  [ 0 , p ] ∪ ( p , 1 ] [0,p]\cup(p,1] . Coordinates outside
 H H  are neither tested nor conditioned. To prove the remaining
claim, ( 7.68 ) makes the bulk sites independent with
 q q -density  q q  and the boundary sites independent with conditional
 q q -density  r = ( q − p ) / ( 1 − p ) r=(q-p)/(1-p) . Fix  j j . In an auxiliary ordinary
 p p -configuration on  T j ∖ F T_{j}\setminus F , with  F F  declared open,
choose a shortest open path from the set  C C  to the set  D D  in
 G ⁡ [ T j ∪ F ] G[T_{j}\cup F] . Choose its endpoints to be its first visit to  C C 
and last visit to  D D ; by shortestness, all its internal sites lie
outside  F F . Only the first internal site can be adjacent to  C C , and
only the last can be adjacent to  D D : any other such adjacency would
give a strictly shorter path in the same induced graph. Thus the path
uses at most two sites of  ∂ V H F \partial_{V}^{H}F . If  r < p r<p , independently
thin the open boundary sites with retention  r / p r/p , while coupling every
other trial site monotonically from density  p p  to density  q q ; the
chosen witness survives with probability at least  ( r / p ) 2 (r/p)^{2} . If
 r ≥ p r\geq p , couple every trial coordinate monotonically. This proves
( 7.69 ). Each resulting event is measurable with respect to the
coordinates in  T j ∖ F T_{j}\setminus F , since the sites of  F F  are fixed
open. The assumed disjointness of these coordinate sets therefore proves
conditional independence, even though the deterministic set  F F  is
shared by all trial domains. 
 
 
 No extension to arbitrary seed-generated cluster unions is asserted.
Indeed, conditioning unused seeds to be closed may impose additional
one-coordinate restrictions away from the cluster boundary. In the
peeling application below, Lemma  7.4 
instead proves the stronger fact that unused seed sites and extinct
exploration data lie outside every later trial-coordinate set. The
residual product law is then stated in ( 7.104 ), and the
shortest-path comparison is repeated directly in
 G ⁡ [ Q u ∪ F i ] G[Q_{u}\cup F_{i}] .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma 7.2   (Adjacent-center annular comparison) . 
 
 Let  x , y x,y  be adjacent vertices, and let  a , r , R , b a,r,R,b  be nonnegative
integers such that 
 

 
 
 a + 1 ≤ r < R < b − 1 . a+1\leq r<R<b-1. 
 
 (7.70) 
 
 If a path  P P , oriented from its first specified endpoint to its
second, starts on  S a ​ ( y ) S_{a}(y)  and ends on  S b ​ ( y ) S_{b}(y) , then  P P  contains
a subpath in  B R ​ ( x ) B_{R}(x)  joining  B r ​ ( x ) B_{r}(x)  to  S R ​ ( x ) S_{R}(x) . In particular,
if  P P  is open, the cluster of the configuration induced on
 B R ​ ( x ) B_{R}(x)  that contains this subpath is an  [ r , R ] [r,R] -crossing cluster
centered at  x x . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Write  z z  and  w w  for the specified initial and terminal vertices of
 P P . The triangle inequality gives 
 

 
 
 d ⁡ ( x , z ) ≤ a + 1 ≤ r , d ⁡ ( x , w ) ≥ b − 1 > R . d(x,z)\leq a+1\leq r,\qquad d(x,w)\geq b-1>R. 
 
 
 Graph distance from  x x  changes by at most one across an edge. Hence
the portion of  P P  from  z z  through its first subsequent visit to
 S R ​ ( x ) S_{R}(x)  is contained in  B R ​ ( x ) B_{R}(x)  and joins  B r ​ ( x ) B_{r}(x)  to
 S R ​ ( x ) S_{R}(x) , as required.
∎ 
 
 
 
 The low-growth step below uses only
Lemma  7.0  and Auxiliary
Lemma  7.B  for geometry,
Proposition  6.2  for site snowballing, the site
Hamming lemma ( 7.65 ), the fixed-cluster law
( 7.68 )–( 7.69 ), and the peeling law in
Lemma  7.4 . All
five inputs have their complete hypotheses stated here. The remaining
operations are site FKG, monotonicity, union bounds, and deterministic
annular chaining. Writing 
 

 
 
 δ K ​ ( b , n ) = ( K ​ log ⁡ log ​ n min ⁡ { log ⁡ n , log ⁡ Gr G ⁡ ( b ) } ) 1 / 4 , \delta_{K}(b,n)=\left(\frac{K\log\log n}{\min\{\log n,\log\operatorname{Gr}_{G}(b)\}}\right)^{1/4}, 
 
 
 this is the sprinkling amount used when
Proposition  7.3  is invoked in
Proposition  7.1 . 
 
 
 Proposition 7.3   (Parameterized site low-growth step) . 
 
 For every integer  d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 ,  D ≥ 1 D\geq 1 , and  b 0 ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) b_{0}\in(0,1) , there

 then either  p 2 ≥ p c s ​ ( G ) p_{2}\geq p_{c}^{\rm s}(G) , or 
 

 
 
 κ p 2 s ​ ( e ( log ⁡ n ) c out ​ λ , n ) ≥ e − 3 ​ T . \kappa_{p_{2}}^{\rm s}\bigl(e^{(\log n)^{c_{\rm out}\lambda}},n\bigr)\geq e^{-3T}. 
 
 (7.72) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof of Proposition  7.3 . 
 
 
 Work throughout with i.i.d. uniform labels and
 ω q = { z : U z ≤ q } \omega_{q}=\{z:U_{z}\leq q\} . Thus all parameter values below live on
one monotone probability space. Let  s 1 , s 2 , s 3 , h 0 s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},h_{0}  be the constants

 The later induction uses the special case
 T = log ⁡ log ⁡ n T=\sqrt{\log\log n} , but its proof is uniform in the endpoint lower
bound. Indeed, throughout the argument that lower bound occurs only as

 for every fixed  c ∗ > 0 c_{*}>0 , provided also that  T ≥ T min ​ ( c ∗ ) T\geq T_{\min}(c_{*}) .
These four inequalities are exactly the numerical comparisons needed
in those occurrences. Thus the proof below establishes the displayed

 q 2 = Spr ⁡ ( p 1 ; δ K ​ ( b , n ) ) q_{2}=\operatorname{Spr}(p_{1};\delta_{K}(b,n)) . If

therefore assume  p 2 = q 2 < p c s p_{2}=q_{2}<p_{c}^{\rm s} . The whole sprinkling interval
is then subcritical, so the uniqueness hypothesis of Proposition 6.2

 Use in both snowballing applications the intensity 
 

 
 
 h = max { Gr G ( b ) − 1 , n − 1 / 15 } . h=\max\{\operatorname{Gr}_{G}(b)^{-1},n^{-1/15}\}. 
 
 
 It is at least  | B b | − 1 |B_{b}|^{-1} . Since  δ ≤ 1 \delta\leq 1  and

 Thus  h ≤ h 0 h\leq h_{0}  for large  n n , and the fourth-power chain-length

is checked after its proof.

 Lemma 7.4   (Site peeling filtration) . 
 
 Fix a possible history
 ℋ i = ( ℱ 0 , … , ℱ i ) \mathcal{H}_{i}=(\mathscr{F}_{0},\ldots,\mathscr{F}_{i}) , and put
 F i = ⋃ ℱ i F_{i}=\bigcup\mathscr{F}_{i} . Let  Q 1 , … , Q s Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{s}  be pairwise
disjoint subsets of 
 

 
 
 B r i + 1 + m ( log m ) − λ / c 1 + ( m / 2 ) ( log m ) − 4 λ / c 1 . B_{r_{i+1}+m(\log m)^{-\lambda/c_{1}}+(m/2)(\log m)^{-4\lambda/c_{1}}}. 
 
 
 If  λ / c 1 > D + 2 \lambda/c_{1}>D+2  and  n n  is sufficiently large, then,
conditionally on  ℋ i \mathcal{H}_{i} , the coordinates in
 ( ⋃ j Q j ) ∖ F i (\bigcup_{j}Q_{j})\setminus F_{i}  are mutually independent and have
laws 
 

 
 
 z ∈ ∂ V D m F i U z  is uniform on  ( q i , 1 ] , d ⁡ ( z , F i ) ≥ 2 U z ​  is uniform on  ​ [ 0 , 1 ] . \begin{array}[]{c|c}z\in\partial_{V}^{D_{m}}F_{i}&U_{z}\text{ is uniform on }(q_{i},1],\\
d(z,F_{i})\geq 2&U_{z}\text{ is uniform on }[0,1].\end{array} 
 
 (7.99) 
 
 Here  ∂ V D m \partial_{V}^{D_{m}}  is the external vertex boundary relative
to  D m D_{m} . The sites of  F i F_{i}  may have additional interval
restrictions recording when they entered the exploration, but they
are deterministically  q i q_{i} -open and are not random inputs to a
trial. 
 
 
 
 Proof.  Explore a site cluster by revealing each reached open site and
each tested site in its external vertex boundary. For  i = 0 i=0 , specifying
 𝒞 0 \mathscr{C}_{0}  reveals  F 0 F_{0} ,  ∂ V D m F 0 \partial_{V}^{D_{m}}F_{0} , and the closed sites
of  S r 0 ∖ F 0 S_{r_{0}}\setminus F_{0} , and nothing else. The last set is outside all
the  Q j Q_{j} ’s by the displayed radial bound below. 
 
 
 Proceed inductively. A member of  ℱ j \mathscr{F}_{j}  that is not used as a
seed at stage  j + 1 j+1  does not meet  S r j + 1 S_{r_{j+1}} . Since it is connected
and met the preceding outer sphere, it cannot enter  B r j + 1 − 1 B_{r_{j+1}-1} ,
and its revealed boundary cannot enter  B r j + 1 − 2 B_{r_{j+1}-2} . Thus all
coordinates belonging only to an extinct part of the history lie outside
the later trial region. Every member that is used as a seed is contained
in a member of  ℱ i \mathscr{F}_{i} . A site revealed earlier as closed and
adjacent to such a surviving cluster is either subsequently opened and
belongs to  F i F_{i} , or is still closed and belongs to
 ∂ V D m F i \partial_{V}^{D_{m}}F_{i} . Therefore, on sites in the trial region outside
 F i F_{i} , the entire history imposes exactly the two coordinatewise
conditions in ( 7.99 ). To state the factorization explicitly, let  W i W_{i} 
be the ball displayed in the lemma. There are intervals  I z ⊆ [ 0 , q i ] I_{z}\subseteq[0,q_{i}] , one for each  z ∈ F i ∩ W i z\in F_{i}\cap W_{i} , and an event  R i R_{i} 
measurable with respect to coordinates outside  W i W_{i} , such that 
 

 
 
 { ℋ i = ( ℱ 0 , … , ℱ i ) } = R i ∩ ⋂ z ∈ F i ∩ W i { U z ∈ I z } ∩ ⋂ z ∈ ∂ V D m F i ∩ W i { U z > q i } . \{\mathcal{H}_{i}=(\mathscr{F}_{0},\ldots,\mathscr{F}_{i})\}=R_{i}\cap\!\bigcap_{z\in F_{i}\cap W_{i}}\!\{U_{z}\in I_{z}\}\cap\!\bigcap_{z\in\partial_{V}^{D_{m}}F_{i}\cap W_{i}}\!\{U_{z}>q_{i}\}. 
 
 (7.100) 
 
 Here is the induction proving that no further condition is hidden in
( 7.100 ); set  F − 1 = ∅ F_{-1}=\varnothing . At stage  j j , once the seed set and all earlier explored sets
are fixed, the atom that the newly explored cluster is  K K  is exactly 
 

 
 
 ⋂ z ∈ K ∖ F j − 1 { U z ≤ q j } ∩ ⋂ z ∈ ∂ V D m K ∖ F j − 1 { U z > q j } . \bigcap_{z\in K\setminus F_{j-1}}\{U_{z}\leq q_{j}\}\cap\bigcap_{z\in\partial_{V}^{D_{m}}K\setminus F_{j-1}}\{U_{z}>q_{j}\}. 
 
 (7.101) 
 
 The exploration order adds no condition: the next site is selected from
the already determined frontier before its label is read. Intersecting
( 7.101 ) over  j ≤ i j\leq i  gives one interval condition for each tested
coordinate because  q 0 < ⋯ < q i q_{0}<\cdots<q_{i} . A coordinate can change from a
boundary site to an open-cluster site only once. The radial induction
above places every tested coordinate in  W i W_{i}  either in  F i F_{i}  or in
 ∂ V D m F i \partial_{V}^{D_{m}}F_{i} ; all tests belonging only to an extinct cluster
or an earlier seed sphere are outside  W i W_{i}  and are absorbed into
 R i R_{i} . This proves ( 7.100 ) by induction on  j j .
Indeed, the upper endpoint of  I z I_{z}  is  q a q_{a} , where  a a  is the
first stage at which  z z  belongs to an explored cluster. Its lower
endpoint is  q b q_{b}  if  b < a b<a  is the last earlier stage at which  z z 
was exposed as a boundary site, and is zero if there was no such stage.
The preceding induction proves ( 7.100 ): all other earlier
conditions belong to extinct clusters or seed spheres and hence to
 R i R_{i} . Once the vertex sets are fixed, their connectedness and their
partition into clusters are deterministic. Since ( 7.100 ) is a product of
one-coordinate interval events, conditioning preserves independence
between all coordinates in  W i ∖ F i W_{i}\setminus F_{i} ; this is precisely the
partition-cell conclusion of Lemma  2.1 .
Thus the terminal history, even when generated adaptively, imposes no
additional condition on a trial coordinate, proving the claim.
 □ \square 
 
 
 It remains to verify the radial bound used in the lemma. Increase the
auxiliary tube parameter  λ \lambda , if necessary, so that
 λ / c 1 > D + 2 \lambda/c_{1}>D+2 ; this only increases the constant in the geometric
input. We have 
 

 
 
 r i − r i + 1 = m 40 ​ ( log ⁡ n ) − D , r_{i}-r_{i+1}=\frac{m}{40}(\log n)^{-D}, 
 
 
 whereas a trial center lies in
 B r i + 1 + m ( log m ) − λ / c 1 B_{r_{i+1}+m(\log m)^{-\lambda/c_{1}}}  and its trial radius is
 m 2 ( log m ) − 4 λ / c 1 \frac{m}{2}(\log m)^{-4\lambda/c_{1}} . The prescribed choice of
 λ \lambda , followed by increasing  K 1 ​ ( d , D , λ ) K_{1}(d,D,\lambda)  in ( 7.95 ),
gives, for all sufficiently large  n n , 
 

 
 
 m ( log m ) − λ / c 1 + m 2 ( log m ) − 4 λ / c 1 + 2 t + 4 < r i − r i + 1 . m(\log m)^{-\lambda/c_{1}}+\frac{m}{2}(\log m)^{-4\lambda/c_{1}}+2t+4<r_{i}-r_{i+1}. 
 
 (7.102) 
 
 All earlier seed spheres lie still farther out. Applying
Lemma  7.4  with
 ℱ = ℱ i \mathscr{F}=\mathscr{F}_{i} , under the monotone coupling and conditional
on the full history, will therefore expose no extinct-cluster coordinate
inside a trial tube. 
 
 
 We also spell out where the trial centers come from. Fix a value
 ℱ i \mathscr{F}_{i}  compatible with  Ω \Omega , an eligible
 C ∈ ℱ i C\in\mathscr{F}_{i}  other than a deterministically chosen cluster
minimizing  d ⁡ ( o , C ) d(o,C) , where eligible means
 d ⁡ ( o , C ) ≤ r i + 1 d(o,C)\leq r_{i+1} , and
put 
 

 
 
 r ∗ = ⌊ m ( log m ) − 2 λ / c 1 ⌋ , a ∗ = ⌊ r ∗ 3 ⌋ . r_{*}=\left\lfloor m(\log m)^{-2\lambda/c_{1}}\right\rfloor,\qquad a_{*}=\left\lfloor\frac{r_{*}}{3}\right\rfloor. 
 
 
 The power gaps defining  𝒮 ⁡ ( n ) \mathscr{S}(n)  imply, uniformly for the
selected  m m  and all sufficiently large  n n , 
 

 
 
 m 1 ≤ a ∗ ≤ r ∗ ≤ m 2 , a ∗ ≥ 1 4 m ( log m ) − 2 λ / c 1 . m_{1}\leq a_{*}\leq r_{*}\leq m_{2},\qquad a_{*}\geq\tfrac{1}{4}m(\log m)^{-2\lambda/c_{1}}. 
 
 (7.103) 
 
 Thus both applications of Lemma  7.0  below are at
integer scales belonging to its guaranteed interval. If
 d ⁡ ( o , C ) ≤ r ∗ d(o,C)\leq r_{*} , connectivity, the fact that every member descends
from a cluster meeting  S r 0 S_{r_{0}} , and the choice of the distinguished

scale  r ∗ r_{*} . 
 
 
 If  d ⁡ ( o , C ) > r ∗ d(o,C)>r_{*} , let  v ∈ C v\in C  minimize  d ⁡ ( o , v ) d(o,v) . A path in  C C

 Orient every resulting connecting path from its crossing in  C C  toward

Otherwise take the first path vertex outside the

 ℱ i \mathscr{F}_{i} . In the exit case the center is therefore within
 2 ​ t + 4 2t+4  of  C C  and within  2 ​ t + 3 2t+3  of another old cluster. These two
case-by-case bounds are exactly the two ball-intersection assertions below.

 The only boundary-count comparison used in absorbing the constants above is
 | ∂ V A | ≤ | ∂ E A | ≤ d ​ | ∂ V A | |\partial_{V}A|\leq|\partial_{E}A|\leq d|\partial_{V}A| . We emphasize the
correct finite-energy bookkeeping for path surgery. A path of  r r  edges
contains at most  r + 1 r+1  sites. If the percolation parameter ranges in a
fixed compact interval  I = [ p − , p + ] ⊂ ( 0 , 1 ) I=[p_{-},p_{+}]\subset(0,1) , forcing those sites
open has cost at most 
 

 
 
 p − − ( r + 1 ) ≤ exp ⁡ { C I ​ ( r + 1 ) } ; p_{-}^{-(r+1)}\leq\exp\{C_{I}(r+1)\}; 
 
 
 equivalently, an injective opening surgery that records the modified subset
has a Radon–Nikodym and fibre loss of the form  exp ⁡ { O I ​ ( r + 1 ) } \exp\{O_{I}(r+1)\} , not a
constant uniform in  r r . No such additional surgery is used in the
derivation of ( 7.110 )–( 7.112 ): that derivation uses FKG,
the displayed pivotal estimates, and deterministic local chaining. In the
places where path-opening surgery is used, its length-dependent loss is
retained explicitly, as in ( 6.12 ) and
Lemma  4.1 .  □ \square 
 
 
 For later use in the Full-Space implication, we record separately the
numerical check for Proposition 6.2. Let  s 1 , s 3 s_{1},s_{3}  be its constants with
 D = 1 D=1 , put 
 

 
 
 c 0 = ( 8 ⋅ 3 20 ) − 1 , h j = exp ⁡ [ − c 0 ​ ( log ⁡ n j ) 20 ] , Δ j = T j − 1 . c_{0}=(8\cdot 3^{20})^{-1},\qquad h_{j}=\exp[-c_{0}(\log n_{j})^{20}],\qquad\Delta_{j}=T_{j}^{-1}. 
 
 
 If  r ∈ [ n j 1 / 3 , n j ] r\in[n_{j}^{1/3},n_{j}]  satisfies
 log ⁡ Gr ⁡ ( r ) > ( log ⁡ r ) 20 \log\operatorname{Gr}(r)>(\log r)^{20}  and
 ρ = ⌊ r / 3 ⌋ \rho=\lfloor r/3\rfloor , submultiplicativity gives
 Gr ⁡ ( ρ ) ≥ h j − 1 \operatorname{Gr}(\rho)\geq h_{j}^{-1}  for large  n j n_{j} . Moreover,
since  n j + 1 = exp ⁡ [ ( log ⁡ n j ) 9 ] n_{j+1}=\exp[(\log n_{j})^{9}] , 
 

 
 
 h j s 1 ​ Δ j 4 ≤ s 3 2 ​ n j + 1 + 1 , 4 ​ h j s 1 ​ Δ j 4 ≤ e − T j , h j ≤ h 0 . h_{j}^{s_{1}\Delta_{j}^{4}}\leq\frac{s_{3}}{2n_{j+1}+1},\qquad 4h_{j}^{s_{1}\Delta_{j}^{4}}\leq e^{-T_{j}},\qquad h_{j}\leq h_{0}. 
 
 (7.113) 
 
 Indeed, the logarithms on the left are of order
 − ( log n j ) 20 / ( log log n j ) 2 -(\log n_{j})^{20}/(\log\log n_{j})^{2} . The logarithms on the right have
orders  − ( log ⁡ n j ) 9 -(\log n_{j})^{9}  and  − log ⁡ log ⁡ n j -\sqrt{\log\log n_{j}} , respectively. 
 
 
 We record the assembly into the induction rather than leave the three
implications implicit. Given  n 0 , p 0 , δ 0 n_{0},p_{0},\delta_{0} , first define 
 

 
 
 n − 1 := ( log ⁡ n 0 ) 1 / 2 . n_{-1}:=(\log n_{0})^{1/2}. 
 
 
 For each integer  i ≥ 1 i\geq 1 , recursively define 
 

 
 
 n i := e ( log ⁡ n i − 1 ) 9 , δ i := ( log log n i ) − 1 / 2 . n_{i}:=e^{(\log n_{i-1})^{9}},\qquad\delta_{i}:=(\log\log n_{i})^{-1/2}. 
 
 
 Finally set  p 1 := Spr ⁡ ( p 0 ; δ 0 ) p_{1}:=\operatorname{Spr}(p_{0};\delta_{0}) , and, for each
integer  i ≥ 1 i\geq 1 , recursively set
 p i + 1 := Spr ⁡ ( p i ; δ i ) p_{i+1}:=\operatorname{Spr}(p_{i};\delta_{i}) . Define 
 
 
 
 
 T i \displaystyle T_{i} 
 = log ⁡ log ⁡ n i ( i ≥ − 1 ) , \displaystyle=\sqrt{\log\log n_{i}}\quad(i\geq-1), 
 
 (7.114) 
 
 
 𝖥 0 \displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{0} 
 = { ℙ p 0 ​ ( u ↔ v ) ≥ e − T − 1 ​  for all  ​ u , v ∈ B n 0 } , \displaystyle=\left\{\mathbb{P}_{p_{0}}(u\leftrightarrow v)\geq e^{-T_{-1}}\text{ for all }u,v\in B_{n_{0}}\right\}, 
 
 
 
 𝖥 i \displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{i} 
 = { ℙ p i ( u ↔ v ) ≥ e − T i  for all  u , v ∈ B n i } ( i ≥ 1 ) , \displaystyle=\left\{\mathbb{P}_{p_{i}}(u\leftrightarrow v)\geq e^{-T_{i}}\text{ for all }u,v\in B_{n_{i}}\right\}\quad(i\geq 1), 
 
 
 
 𝖢 i \displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{i} 
 = { κ p i s ( e ( log ⁡ m ) 10 , m ) ≥ e − T i  for every  m ∈ ℒ ( G , 20 ) ∩ [ n i − 2 , n i − 1 ] } ( i ≥ 1 ) . \displaystyle=\left\{\kappa_{p_{i}}^{\rm s}(e^{(\log m)^{10}},m)\geq e^{-T_{i}}\text{ for every }m\in\mathscr{L}(G,20)\cap[n_{i-2},n_{i-1}]\right\}\quad(i\geq 1). 
 
 
 Notice the exact identities 
 

 
 
 T i + 1 = 3 ​ T i ( i ≥ 0 ) , log ⁡ log ⁡ m ∈ [ T i 2 / 9 , T i 2 ] ( m ∈ [ n i − 1 , n i ] , i ≥ 1 ) . T_{i+1}=3T_{i}\quad(i\geq 0),\qquad\log\log m\in[T_{i}^{2}/9,T_{i}^{2}]\quad(m\in[n_{i-1},n_{i}],\ i\geq 1). 
 
 (7.115) 
 
 Proposition  7.3  gives the two Corridor
implications, and Proposition  6.2  gives the
Full-Space implication. The numerical checks
( 7.113 )–( 7.74 ) yield constants  K ⁡ ( d , a , b ) , N ⁡ ( d , a , b ) K(d,a,b),N(d,a,b)  such that 
 
 
 
 
 𝖥 0 \displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{0} 
 ⟹ [ 𝖢 1 or p 1 ≥ p c s ] , \displaystyle\Longrightarrow[\mathsf{C}_{1}\ \text{or}\ p_{1}\geq p_{c}^{\rm s}], 
 
 (7.116) 
 
 
 𝖥 i ∩ ⋂ k = 1 i 𝖢 k \displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{i}\cap\bigcap_{k=1}^{i}\mathsf{C}_{k} 
 ⟹ [ 𝖢 i + 1 or p i + 1 ≥ p c s ] \displaystyle\Longrightarrow[\mathsf{C}_{i+1}\ \text{or}\ p_{i+1}\geq p_{c}^{\rm s}] 
 
 ( i ≥ 1 ) , \displaystyle(i\geq 1), 
 
 
 
 𝖥 j ∩ 𝖢 j + 1 \displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{j}\cap\mathsf{C}_{j+1} 
 ⟹ [ 𝖥 j + 1 or p j + 1 ≥ p c s ] \displaystyle\Longrightarrow[\mathsf{F}_{j+1}\ \text{or}\ p_{j+1}\geq p_{c}^{\rm s}] 
 
 ( j ≥ 0 ) . \displaystyle(j\geq 0). 
 
 
 
 
 We give the details for the two possible scale regimes. 
 
 
 The base Corridor implication.  Fix
 m ∈ ℒ ⁡ ( G , 20 ) ∩ [ n − 1 , n 0 ] m\in\mathscr{L}(G,20)\cap[n_{-1},n_{0}] , and take
 b = b s ​ ( m , p 0 ) b=b_{\rm s}(m,p_{0}) . Finiteness of the burn-in gives  b ≥ 1 b\geq 1 , its

 By transitivity,  𝖥 0 \mathsf{F}_{0}  gives

 Apply Proposition  7.3  with  n = m n=m  and
 T = log ⁡ log ⁡ m T=\sqrt{\log\log m} . Its corridor alternative is at least

 T T  in Proposition  7.3  is satisfied
throughout the base interval.
